Expert trajectories offer a remedy, but they are incompatible with the current policy and often lie in the student's low-probability regions, so the surviving updates demand large parameter shifts and decay into surface-level imitation rather than transferable reasoning. We argue that a useful learning signal on hard problems must be both verifiably correct and sufficiently compatible with the current policy. We propose Policy-Compatible Reinforcement Learning (PICO), which performs distribution-constrained joint decoding: expert data guides decoding at the token level while preserving the student's relative token probabilities, and a dynamic top-k mechanism confines the sampling distribution under a finite divergence budget, yielding trajectories that are verified and policy-compatible by construction. These trajectories are optimized jointly with on-policy rollouts under a mixed-behavior objective that mitigates their distribution mismatch via importance sampling. Experiments across reasoning diverse datasets and agentic benchmarks demonstrate that PICO consistently outperforms standard RLVR and prior off-policy baselines.
  </details>

- **[SetGRPO: Calibrating Grader-Induced Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36807)**  `arXiv:2610.36807`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiale Huang, Zixu Li, Zhiwei Chen, Zhiheng Fu, Jinhe Bi, Yupeng Hu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Rubric-based reinforcement learning uses fine-grained grader feedback to train large language models on open-ended tasks, but grader randomness can alter GRPO updates in ways that score discrepancies alone cannot characterize. Because GRPO jointly normalizes rewards within each response group and combines the resulting training coefficients with response-level gradients, a scoring discrepancy can shift the coefficients of the entire group and induce policy-dependent update changes. Selective execution further affects the direction-reversal risk of retained updates. To address this, we propose Set-Valued Group Relative Policy Optimization (SetGRPO). It comprises three modules: (a) Joint Reward Set Calibration constructs a joint uncertainty set for the training coefficients; (b) Geometry-Guided Safe Recovery maps candidate coefficients into the current policy's local update geometry and determines the largest safe recovery ratio for each response group; and (c) Selective Risk-Gated Optimization selects a global threshold through independent risk calibration to control the conditional direction-reversal risk of accepted updates while preserving the original GRPO objective. Under exchangeability, SetGRPO provides finite-sample marginal coverage for the joint coefficient set and simultaneous risk control across candidate thresholds. On ten open-ended benchmarks spanning three domains, SetGRPO achieves the highest macro average among all compared training methods on both Qwen3.5 and Llama3.2 model families.
  </details>

- **[Robust LLM Data Selection via Content-Invariant Gradient Attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36755)**  `arXiv:2610.36755`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Jia Gu, Liang Pang, Huawei Shen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Gradient-based data attribution has become an important tool for high-quality data selection in the instruction fine-tuning of large language models (LLMs), as it estimates the contribution of training examples through their influence on model optimization. However, we find that existing global gradient attribution methods suffer from surface-form bias: minor changes to semantically irrelevant details, such as wording or formatting, can produce highly inconsistent attribution scores even when the underlying content remains unchanged. To address this issue, we propose Content-Invariant Gradient Attribution (CIGA), a robust framework for instruction data selection. CIGA first identifies layers whose gradients are both influential for attribution and stable under semantics-preserving surface-form perturbations, thereby reducing formatting noise in gradient features. It then dynamically fuses these attribution signals with semantic representations of the input, combining optimization-level influence with content-level information. Extensive experiments across multiple benchmarks show that CIGA produces more stable rankings under surface-form perturbations while preserving downstream capabilities and improving average performance. CIGA also reduces attribution cost, offering a robust and efficient approach to data selection for LLM instruction tuning.
  </details>

- **[Less is more: learning what to profile for GPU kernel optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36576)**  `arXiv:2610.36576`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yihan Wang, Peiyu Liu, Runyu Chen, Wei Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models have recently shown strong potential for automatically optimizing GPU kernels, yet their effectiveness depends critically on the hardware information available to the kernel optimization model. Existing systems typically rely on predefined profiling strategies, leaving what to profile largely outside the optimization process. We show that effective profiling is not simply about collecting more information: increasing the number of profiling metrics does not monotonically improve kernel optimization, while the most useful profiling information varies across kernels. Our theoretical analysis further shows that, under such heterogeneous profiling utility, kernel-conditioned selection can outperform any single fixed profiling configuration. Motivated by this observation, we propose AutoProfiler, a feedback-driven framework for learning a deterministic profiling tree from optimization feedback. The tree is iteratively refined through REWRITE and SPLIT operations, with an LLM generating candidate modifications and empirical evaluation determining their acceptance. Once constructed, the tree quickly gathers the required profiling information for any kernel, allowing the LLM rewriter to be invoked only once. Experiments on KernelBench show that our approach improves kernel optimization over fixed profiling configurations under constrained profiling budgets. These results suggest that hardware profiling should evolve from static metric collection toward profiling reasoning, making profiling itself a new dimension for improving kernel optimization. The codes are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Proposing Better Rollouts for On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36477)**  `arXiv:2610.36477`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Sirui Chen, Yunzhe Qi, Haobo Xu, Jiaru Zou, Mengting Ai, Zihao Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation (OPD) improves language-model post-training by letting the student learn from its own sampled trajectories under teacher supervision, aligning the training process with inference-time behavior. However, the rollout policy in OPD is usually treated as fixed: each round samples directly from the current student. We show that this choice can create a compounding teacher--student mismatch, because it requires the teacher to provide effective supervision on whichever trajectories the student samples, including ones less reachable by the teacher itself. On such trajectories, teacher supervision can become noisy or even misleading, leading to undesirable student patterns such as repetition. Moreover, since the updated student in turn defines the rollout policy of subsequent rounds, these patterns persist and potentially amplify over training. This failure mode suggests that improving OPD requires treating the rollout policy itself as an optimization target, rather than only modifying the distillation objective. We propose Mixture Proposal On-Policy Distillation (MPOPD), which derives a student-anchored, teacher-guided rollout distribution from a local trust-region objective based on relative teacher support. The resulting target is a geometric mixture of the student and teacher policies, and we introduce a proposal policy that approximates this mixture, enabling rollout generation in a single forward pass. Experiments on 16 math, knowledge, and medical reasoning benchmarks show consistent gains over OPD and recent variants. On math reasoning, our method improves average accuracy by 6.91 over OPD on Qwen3-4B-Base while reducing average generation length by 41%, suggesting that rollout policy design provides an effective way to improve OPD.
  </details>

- **[Reliability-Aware Short-Term Glycemic Risk Prediction Using an Anchor-Based Comparative Framework of Sequence, Multimodal, and State-Updating Digital-Twin Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36363)**  `arXiv:2610.36363`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Pir Bakhsh Khokhar, Carmine Gravino, Fabio Palomba_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Anticipating short-term hypoglycemic and hyperglycemic risk is essential for proactive diabetes management. However, existing machine learning approaches often use inconsistent evaluation protocols and emphasize predictive performance over reliability. This study introduces an anchor-based benchmarking framework integrating longitudinal glucose sequences and multimodal patient information. Using the T1DiabetesGranada dataset, 5,503,441 standardized prediction anchors were constructed from 736 individuals with type 1 diabetes to evaluate hypoglycemia and hyperglycemia prediction at 30-, 60-, and 120-minute horizons. Three neural architectures, a Hybrid LSTM-Transformer, a Multimodal Digital Twin, and a State-Updating Digital Twin with gap-aware longitudinal state progression, were compared under identical patient-level validation protocols. Beyond predictive performance, the evaluation examined calibration, classification thresholds, explanation reliability, cross-model disagreement, and robustness to controlled perturbations. No statistically significant predictive differences were observed after Holm correction, although the models exhibited complementary reliability characteristics. The Multimodal Digital Twin achieved the lowest mean Brier score, while the State-Updating Digital Twin demonstrated the lowest mean expected calibration error (ECE-10). The Hybrid LSTM-Transformer showed strong explanation faithfulness and stability under its temporal attribution protocol. These findings highlight the importance of evaluating digital-twin models beyond predictive accuracy and emphasize the need for reliability assessment and external validation before clinical deployment.
  </details>

- **[Freeze, Don't Clone: Fine-Tuning with Expressive Policies Without Retaining Offline Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36247)**  `arXiv:2610.36247`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Sami Nur Islam, Johan Obando-Ceron, Doina Precup, Aaron Courville, Pablo Samuel Castro_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Offline-to-online reinforcement learning uses offline pretraining to accelerate subsequent online learning. While many methods assume the offline dataset remains available throughout fine-tuning, there are many settings where this is not the case. Prior work attributes failures in this setting primarily to critic recalibration under distribution shift. We identify a complementary actor-side failure: once the offline data disappears, continued behavioral cloning (BC) no longer preserves the offline behavior distribution and instead refits the behavioral model to the agent's limited, and nonstationary, online experience. We demonstrate that stopping online behavioral fitting while preserving the pretrained expressive prior substantially improves no-retention fine-tuning across implicit flow Q-learning (IFQL), flow Q-learning (FQL), and action-chunked RL (QC) on seven OGBench domains. We trace this failure to a behavioral feedback loop: continuing online behavioral cloning rapidly erases the pretrained prior, inducing representational collapse and action diversity loss that starves the critic of useful exploration. Freezing the prior breaks this cycle, preserving offline coverage while allowing value learning to drive continuous improvement.
  </details>

- **[Concorde: Geometry-Aware Link Prediction via Decoupled Energy Minimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36204)**  `arXiv:2610.36204`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Ege Demirci, Ambuj Singh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Link prediction remains difficult in sparse graphs, where diffusion heuristics lack connectivity and geometric methods suffer spectral instability when coupling learned representations with diffusion operators. We introduce Concorde, an energy-based framework that resolves this by decomposing link likelihood into three structurally decoupled branches: (i) a parameter-free spectral anchor that establishes permutation-invariant topological stability; (ii) a mixed-curvature residual on a learnable $\mathbb{H}^{d_h}_{c_h} \times \mathbb{S}^{d_s}_{c_s}$ product manifold, regularized by a feature-aware Ollivier-Ricci curvature proxy; and (iii) a continuous vector field on the tangent bundle of hyperbolic space for non-local structural alignment across topological gaps. The branches are fused via learned gating and trained with an N-pair ranking loss aligned with retrieval-style evaluation. Across five benchmarks spanning sparse, dense, featureless, and attributed regimes, Concorde establishes state-of-the-art early retrieval precision, nearly doubling P@10% over leading baselines in sparse settings.
  </details>

- **[DuplexTune: Coupled-State Replay for Memory-Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36009)**  `arXiv:2610.36009`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Paul Raj, Simon Caldwell, Daniel Whitmore, Mutlu Mete_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sparse expert execution does not remove the stored states needed to adapt a mixture-of-experts language model. We present DuplexTune, a framework for replaying coupled hidden states through pretrained attention and expert modules. Trainable width bridges connect two residual streams to the backbone. Bridge warm-up precedes backbone adaptation, with router weights kept frozen. We analyze the implicit state-recovery equation and identify the convergence and replay conditions needed for reconstruction. On Qwen1.5-MoE-A2.7B, the single-H800 evaluation reports 38.8 GB peak memory, versus 64.8 GB for supervised fine-tuning with checkpointing, and throughput of 25.1 versus 19.9 samples/s. DuplexTune achieves 67.2% MMLU, 75.6% GSM8K, and 7.69 MT-Bench, with a weaker multilingual score than the checkpointed baseline. The results characterize a configuration-dependent tradeoff among state storage, throughput, and adaptation quality.
  </details>

- **[Multiple Leak Localization in Water Distribution Networks through Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35972)**  `arXiv:2610.35972`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Arvin Ajoodani, Sara Nazif, Pouria Ramazi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Leak localization in water distribution networks is challenging when multiple leaks occur simultaneously, particularly under sparse monitoring. Existing data-driven methods often assume a single or predefined number of leaks or require extensive enumeration of leak combinations. This study proposes a graph neural network (GNN) model that localizes a variable number of simultaneous leaks without specifying the leak count in advance or exhaustively generating every possible combination of leak locations for training. The model is evaluated on two benchmark networks of different sizes, the 31-node Hanoi network and the larger 92-node NET3 network, under variable demand, sparse pressure-sensor coverage, variable leak magnitudes, and varying numbers of simultaneous leaks. The GNN outperforms multilayer perceptron, support vector machine, K-nearest neighbors, and random forest classifiers by up to 12% and 20% in accuracy respectively in Hanoi and NET3 networks. Consistent performance across both networks supports the scalability of the proposed approach.
  </details>

- **[Curriculum-Driven Deep Reinforcement Learning for Autonomous Far-Range Rocket Landing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35954)**  `arXiv:2610.35954`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Atin Chowdhury, Piyush Joshi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous vertical landing of reusable launch vehicles is a critical yet formidable challenge in aerospace engineering, primarily due to highly nonlinear dynamics, stringent precision require- ments, and inherent environmental uncertainties. While reinforcement learning (RL) has emerged as a promising alternative to traditional model-based control, existing approaches frequently rely on flat training paradigms and highly simplified initial conditions, drastically limiting their real-world applicability. This paper proposes a comprehensive curriculum-driven deep reinforcement learning framework designed to master both far-range navigation and precision landing. By employing a three-stage curriculum augmented with a critic warm-up procedure and a rehearsal mechanism to prevent catastrophic forgetting, alongside a high-dimensional observation vector utilizing a full 3 X 3 rotation-matrix, the agent is trained to navigate from an initial horizontal distance of 1,000 to 1,500 units vastly farther than prior benchmarks before executing a soft touchdown. Crucially, we intro- duce a redesigned reward function that reduces unnecessary computational overhead and addresses limitations of earlier formulations, including poorly calibrated reward components and potentially conflicting optimization objectives. The redesigned function bounds the per-step reward magnitude by (O(1)), while its progress term accumulates the total distance closed over the trajectory. The available 100,000-episode results report a 99.98% success rate for the proposed policy and 68.31% for the baseline under the evaluation conditions. In the evaluation, the proposed policy retained 69.1% of its initial fuel capacity and achieved a mean final distance from the pad centre of 7.07 units, compared with 13.10 units for the baseline. Landing visualizations are available in Video 1 and Video 2.
  </details>

- **[SEEK: Skill Extraction with Evolving Knowledge against Frontier Science Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34365)**  `arXiv:2610.34365`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Cheng Gui, Xiang Zheng, Yutao Wu, Hanxun Huang, Yige Li, Kaidi Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Science agents increasingly package domain expertise as reusable skills that plan analyses, select tools, and execute workflows. This modularity creates a confidentiality risk: the interactions that deliver a skill's capability can also reveal the skill itself. We build an extensible benchmark of 40 protected skills across eight open- and closed-source science-agent platforms, comparing direct, fixed-strategy, and bandit-guided iterative probing under reference-based and reference-free evaluation. Leakage is pervasive: all eight platforms (100%) leak at least one protected skill, and four reach 100% attack success under a single direct request. To stress-test platforms that resist simple probing, we propose SEEK (Skill Extraction with Evolving Knowledge), a black-box, self-evolving red-teaming agent that consolidates response-derived evidence in persistent memory and evolves reusable attack skills, using only user-visible responses. On 12 skills from SpatialAgent and Biomni, SEEK achieves 100% attack success while reducing the mean first-success round by 31.8% and 38.2% relative to bandit-guided probing. Against a confidentiality policy that blocks all three benchmark protocols, SEEK still extracts one of six protected skills. These findings show that current science agents do not reliably protect their skills and that defenses must be evaluated against adaptive, multi-turn attackers.
  </details>

- **[The Shape of the DPO Hyperparameter Landscape: What to Measure, How It Moves with Scale, and When Not to Transfer It](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27895)**  `arXiv:2610.27895`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Hannan Mahadik, David Salinas, Jacek Golebiowski, Aaron Klein_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Direct preference optimisation (DPO) is widely used for language model post-training, but its performance depends strongly on the choice of its hyperparameters, such as the learning rate and the reference-policy regularisation parameter $\beta$. To better understand the DPO hyperparameter landscape, we conduct extensive experiments using a dense hyperparameter grid across different model scales from the same family. This allows us to analyse how the optimal DPO hyperparameters evolve with model size and the number of update steps, with intermediate checkpoints providing an inexpensive signal to accelerate the hyperparameter optimisation process. Furthermore, by evaluating our models on both static and dynamic benchmarks, we identify which benchmarks provide the strongest signal for hyperparameter selection. Finally, we investigate how different state-of-the-art hyperparameter optimisation strategies perform in this setting. Our experiments show that the optimal learning rate varies with model size and that hyperparameters are not transferable across training datasets. We also find that validation loss is a poor proxy for hyperparameter optimisation, as hyperparameters that minimize validation loss only weakly correlate with optimal hyperparameters for downstream benchmarks. Therefore, DPO hyperparameters need to be tuned specifically for the target model, dataset, and evaluation objective. We also show that short candidate training runs can substantially reduce the cost of this selection. We release all our training configurations and evaluation results to support reproducible analysis of selection quality and training cost.
  </details>

- **[First-Order LoRA Training with Phone NPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24816)**  `arXiv:2610.24816`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Xu Zheng, Wei Cheng, Zhuomin Chen, Dongkuan Xu, Mo Sha, Jingchao Ni, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-device fine-tuning lets a large language model (LLM) learn from its user's private data without uploading it. Low-rank adaptation (LoRA) reduces the trainable parameters, but each first-order training step still multiplies by the frozen base-model weights during the forward pass, activation recomputation, and input-gradient computation. A phone's neural processing unit (NPU) already holds these weights for inference; using it for training also requires computing input gradients, which its inference interface does not support. We present a system that runs all three frozen-weight products on the NPU while keeping the training graph, adapters, and optimizer on the CPU. Its input-gradient kernels reuse the resident weight tiles and the forward dequantizer, avoiding a transposed weight copy and ensuring forward and backward use the same effective weights. To preserve usable gradients, the system selects precision by gradient sensitivity and applies a per-row range guard for half-precision (FP16) outputs. Its runtime fuses the remaining CPU operators and overlaps NPU execution with adapter computation, while resumable execution and thermal pacing let training proceed as a background job. We evaluate it on two Qualcomm Snapdragon platforms using four LLMs from the Qwen and Llama families. Our workloads cover nightly personalization with labels supplied on the phone and the public LaMP-2 benchmark; comparisons include an optimized CPU trainer, a phone-GPU trainer, forward-only training, and GPU-trained references.
  </details>

- **[Memorilla: Latent Semantic Memory for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21375)**  `arXiv:2610.21375`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Adibvafa Fallahpour, Parsa Idehpour, Vignesh Kothapalli, Nikita Mounier, Shirley Wu, Shayan Pardis, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Memory enables large language models (LLMs) to use information beyond their parameters, such as past user conversations, long documents, or an agent's earlier actions. For a frozen LLM, access to this accumulated knowledge is limited to what a memory system places in its context window. However, gathering and combining relevant information needed for each query while keeping the model's context short remains challenging for existing systems. Here we introduce Memorilla, a memory system that gathers information from an entire document collection into a fixed number of query-dependent 'memory tokens' for a frozen LLM. Memorilla encodes documents (such as user chats, book chapters) once into reusable embeddings, then learns to retrieve and place query-specific information in the LLM's input embedding space. Using only 16 memory tokens, Memorilla achieves the best performance against baselines including Mem0 and MemoRAG on five of eight benchmarks spanning personalization, long-document and knowledge-intensive question answering. Notably, on FactKG, it reaches 84.33% accuracy versus 80.43% with full-context inference, while using 108x fewer tokens to represent the source documents. Furthermore, Memorilla can facilitate continual learning in agentic systems where the document count (the agent's past turns) increases throughout the trajectory. In a TextWorld environment, we show that Memorilla reduces the agent's tendency to wander between rooms it has already explored, and improves its average success rate from 18.6% with full-history in-context learning to 24.2%. Overall, Memorilla aids frozen LLMs in accumulating and using knowledge effectively across long-context interactions and takes a step towards building continually learning AI systems.
  </details>

- **[Interpretable Synthetic Medical Tabular Data Generation for Clinical Decision Support Using Fuzzy Cognitive Maps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00391)**  `arXiv:2610.00391`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Michael Vasilakakis, Dimitris K. Iakovidis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Synthetic medical tabular data generation has become essential for developing and validating computer-based medical systems (CBMSs) when real clinical data is restricted due to privacy, ethical, or data availability limitations. Existing probabilistic and deep generative models often lack interpretability and fail to preserve clinically meaningful dependencies, limiting their suitability for safety-critical applications. This paper proposes a novel application of Fuzzy Cognitive Maps (FCMs) in a framework for synthetic medical tabular data generation with explicit causality and privacy preservation. Clinical features are described using linguistically interpretable fuzzy sets, and inter-feature dependencies are encoded as FCM edge weights computed from fuzzy set intersections. Synthetic patient records are generated by propagating randomly initialized linguistic activation vectors through the FCM until convergence, followed by defuzzification to produce clinically coherent numerical values. The approach natively handles mixed data types, and domain constraints common in health records. Experimental evaluation on UCI medical benchmark datasets demonstrates competitive performance under a Train-on-Synthetic-Test-on-Real (TSTR) protocol. The proposed method achieves accuracy of up to 0.81 and AUROC of up to 0.90 on the Heart Disease dataset, matching or exceeding TVAE and Gaussian Copula baselines while running exclusively on CPU. Fidelity metrics including KS Complement (up to 0.91) and Correlation Similarity (up to 0.95) confirm strong statistical coherence, and DCR Baseline Protection scores consistently exceed those of TVAE, confirming adequate privacy guarantees. These results demonstrate that causally grounded, interpretable fuzzy modeling offers a computationally efficient and transparent alternative to deep generative models for trustworthy synthetic data generation in CBMSs.
  </details>

- **[Suan: Rectifying Direct Preference Safety Alignment in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08634)**  `arXiv:2609.08634`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Oleksandr Cherednichenko, Roman Klypa_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Integrating robust safety guardrails into Large Language Models (LLMs) is essential for delivering helpful yet harmless responses. While proprietary systems exhibit reliable safety controls, their underlying methodologies and trade-offs remain largely undisclosed. Achieving comparable security in open-weight models remains a persistent challenge, as post-trained variants frequently suffer from over-refusal and degraded general quality. To overcome these drawbacks, we introduce Suan, a novel preference optimization algorithm. Unlike existing methods, we formulate the optimization objective directly at the gradient level, bypassing the standard variational derivation. As a result, we obtain more interpretable and robust training dynamics. Extensive evaluations across a diverse suite of competitive baselines and benchmarks demonstrate that Suan achieves superior safety alignment while fully preserving response utility.
  </details>

- **[Actionable CBFI: Prescriptive Interventional Diagnosis and Interaction-Aware Escalation for Sparse Causal Algorithmic Recourse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27821)**  `arXiv:2608.27821`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Sejong Oh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Algorithmic recourse tells an individual which feasible actions would change an unfavourable prediction. Feature attribution explains a prediction, but the most important causes are often not the best levers. We propose Actionable Case-Based Feature Importance (A-CBFI), which turns the descriptive decomposition of Localized-CBFI into a prescriptive, interventional diagnosis on a structural causal model. A-CBFI prescribes the cheapest single action when one exists and searches joint actions only when no single action is enough. We show that interventional interaction also arises from path blocking and shared descendants, which model-only interaction indices cannot see, and that averaged interaction scores cancel where additive targeting fails. On ten tabular benchmarks (6,657 recourse queries), A-CBFI was never significantly less successful than an untargeted causal beam search, prescribed fewer direct actions (1.10 vs 1.38) with fewer model evaluations (119 vs 150), and offered substitutable single actions for 68.2% of the queries a single action solves. Success gains appeared mainly where causes are immutable and single actions are insufficient.
  </details>

- **[From Deterministic to Generative Deep Learning for Urban Air Quality Reconstruction from Sparse Observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25687)**  `arXiv:2607.25687`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Abhishek A.Sabnis, Mihai Mitrea, Lya Lugon, Karine Sartelet, Marc Bocquet, Xiaoyuan Cheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Full-field reconstruction of air pollution is essential for evaluating pollution exposure and supporting public health decision-making. However, the complex interactions among pollutants, hard-to-predict weather patterns, and limited monitoring station coverage make this a complex task. We apply deep learning techniques to provide fast and accurate reconstructions from sparse observations of four key pollutants: NO2, O3, PM2.5 and PM10. Models are trained on full-field simulation data and evaluated on real-world observations collected from 9 to 28 monitoring stations in the city of Paris. We introduce a diffusion-based generative framework for multi-pollutant reconstruction and benchmark its performance against deterministic deep learning models. Despite noisy observations and strong spatial variability, the models achieve high structural similarity on simulated validation data and produce realistic spatial patterns on real-world observations, as indicated by power-spectrum analysis. We introduce data augmentation methods that enable transfer to real-world observations without retraining, allowing the models to generalise beyond the training period. These findings highlight the potential of ML models for reliable real-world deployment in air pollution reconstruction tasks.
  </details>

- **[BoLT: A Benchmark to Democratize Black-box Optimization Research for Expensive LLM Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17000)**  `arXiv:2605.17000`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Ruth Wan Theng Chew, Zhiliang Chen, Apivich Hemachandra, Bryan Kian Hsiang Low_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Optimization of LLM training and inference configurations, such as hyperparameters, data mixtures, and prompts, is critical to performance, but it is often approached heuristically in practice, leading to potentially suboptimal outcomes. By framing them as noisy, expensive, and derivative-free optimization problems, Bayesian optimization (BO) and other black-box optimization (BBO) methods offer a promising yet underexplored direction for principled, sample-efficient methods. However, LLM training and inference costs are prohibitively high for most of the BBO research community, and new methods are often only evaluated on synthetic test functions and small-scale datasets that fail to capture the challenges of modern LLM optimization problems. This impedes the development of BBO methods and makes it difficult to assess their effectiveness on modern LLM tasks. We introduce BoLT, the first LLM-centric benchmark that democratizes LLM research for the BBO community. BoLT is released atthis https URL. BoLT covers broad and well-motivated LLM optimization problems, involving multi-fidelity, multi-objective, heteroscedastic noise, and high-dimensional search spaces. Each problem in BoLT is grounded in real experimental data and made fully reproducible and accessible through lightweight surrogate models fitted to the results of thousands of real LLM experiments. We benchmark BoLT against an extensive range of BO and BBO methods, showing that selected BO methods consistently outperform others across tasks and highlighting gaps in existing BBO methods on LLM tasks, underscoring the need to modernize benchmarks for the BBO community.
  </details>

- **[On the Tradeoffs of On-Device Generative Models in Federated Predictive Maintenance Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07860)**  `arXiv:2605.07860`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Usevalad Milasheuski, Piero Baraldi, Enrico Zio, Stefano Savazzi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Federated Learning (FL) has emerged as a promising paradigm for preserving client data ownership and control over distributed Internet of Things (IoT) environments. While discriminative models dominate most FL use cases, recent advances in generative models - such as Variational Autoencoders (VAE), Generative Adversarial Networks (GAN), and Diffusion Models (DM) - offer new opportunities for unsupervised anomaly detection in time series analysis, with relevant applications in predictive maintenance (PdM) in critical industrial infrastructures. In this work, we present a comprehensive analysis of VAEs, GANs, and DMs in the context of federated PdM. We analyze their performance and communication overhead under both full and partial federation setups, where only subsets of model components are shared. Building on split-parameter personalized FL, this paper provides an empirical instantiation of shared/private parameter partitioning for unsupervised generative Time-Series Anomaly Detection (TSAD), mapping the split to analysis and synthesis modules across the generative architectures. Our experiments over five clients and two industrial time-series benchmarks reveal distinct trade-offs in model utility, stability, and communication efficiency in heterogeneous cross-silo FL. For the evaluated GAN-based configurations, full federation improves final anomaly-detection utility relative to independent local training, although these models remain less robust than the VAE- and DDPM-based alternatives. For DMs, decoder sharing matches full federation while substantially reducing the communication payload.
  </details>

- **[Dr. Post-Training: A Data Regularization Perspective on LLM Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07063)**  `arXiv:2605.07063`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Pingbang Hu, Xueshen Liu, Z. Morley Mao, Jiaqi W. Ma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Data selection methods address a critical challenge in LLM post-training: effectively leveraging scarce, high-fidelity target data alongside abundant but imperfectly aligned general training data. In this work, we move beyond the data-selection framing and introduce Dr. Post-Training (Data-Regularized Post-Training), a novel framework that reconceptualizes general training data as a data-induced regularizer that prevents overfitting to the scarce target objective, rather than serving as a pool for selection. Specifically, our framework proposes that, at each training step, we construct a feasible set of model update directions using the general training data, and project the model update direction specified by the scarce target data onto that feasible set. Standard training and existing data selection methods arise as special cases with different choices of the data-induced regularizer, and these methods correspond to different points on a bias-variance spectrum with different regularization strengths. Building on this view, we propose a family of methods offering a richer design space and more flexible bias-variance tradeoffs. For practical LLM-scale use, we introduce careful system optimizations that realize these methods with minimal overhead. Extensive experiments across SFT, RLHF, and RLVR show that our methods outperform standard training and state-of-the-art data selection baselines, with the largest gains where the general and target data are most misaligned, and system benchmarks confirm their efficiency.
  </details>

- **[Posterior-First Neural PDE Simulation: Inferring What Downstream Tasks Need from a Single Field](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.03247)**  `arXiv:2605.03247`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Wenshuo Wang, Fan Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Neural PDE simulators often receive only a single observed field at deployment. In this setting, a field-to-future predictor can collapse distinct latent problem states into the same deterministic interface, losing the ambiguity needed for reliable rollout and downstream decisions. We propose posterior-first neural PDE simulation, a task-to-target-to-interface paradigm: the declared downstream tasks determine the minimal task-sufficient problem state to infer, and the posterior over that state conditions the rollout and downstream queries. This posterior can be supplied by amortized posterior estimators, including simulation-based inference with learned summaries; our contribution is the target, the interface, and their theory. We show that Bayes downstream values factor through this posterior, proper scoring rules on refinement labels identify it as their population-risk minimizer, and deterministic collapse incurs an ambiguity barrier whenever the true posterior is non-Dirac. Synthetic exact-ambiguity experiments show that point-versus-posterior gaps track the predicted barrier. On metadata-hidden PDEBench tasks, posterior recovery reduces pooled rollout nRMSE from 0.175 to 0.132, closing 59.4% of the direct-to-oracle gap. Matched controls attribute the gains to task-aligned targeting and posterior deployment, and the gains persist with a latent-diffusion backbone. These results suggest that single-observation neural PDE simulation should be posterior-first rather than monolithic field-to-future prediction.
  </details>

- **[One-Shot Localisation of the Global Minimum of a Noisy One-Dimensional Function: A Controlled Comparison of an Iterative Neural Minimizer, Set Transformers and Classical Estimators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.03614)**  `arXiv:2604.03614`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Qusay Muzaffar, David Levin, Michael Werman_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study passive global optimisation: from twenty noisy samples of an unknown one-dimensional function, with no further queries, estimate where its global minimum lies. We build a benchmark and a self-checking harness and use them for a controlled comparison. The Neural Function Minimizer (NFM), which we introduce, moves a position across the domain for fifteen steps and re-reads the samples from it at every step; a same-size Set Transformer answers in one pass. In a 2x2 design each has one output head or an added mixture head, with the same data and inputs and three seeds each. Within the training families the two are tied on location error, and the mixture loss lowers it by about 0.45 points of the domain in both. The NFM's answers have lower regret, by 2.90 and 3.12 points of the range with one and two heads (significant after correction, and again on a block of fresh cases), mainly because its right-basin answers lie closer to the floor; a Set Transformer looped to the NFM's computation does not reproduce this within the training families, though it has the lower error on unseen functions (exploratory). The NFM also differs from the Set Transformers in encoder, local read, activation, losses and training time. At near-tied minima the form of the answer decides: point answers hedge in about a quarter of exact ties, mixture modes almost never, mixture means in more than 80%. Gaussian-process estimators, with no training, have the lowest regret and are the most accurate on unseen functions. We will release the benchmark, the models and the harness.
  </details>

- **[Efficient Token-Level Policy Optimization for Masked Diffusion Models via Velocity Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.01563)**  `arXiv:2603.01563`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Chenxing Wei, Jiazhen Kang, Hong Wang, Jianqing Zhang, Hao Jiang, Xiaolong Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) successfully aligns autoregressive models in rigorous domains like mathematical reasoning and code generation. However, applying RLVR to Diffusion Large Language Models (dLLMs) is hindered by intractable sequence-level likelihoods, forcing existing methods to rely on unstable, high-variance approximations. We propose Sequence Likelihood-Free Policy Optimization (LFPO), an efficient framework that circumvents this bottleneck by formulating policy alignment as discrete velocity alignment. Inspired by continuous flow matching, LFPO eschews trajectory-level estimation and directly optimizes denoising logits via contrastive token-level updates, providing a stable, low-variance surrogate. Furthermore, predicting terminal solutions from intermediate states implicitly straightens the generation trajectory, enabling high-quality sampling with significantly fewer denoising iterations. Extensive experiments on reasoning and coding benchmarks demonstrate that LFPO matches or exceeds the accuracy of likelihood-based baselines while substantially accelerating inference.
  </details>

- **[Flowette: Flow Matching with Graphette Priors for Graph Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.23566)**  `arXiv:2602.23566`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Asiri Wijesinghe, Sevvandi Kandanaarachchi, Daniel M. Steinberg, Cheng Soon Ong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study generative modeling of graphs with recurring subgraph motifs. We propose Flowette, a continuous flow matching framework that employs a graph neural network-based transformer to learn a velocity field over graph representations with node and edge attributes. Our model promotes topology-aware alignment through optimal transport-based coupling and preserves structural fidelity through endpoint- and chemistry-aware regularisation. To incorporate domain-driven structural priors, we introduce graphettes, a new probabilistic family of graph structure models that generalize graphons via controlled structural edits for motifs such as rings, stars, and trees. We theoretically analyze the coupling, invariance, and structural properties of the framework, evaluate it on synthetic and molecular benchmarks, and isolate the contributions of the structural prior, the optimal-transport coupling, and the regularisation terms through controlled ablations. Flowette achieves competitive performance overall, attaining state-of-the-art results on several metrics across multiple benchmarks, highlighting the effectiveness of combining structural priors with flow-based training for modeling complex graph distributions.
  </details>

- **[A swap-adversarial framework for domain generalization in pre- and post-stimulation Parkinsonian state classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.10528)**  `arXiv:2602.10528`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Seongwon Jin, Hanseul Choi, Sunggu Yang, Sungho Park, Jibum Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We propose a novel swap-adversarial framework that mitigates high inter-subject variability and the high-dimensional low-sample-size problem in electrocorticography (ECoG) data. It achieves robust domain generalization across ECoG and electroencephalography (EEG)-based brain-computer interface datasets. Our framework integrates (1) robust preprocessing, (2) inter-subject balanced channel swap (ISBCS) for cross-subject augmentation, and (3) domain-adversarial learning (DAL) to suppress subject-specific bias. The ISBCS method is a bio-inspired channel swapping strategy that exchanges only functionally corresponding channels across subjects, guided by a brain map, to mitigate inter-subject distribution differences. The DAL strategy encourages the model to learn task-relevant shared features. In addition, we introduce a new ECoG dataset, the first reproducible benchmark, which is constructed from long-term ECoG recordings of 6-hydroxydopamine-induced rat models and annotated with neural responses measured before and after electrical stimulation. We validate the effectiveness of this framework through extensive experiments under cross-subject, cross-session, and cross-dataset settings. Our results show that the proposed framework exhibits the best and robust generalization performance both for our newly constructed ECoG and public EEG datasets.
  </details>

- **[Scaling Laws for Tool-Use Agents via Controllable Environment Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37648)**  `arXiv:2610.37648`  `cs.LG`  
  _Andrew Szot, Samira Abnar, Omar Attia, Abbas Kazerouni, Michael Kirchhof, Cheng-Yu Hsieh, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We explore how to build performant large language model (LLM) tool-use agents that complete complex, multi-step tasks. Specifically, we study how data composition in synthetically generated tool-use environments affects the training of such agents. To do so, we propose the SynthAPI pipeline, which explicitly controls the diversity of generated environments along the axes of tools, tasks, and the databases on which the tools operate. By systematically varying SynthAPI's generation controls in 458,752 tasks across 849 trained models of four different sizes, we derive a scaling law that predicts downstream tool-use agent generalization based on the scale and composition of the synthesized environments. These predictable relationships reveal several lessons for scaling tool-use agent data. As data budgets grow, scaling tool and database diversity becomes more effective than increasing the number of tasks, and increasing data complexity yields larger gains. Scaling according to these findings yields agents that outperform models trained on data from prior work in large-scale tool-use agent and synthetic environment generation across four evaluation benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Pneuma: A Large-Scale Real-World Time Series Benchmark for Commercial Aircraft Predictive Maintenance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37563)**  `arXiv:2610.37563`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jiafeng Lin, Yutian Zhang, Huan Zhang, Yuxuan Wang, Jinyan Feng, Yuhui Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Predictive maintenance is vital for commercial aviation, yet progress remains hindered by the lack of public, large-scale real-world datasets. As a result, most methods are developed and evaluated on proprietary data, limiting reproducibility. We introduce Pneuma, the first publicly available large-scale benchmark for predictive maintenance using onboard time series from in-service commercial aircraft. Built around bleed air systems, Pneuma contains over 920 million multivariate time steps across four aircraft types and 9 verified fault scenarios, directly aligned with real maintenance logs. The dataset exhibits key real-world challenges, including substantial class imbalance, subtle fault evolution patterns, and significant variance across aircraft configurations. We structure the benchmark around three tasks: multivariate forecasting, supervised fault classification, and unsupervised anomaly detection, with labels derived from actual maintenance records. Under unified settings, we evaluate over 25 models from four methodological families on 9 carefully curated subsets. The results reveal that even strong contemporary models struggle with subtle fault signatures. Further analyses show consistent gains from additional in-context examples and multivariate inference, while forecasting quality is strongly associated with anomaly detection performance. Together, these findings provide practical insights into effectively modeling complex real-world aviation time series. We hope Pneuma will serve as a rigorous and reproducible foundation for advancing data-driven predictive maintenance toward real-world deployment in commercial aviation.
  </details>

- **[GTFormer: A Syntax-Free Graph Network Alternative to Transformers for 8192$\times$ Length Extrapolation in Linear Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37524)**  `arXiv:2610.37524`  `cs.LG`  
  _Elisabeth Fink_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-attention mechanisms have established the Transformer as the dominant backbone across natural language processing, source code intelligence, and automated theorem proving. However, all-to-all pairwise token comparison enforces a quadratic $\mathcal{O}(N^2)$ compute and memory barrier alongside severe softmax attention dilution as sequence length $N$ scales beyond the training horizon. Inspired by recent advances in algebraic graph learning (\textbf{WPNet}), this work introduces \textbf{GTFormer}, a syntax-free hierarchical Graph Neural Network (GNN) that replaces flat self-attention for general long-context sequence modeling. GTFormer organizes raw token streams into a bounded-degree hierarchy combining a balanced binary reduction tree ($\lceil \log_2 N \rceil$ depth), horizontal boundary-bridging shift edges, random expander shortcuts, multi-channel convex highway gating, and a top-down broadcast pass. Across eight long-context code, log, and multi-hop retrieval benchmarks trained exclusively on sequences of length $N \le 512$, GTFormer extrapolates zero-shot to $N = 4{,}194{,}304$ ($8192\times$) on a single commodity GPU in linear $\mathcal{O}(N)$ memory, maintaining near-perfect accuracy where standard and RoPE Transformers suffer from out-of-distribution attention collapse and out-of-memory exhaustion.
  </details>

- **[Euclidean Crowding-Driven Adaptive Euclidean-Hyperbolic Graph Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37373)**  `arXiv:2610.37373`  `cs.LG`  
  _Hao Chen, Jian Sun, Chuan Shi, Hongying Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hyperbolic graph representation learning has emerged as a powerful paradigm for modeling hierarchical data. Unlike Euclidean space, which exhibits polynomial volume growth, hyperbolic space grows exponentially with radius, naturally accommodating tree-like structures without the geometric distortion known as Euclidean crowding. Despite strong empirical performance, the theoretical mechanisms underlying hyperbolic embeddings remain poorly understood. In this work, we provide a geometric quantifcation research for Euclidean crowding, a generalization-theoretic explanation for the superiority of hyperbolic space on hierarchical data, and further develop an adaptive Euclidean-hyperbolic fusion learning framework toward bridging this gap. Specifically, we \textbf{first} establish a geometric quantification of Euclidean crowding by analyzing layer-wise minimum separation in node embeddings, proving that fixed-dimensional Euclidean embeddings of tree-like graphs inevitably exhibit layer-wise crowding that leads to weaker margin-based generalization bounds. \textbf{Second}, we provide a generalization-bound for the superiority of hyperbolic geometry on hierarchical data. \textbf{Third}, we propose EH-Fusion, a geometry-adaptive framework that lifts Euclidean GNN representations into hyperbolic space via exponential mapping and adaptively fuses both geometries based on node-level neighborhood expansion patterns. Extensive experiments validate our theoretical insights and demonstrate the effectiveness of EH-Fusion on benchmark datasets.
  </details>

- **[Structured Representation Probing for Risk Anticipation in End-to-End Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37299)**  `arXiv:2610.37299`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jiawei Liang, Tianrui Lou, Le Yang, Xianghao Jiao, Wei Wang, Xiaochun Cao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  End-to-end driving policies form structured internal representations of traffic, ego behavior, and road constraints. These representations offer a monitoring interface for anticipating failures, complementing risk signals from trajectories, controls, and manually designed features. We introduce the Structured Representation Probe (SRP), a lightweight monitor that reads actor, ego, and map representations from a frozen policy. SRP learns a separate linear risk direction for each source and aggregates element scores through cardinality-normalized log-mean-exp pooling, retaining sensitivity to salient evidence while reducing dependence on token count. Event-window supervision links the readout to upcoming hard failures, and auxiliary event type heads jointly train the source directions across failure modes. Online alerting uses a single generic score without modifying the driving policy. Bench2Drive experiments on DriveTransformer, UniAD, and VAD show higher frame-level PR-AUC and ROC-AUC than the evaluated runtime baselines. On DriveTransformer at a horizon of 2 seconds, ROC-AUC reaches 0.833 versus 0.715 for temporal consistency, with 2.67% readout runtime overhead. The trained probes also retain the ability to distinguish impending failures on new routes.
  </details>

- **[Sharpness-Aware Muon: Matrix-Aware Optimization with Momentum-Guided Perturbations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37225)**  `arXiv:2610.37225`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhiwei Zhai, Wenjing Yan, Ying-Jun Angela Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Muon is a matrix-aware optimizer that combines momentum with orthogonalization to produce structured update directions with strong empirical behavior. However, it only shapes the geometry of the update itself and does not explicitly control neighborhood worst-case loss or bias training toward flat regions, which are often linked to stronger generalization. To address this limitation, we introduce the sharpness-aware formulation of Muon. We first develop \textsc{MuSAM}, which incorporates Sharpness-Aware Minimization (SAM)-style perturbation into Muon's orthogonalized update and establishes a principled bridge between sharpness-aware training and matrix-aware optimization. We then show that this formulation faces an efficiency--stability tension: constructing the perturbation requires an extra gradient evaluation, while the perturbation itself is generated from a single minibatch gradient and is therefore noisy. To resolve this issue, we propose \textsc{MoMuSAM}, which turns Muon's momentum from an update variable into a sharpness-probing signal. By using normalized momentum as the perturbation direction, \textsc{MoMuSAM} removes the extra clean-gradient computation and replaces the single-batch perturbation proxy with a temporally aggregated one. We prove that both \textsc{MuSAM} and \textsc{MoMuSAM} attain the same asymptotic first-order stationarity order, and further show that \textsc{MoMuSAM} yields a more stable stochastic linearization than \textsc{MuSAM}. Experiments on standard benchmarks demonstrate that sharpness awareness consistently improves Muon.
  </details>

- **[CaLLM: Continual Adaptation of LLMs via Non-Parametric Adapter Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36994)**  `arXiv:2610.36994`  `cs.LG`  
  _Bilge Celik, Anna Vettoruzzo, Joaquin Vanschoren_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continual adaptation of large language models (LLMs) to evolving task distributions while mitigating catastrophic forgetting remains an open problem. Existing strategies often struggle to scale or lack a robust mechanism for zero-shot task-to-expert assignment. We introduce CaLLM, a framework that resolves these tensions by combining metric-based meta-learning with modular parameter isolation via lightweight adapters. First, CaLLM projects each input batch into a task-agnostic embedding space to find its nearest prototype. It then routes the batch to the specific adapter specialized for that data distribution. This formulation provides (i)~controllable cross-task interference by design, (ii)~constant computational complexity regardless of task count, (iii)~seamless integration of novel distributions through prototype expansion, and (iv)~regime agnosticism as the local routing decision enables a unified approach to both online and offline settings. Our experiments demonstrate that this routing mechanism discovers semantically coherent task groupings while facilitating constructive interference between similar tasks, and keeping overhead at a minimum. Across diverse and fine-grained continual learning benchmarks, CaLLM consistently outperforms traditional isolation and replay methods, establishing prototype-based modular routing as a principled and scalable approach to lifelong adaptation of LLMs.
  </details>

- **[Live-OPSD: On-Policy Self-Distillation for Proactive Streaming Large Video Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36959)**  `arXiv:2610.36959`  `cs.LG`  
  _Kaixuan Du, Xin Wan, Dai Guan, Ming Chen, Xiaoxi Jiang, Guanjun Jiang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Streaming multimodal assistants must make response decisions while events are still unfolding. They need to recognize when the available evidence warrants a response, produce an appropriate continuation, and remain silent otherwise. Supervised fine-tuning learns from recorded assistant turns, but does not directly train on the response prefixes produced by the evolving policy itself. We introduce Live-OPSD, an on-policy self-distillation method that uses privileged hints available only during training to supervise the policy on its own outputs. The policy first generates a continuation from the streaming context alone. The same model then re-evaluates that continuation with a hint describing the relevant evidence, and the resulting distributions guide the unhinted policy. On Qwen3-Omni-30B-A3B, Live-OPSD attains an average score of 59.64 on LiveProBench, 5.26 points above the strongest continued-SFT baseline, and stays competitive on OVO-Bench (65.84). These results indicate that guiding the policy on its own outputs with privileged hints improves both when and how a streaming assistant responds.
  </details>

- **[RankFormer: Rethinking Time Series Transformers through Representation Capacity Analysis and Progressive Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36703)**  `arXiv:2610.36703`  `cs.LG`  
  _Peiwang Tang, Cheng HE, Xijie Liang, Defu Lian_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multivariate time series forecasting plays a critical role in Web and cyber-physical systems, where large-scale services and IoT infrastructures generate complex temporal data. Transformer-based models have achieved strong empirical performance in this domain, yet the principles governing the expressive capacity of their learned representations remain insufficiently understood. Moreover, many existing models rely on simplified decoding strategies, including channel-independent modeling and direct projection heads, which limit their ability to capture complex multivariate dynamics. To address these challenges, we propose RankFormer, a novel encoder-decoder architecture for multivariate time series forecasting. We establish a rank-based analysis framework to investigate how tokenization, embedding, and attention designs affect the representation capacity of time series Transformers. Our analysis reveals that patchification increases the potential representation capacity by aggregating multiple temporal observations into each token, while subsequent encoding operations determine how much of this capacity is realized. Guided by these insights, we develop a rank-enhancing encoder with Hourglass Attention to improve rank stability during information interaction. Furthermore, we introduce the Interactive Progressive Decoder (IPD), which improves future trajectory modeling through global-guided cross-channel interaction and progressive refinement. Extensive experiments on real-world long-term forecasting benchmarks demonstrate that RankFormer achieves competitive performance against state-of-the-art methods. Code repository:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[CoHARL: Co-Evolving Agent Policies and Executable Harnesses with Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36469)**  `arXiv:2610.36469`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jianwen Chen, Peng Xia, Kaide Zeng, Siwei Han, Zhen-Yu Zhang, Charles Fleming, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents interact with their environments through executable harnesses that govern tool use and feedback. Optimizing a harness for a fixed policy overlooks how its behavior changes during learning, while training a policy under a fixed harness leaves the interface that generates its training experience unchanged. This motivates adapting the harness throughout policy training. We introduce CoHARL, a population-based reinforcement learning framework that co-evolves a shared policy and a population of executable harnesses. The policy learns from rollouts across the population, and a proposer uses its execution traces to revise harness code during training. CoHARL achieves a 27.6% mean resolved rate on SWE-rebench, improving over harness-only search by 6.4 percentage points. Experiments on SWE-bench Verified and Terminal-Bench 2.0 show gains in software engineering and terminal-based tasks. Under matched training budgets, CoHARL also outperforms searching for a harness once before RL and alternating policy updates with revisions to a single active harness.
  </details>

- **[Auto-Research Arena: Benchmarking and Advancing Auto-Research in Language Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35866)**  `arXiv:2610.35866`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xi Chen, Chongjian Ge, Yiran Xu, Yiqun Mei, Henrik I. Christensen, Hao Tan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As autonomous research systems become more capable, benchmarks must both evaluate their performance and support meaningful research progress. We introduce Auto-Research Arena, a multi-target benchmark for evaluating autonomous research systems and discovering ideas in language modeling. Arena supports diverse research objectives within a shared experimental environment by separating admissibility from improvement: model quality and resource constraints determine whether a result qualifies, while the selected target measures progress among admissible results. We instantiate Arena in language model pretraining with nine targets spanning model, data, training, and inference efficiency, and evaluate seven research harnesses using two foundation models. Arena clearly differentiates existing harnesses across research objectives, with several sophisticated methods outperformed by our simple sequential search baseline, indicating substantial headroom in current auto-research systems. Beyond evaluation, Arena reveals a substantially broader frontier of model and system ideas aligned with active research directions. Reusing these discoveries in a new quality-driven search yields lower training loss under matched data and FLOPs and lower held-out BPB at the same model size. Together, Arena provides a common framework for benchmarking autonomous research and expanding its scope in language modeling. The code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[INTRA: Interleaved Non-contiguous Token spaRse Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34056)**  `arXiv:2610.34056`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yuxuan Xia, Wenbo Lu, Shaoyi Zheng, Shengjie Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Transformers achieve strong performance across modalities but are bottlenecked by the quadratic cost of attention. Sparse attention can reduce this cost, but effective sparsity must preserve useful long-range communication and map well to GPU hardware. We introduce INTRA--Interleaved Non-contiguous Token spaRse Attention--a hardware-aware static sparse attention design based on a relay-communication view under Computational Query Set (CQS) constraints. Rather than directly covering all token-to-token interactions within each sparse layer, INTRA uses complementary sparse groups to provide short mediated communication paths across layers. Scatter forms relay groups that sample broadly across local neighborhoods, while Gather reconnects these groups in subsequent layers. Under a partition model, this structure guides CQS-compatible sparse patterns with global reachability. Our CQS-aware FlashAttention-style kernel computes structured indices at runtime, loads selected non-contiguous tokens, and packs them densely for computation. On an A100, INTRA achieves a $1.53\times$ speedup in $2K$ FLUX.1-dev generation, with better measured image quality than evaluated CLEAR configurations after LoRA self-distillation. On LLaMA-3.1-8B, it improves long-context prefill efficiency with strong teacher-forced retrieval performance under matched LoRA adaptation. Attention-only benchmarks further show up to $8.4\times$ and $10.4\times$ speedups over MoBA and density-constrained NSA, respectively. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Same Luck, Different Choice: Twin-World Credit Assignment for Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31544)**  `arXiv:2610.31544`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zeropy Surio, Tobias Engel, Mei-Lin Chou, Anika Raman, Samuel Okafor_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic reinforcement learning rewards a complete episode, yet the policy needs credit for every decision inside it. Counterfactual branching estimates this credit by re-running the episode after an alternative decision, but the branch draws its sampling noise independently of the factual episode, so the outcome difference mixes the effect of the decision with the luck of everything that follows. We observe that in agentic search the retriever, the corpus, and the grader are deterministic, so an episode is a deterministic function of the policy's decisions and its sampling noise. Twin worlds are therefore realizable: the same noise, a different decision. We introduce TWICE (TWIn-world Credit Estimation), which keys the Gumbel noise of every token by turn and line, rolls out counterfactual twins that inherit the factual episode's downstream noise, and turns their paired outcome differences into turn-level advantages for policy-gradient updates on actor-sampled trajectories; only the actor is deployed. Twin credit is unbiased for any coupling quality, its variance is that of an independent counterfactual scaled by $1-\rho$, where $\rho$ is the twins' outcome correlation, and under coupling the number of rollouts needed to resolve an advantage of size $\Delta$ falls from $\Theta(\Delta^{-2})$ to $\Theta(\Delta^{-1})$. A square-root rule spends the twin budget where outcomes are predicted to disagree per unit cost. On seven question-answering benchmarks, TWICE improves the average exact match of GRPO by 5.3 and 4.5 points on Qwen2.5-3B and 7B, outperforms tree-search and counterfactual-branching baselines, and uses 68% fewer auxiliary tokens than VinePPO.
  </details>

- **[Maestro: Agentic Formal Proof Development by Coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25920)**  `arXiv:2610.25920`  `cs.LG`  
  _Haoyu Li, Zeren Shui, Yuhao Zhang, Ferhat Erata, Benjamin Mikek, Murali Krishna Ramanathan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Lean is increasingly used to verify mathematical proofs and software produced by LLM agents, but writing formal proofs remains hard. Existing prover systems take a divide-and-conquer approach that recursively decomposes hard proof goals into easier lemmas and splits again any lemma that resists proof. However, a lemma can resist proof for different reasons, including insufficient guidance, a false statement, or a flaw in the decomposition that produced it. Existing systems cannot tell these apart as a failed attempt reports little beyond its failure, and responses are confined to the failing node without considering the rest of the proof or revising the plan above it. The system may thus keep decomposing obligations along a flawed proof direction until the budget runs out, causing the whole proof attempt to fail. We introduce Maestro, a Lean prover harness that handles failures through global coordination. Provers report why they failed, not just that they failed, and a coordinator that sees the whole proof chooses an appropriate response, such as guiding a stuck prover, correcting a false lemma, or retracting a flawed subtree and replanning it. The same harness extends to verified code synthesis, where a checked counterexample sends the code back for repair. We evaluate Maestro with four frontier LLMs on seven mathematics and three software verification benchmarks. It proves more targets than each model's native coding agent (Claude Code or Codex) on every benchmark the agent has not already saturated, with the largest gains on research-level mathematics. With Claude Opus 5, it discovers its own proofs of 34 of the 48 theorems in the June 2026 release of arXivLean, against 19 for the native agent. It also outperforms all published prover systems on every benchmark they share, solving 669/672 PutnamBench, 59/60 Lean-IMO-Bench, and 6/6 IMO 2026.
  </details>

- **[Trajectory-Aware Policy Optimization: Closing the State Distribution Gap in Long-Horizon LLM RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13020)**  `arXiv:2610.13020`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhicheng Cai, Hanlin Wu, Weinan Dai, Yuyan Zhou, Wei-Ying Ma, Ya-Qin Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) has become a dominant paradigm for enhancing LLMs' reasoning capabilities. Yet long-horizon autoregressive rollouts introduce a fundamental limitation: local policy mismatch can compound into severe state distribution shift over trajectories. Existing LLM RL methods, such as GRPO and GSPO, control policy mismatch at the token or sequence level, but overlook this trajectory-induced shift, causing training instability and eventual collapse as the horizon grows. To close the state distribution gap, we propose Trajectory-Aware Policy Optimization (TAPO), a principled framework that theoretically derives the state-distribution correction from the off-policy policy gradient and incorporates it into the RL objective. TAPO further employs a geometric-mean estimator for variance reduction, together with a length-aware dynamic clipping that prevents overly deviated updates across the trajectory. Extensive experiments demonstrate that TAPO significantly surpasses existing LLM RL algorithms across eight competition-level benchmarks, achieving up to 48% and 12% improvement over GRPO on mathematical reasoning and algorithmic coding, with substantially enhanced training stability.
  </details>

- **[Bastion: Budget-Aware Speculative Decoding with Tree-structured Block Diffusion Drafting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29727)**  `arXiv:2605.29727`  `cs.LG`  
  _Soowon Oh, Nam Cao, Yujin Kim, Hojung Jung, Huzama Ahmad, Sangmin Bae, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Block-diffusion drafters have recently emerged as a powerful alternative for speculative decoding by predicting multiple future-token distributions in a single parallel step. However, since these parallel predictions are sampled from position-wise marginals rather than fully conditioned sequences, committing to a single greedy path often fails to capture the target model's preferred trajectory. To address this, we propose BASTION, a budget-aware speculative decoding framework with tree-based diffusion drafting. Unlike existing methods that rely on static tree topologies, BASTION dynamically constructs query-dependent trees by balancing draft quality against hardware constraints. Our framework integrates three synergistic components: (1) an acceptance surrogate that estimates expected accepted length via path confidence, (2) an online latency estimator that calibrates a hardware-aware roofline model, and (3) an adaptive best-first expansion that grows the tree until marginal gains no longer justify incremental verification costs. BASTION is training-free, preserves the target model's distribution, and requires no per-setting tuning. Across diverse benchmarks and GPU architectures, BASTION achieves up to a $6.61\times$ speedup over standard autoregressive decoding, outperforming state-of-the-art block-diffusion baselines by 39%.
  </details>

- **[Twincher: Towards Robust and Precise Physical AI via Bijective Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.13470)**  `arXiv:2605.13470`  `cs.LG`  
  _Arkady Gonoskov_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Perception and planning in physical systems often require inverting a continuous forward map $p \mapsto y = f(p)$, e.g., a renderer or a simulator, that can be queried but not inverted in closed form. Learned inverses are limited by their approximation error, whereas iterative solvers in observation space can stall at spurious stationary points. We instead learn a representation $r(y)$, insensitive to perturbations of $y$ normal to the data manifold, in which $p \mapsto r(f(p))$ is a well-conditioned bijection. In these coordinates, the least-squares inverse problem has no spurious interior stationary points, so that Gauss-Newton iteration with the exact forward model converges to the accuracy the data permit; the learned model need only be qualitatively correct. We introduce Twincher, an exactly invertible architecture of localized area-preserving rotations, trained with Jacobian-level objectives and a domain-growing curriculum. On synthetic maps with tunable nonconvexity, the worst-case residual reaches machine precision within five iterations once the query budget suffices, whereas an MLP-based baseline improves only gradually. In 6D localization of industrial objects in real depth images of the MVTec ITODD benchmark, given instance masks, twinchers trained only on CAD renderings make the iteration converge from a fixed initial guess within a few steps, without feature matching, pose regression, or pose hypotheses; in observation space, the same iteration fails for up to half of the views. All validation poses pass the strictest BOP thresholds and fit the sensor data about as closely as the ground-truth poses; 80\% of all 239 instances (67\% with visible masks only) are solved in agreement with the sensor data, and the number of training iterations grew only 1.5-fold from two to four pose parameters. Code is open source.
  </details>

- **[In-Context Multi-Operator Learning with DeepOSets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.16074)**  `arXiv:2512.16074`  `cs.LG`  
  _Shao-Ting Chiu, Aditya Nambiar, Ali Syed, Jonathan W. Siegel, Ulisses Braga-Neto_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  An important application of neural networks in scientific computing is non-linear operator learning. In this framework, a neural network is trained to fit a non-linear map between infinite dimensional spaces, for example, the solution operator of ordinary and partial differential equations. Recently, inspired by the discovery of in-context learning for large language models, an even more ambitious paradigm has been explored, called multi-operator learning. In this approach, a neural network is trained to learn many different operators at the same time. At inference time, the network is passed a small number of example input and output pairs to disambiguate the desired operator. In this work, we provide a precise mathematical formulation of the multi-operator learning problem, propose a parameter-efficient, attention-free architecture, called DeepOSets, and prove its universality in the in-context multi-operator learning task. We perform experiments demonstrating the efficacy of DeepOSets on several benchmarks, including Burgers' equation, 1D and 2D Darcy flow, and a variety of initial-value and boundary-value differential equations. In a comparison with the transformer-based ICON model on the 1D Darcy problem, under a small training budget (10k steps), DeepOSets attained lower error at every context size with 19% of the parameters, while with an extended budget (100k steps), ICON attains 26-41% lower error, but at the cost of roughly 218$\times$ more training time, 12$\times$ higher inference latency, and 5.3$\times$ more parameters.
  </details>

- **[SeeNav-Agent: Enhancing Vision-Language Navigation with Visual Prompt and Step-Level Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.02631)**  `arXiv:2512.02631`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhengcheng Wang, Zichuan Lin, Yijun Yang, Haobo Fu, Deheng Ye_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing Vision-Language Navigation (VLN) agents based on Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) often suffer from errors of perception, reasoning, and planning, which significantly hinder their navigation performance. To address these limitations, a novel VLN agent framework, named SeeNav-Agent, is proposed. First, to reduce hallucinations of the visual module of the VLN agent, a dual-view Visual Prompt (VP) technique is introduced, which can also improve the agent's understanding of current spatial states. Subsequently, a novel step-level Reinforcement Fine-Tuning (RFT) method, Step Reward Group Policy Optimization (SRGPO), is designed for post-training. In SRGPO, we first define verifiable process rewards for navigation, and then perform efficient step-level advantage estimation by randomly grouping navigation steps. SRGPO provides dense reward signals for the RFT of the VLN agent and enhances its planning capability. Experiments on both the EmbodiedBench Navigation (Embench-Nav) and VLN-CE validate the effectiveness of the proposed method. By introducing the zero-shot VP module, the GPT-4.1 achieves a navigation success rate of 86.7%, surpassing the current best LVLM by approximately 20 percentage points (pp) on Embench-Nav and achieves an 11.1 pp improvement on VLN-CE compared to the base model. With VP+SRGPO, the Qwen2.5-VL-3B model reaches a navigation success rate of 72.3%, outperforming the best existing LVLM model by 5.6 pp on Embench-Nav and achieves 8.9 pp improvement on VLN-CE compared to the baseline method. Moreover, compared to GRPO and GiGPO, the proposed SRGPO demonstrates significant improvements in training stability, convergence efficiency, and generalization capability.
  </details>
