Specifically, interaction-free multi-granular recall pre-computes gallery embeddings and matches them with the corresponding query embedding without full-gallery token-level text-image interaction, while text-guided cross-modal reranking with BTIB refines local semantic correspondence within the top-K candidate set. Furthermore, an inter- and intra-modal loss is designed to jointly optimize cross-modal alignment across multi-granular representations. Extensive experiments on public benchmarks demonstrate that the FTF achieves competitive retrieval accuracy while significantly improving retrieval efficiency compared with existing methods.
  </details>

- **[Hy-Nomas: Towards Free-form Interactive World Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36729)**  `arXiv:2610.36729`  `cs.CV`  
  _Qiaoling Chen, Jiaxiang Cheng, Xueqing Deng, Tiankai Hang, Ji Hou, Jiaqi Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Interactive world generation requires models to follow successive commands while preserving relevant history and action consequences. We present Hy-Nomas, a video world model with an 8B diffusion backbone that supports free-form text instructions and continuous camera control across multiple interaction turns. It generates variable-length segments conditioned on current inputs and cached history. Our central design aligns structured video captions with action-aware causal attention: descriptive context remains accessible across segments, while action instructions are restricted to their intended temporal windows. A vision-language Prompt Enhancer (PE) translates user requests and multimodal interaction histories into structured conditions for the next segment. A multi-stage curriculum progressively introduces causal generation, text interaction, camera control, and training on model-generated histories, followed by diffusion distillation. In human evaluation, Hy-Nomas shows superior performance over existing world models. We release model weights, code and benchmarks, together with a lightweight adaptation workflow, to support reproducible research on interactive world generation.
  </details>

- **[SAGE: Semantic-Augmented Geometric Experts for Point Cloud Registration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36658)**  `arXiv:2610.36658`  `cs.CV`  
  _Tomoyasu Shimada, Kazuhiko Murasaki, Shoichiro Takeda, Shogo Sato, Riku Inoue, Toshihiko Nishimura, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Point cloud registration is an essential task for scene reconstruction, requiring the alignment of partially overlapping point clouds. Under low-overlap conditions, this task remains challenging because existing geometry-only methods cannot distinguish geometrically similar but semantically distinct structures (e.g., TV corners vs. desk corners and chair legs vs. table legs), resulting in incorrect correspondences. To address this geometric ambiguity, we present Semantic-Augmented Geometric Experts (SAGE), which integrates frozen pre-trained point cloud semantic representations into the registration pipeline without requiring additional sensors or fine-tuning the semantic encoder. To effectively leverage these semantic distinctions, SAGE employs a Semantic-Guided Mixture of Experts (SG-MoE) architecture. Through semantic-aware routing, SG-MoE enables each expert to specialize in distinct semantic-geometric patterns, reducing mismatches that confound geometry-only methods. With a structure-aware estimator, SAGE achieves the highest registration recall on 3DLoMatch while remaining competitive on 3DMatch. When evaluated with the same number of refinement iterations, SAGE further achieves the highest inlier ratio and registration recall on both benchmarks. These results indicate that frozen semantic representations provide effective cues for resolving geometric ambiguity in low-overlap registration.
  </details>

- **[4D Reconstruction with Events & RGB](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36610)**  `arXiv:2610.36610`  `cs.CV`  
  _Dulanga Weerakoon, Darshana Rathnayake, Van Minh Do, Hemanth Sabbella, Kaichen Zhou, Fangneng Zhan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reconstructing dynamic 3D scenes over time (4D reconstruction) from a single moving camera is central to robotics and AR/VR applications, yet state-of the-art feed-forward methods rely solely on RGB frames and therefore inherit the failure modes of conventional cameras, including motion blur and temporal aliasing under fast or non-uniform camera and object motion. Event cameras report per-pixel brightness changes asynchronously at microsecond resolution, offering a complementary signal that is largely immune to these artifacts, but whether this modality can be incorporated into existing RGB based 4D reconstruction pipelines without a full architectural redesign remains an open question. In this preliminary study, we investigate a minimal extension: we augment Monst3R, an existing RGB-only 4D reconstruction model, with an additional event-camera input branch, leaving the rest of the architecture unchanged. Across 3 benchmark datasets, this simple RGB-event fusion reduces Abs Rel by 3.4% on average relative to the RGB-only Monst3R baseline, and a sensitivity analysis shows that the improvement grows with the magnitude of camera and object motion, with the largest gains observed under high object motion conditions where RGB-only reconstruction degrades most. These early results suggest that even a lightweight, non-invasive fusion of event data can meaningfully improve 4D reconstruction robustness, motivating a more thorough architectural investigation in future work.
  </details>

- **[Identity or Evidence? Auditing Evidence Dependence in Agentic Life-Science Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36510)**  `arXiv:2610.36510`  `cs.CV`  
  _Danai Brilli, Maria Georgila_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic life-science benchmarks give an agent evidence about a named entity and score an answer against a recorded outcome. That score tests reasoning over evidence only if the evidence, rather than the name, determines the answer. We examine this distinction by varying identity and evidence independently on a clinical-outcome benchmark and published tasks from Biomni and LAB-Bench. Blinding identity matters little when a prompt contains a decisive sequence or passage. It matters a great deal when the prompt is mostly a name and candidate list (0.742 named versus 0.082 blinded; chance 0.093). Across 96 LAB-Bench LitQA2 questions, the gap increases under one fixed passage-redaction scheme (+0.000 to +0.135; per-instance slope +0.147, p= 0.004). On the clinical benchmark, a no-evidence recall probe matches prediction accuracy and recovers a database-specific programme count. So, we argue that benchmarks intended to measure evidence-dependent reasoning should report identity-blinded results, retrieval telemetry, and positive controls.
  </details>

- **[ARISE: Authentic Reference-Informed Stability Enhancement for Continual Multimodal Forgery Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36509)**  `arXiv:2610.36509`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yuchen Zhang, Yaxiong Wang, Yujiao Wu, Lianwei Wu, Qi Zhang, Li Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal forgery detection is commonly studied under a static assumption: training attacks are expected to approximate those encountered after deployment. This assumption becomes increasingly fragile as generators, editing systems, and multimodal large language models continually introduce new forgery mechanisms and forensic evidence. We formulate this problem as attack-incremental continual multimodal forgery detection, where the binary real/fake interface remains fixed while emerging attacks must be learned without forgetting previous ones. We introduce Fable-Bench, which organizes attack evolution through complementary joint-modality, text-side, and image-side protocols. We further propose ARISE (Authentic Reference-Informed Stability Enhancement), motivated by the open-ended forged class and the comparatively reusable geometry of authentic content. ARISE fits a real-only Gaussian mixture model to projected authentic replay representations as a compact historical reference. Meanwhile, the proposed Gaussian-Responsibility-Aware Stability Preservation (GRASP) derives a sample-adaptive anisotropic metric that strongly constrains historically stable representation directions while retaining plasticity elsewhere. Our Vulnerability-Aware Replay (VAR) further estimates stage-wise vulnerability from authentic-density degradation, forged-to-authentic boundary confusion, and authentic-mode occupancy drift, then assigns bounded replay emphasis to fragile stages. All adaptive signals are obtained from training-time replay statistics, while inference uses only the original multimodal backbone and binary classification head. Experiments across three attack-evolution protocols and multiple detector architectures demonstrate improved historical retention and final average performance over representative continual-learning baselines.
  </details>

- **[SO-101 Bench: Accessible Spatial Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36420)**  `arXiv:2610.36420`  `cs.CV`  
  _Truman Hickok_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Low-cost arms such as the SO-101 have become the most common entry point to robot learning, yet no released policy performs spatially constrained, language-conditioned tasks on them out of the box, and existing SO-101 benchmarks run only on physical hardware with simple tasks and few held-out objects. We introduce SO-101 Bench, a simulated benchmark for language-conditioned spatial manipulation built on an Isaac Lab digital twin of a real SO-101 setup. It has four tasks scored as five families, 138 objects in seen, unseen-instance and unseen-class splits, 916 evaluation episodes with subsets for held-out appearances, rotate-to-fit planning and occlusion memory, and 3,629 simulated demonstrations on which every learned policy is fine-tuned under an equal compute budget. The twin ranks policies as the real robot does: five policies trained without real data, four VLAs and an LLM agent, keep their exact ranking when the same weights run on the physical arm in an unseen environment with unseen objects (Spearman rho = 1.00, MMRV = 0.000), although simulation overestimates success. Across nine VLAs and world-action models, the best learned policies score near 61%, with spatial placement, unseen object classes and rotate-to-fit planning remaining open. An LLM agent that writes code and hands grasping to a learned policy scores 83.2%, and an ablation shows that this lead comes from the choice of harness and the choice of language model.
  </details>

- **[BoxWorld: Let World Engines Control and Video Generators Render](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36371)**  `arXiv:2610.36371`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zike Wu, Ahmed Hatem, Chintan Trivedi, Wallace Michel Pinto Lira, Xuebin Qin, Lele Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We tackle the challenge of building real-time interactive video worlds with user-defined behaviors. Such worlds must track object states, apply interaction rules, and render the resulting consequences as users act. Yet existing end-to-end video world models typically learn interaction dynamics within the generator, making it difficult to specify object-level rules and reason about their consequences. In this work, we introduce BoxWorld, which separates interaction reasoning from generative rendering. Given user-specified rules, a coding agent creates a Box World Engine that determines interaction outcomes and exposes the resulting object states through a lightweight interface of layout boxes, persistent object attributes, and dynamic status variables, leaving appearance and physical effects to generative rendering. A Box-State Generative Renderer then renders this evolving state stream into video autoregressively, enabling online control over object motion, state switches, and conditional events. Rendering this state stream is non-trivial: the renderer must respond to object-level state updates in real time while remaining coherent with the generated history. To this end, we introduce a three-stage training strategy that learns object grounding from open-domain videos and state transitions from gameplay videos, both annotated automatically, and finally performs rollout distillation on self-generated histories. We further propose PairedStateBench, a paired benchmark for fine-grained interaction control. BoxWorld substantially outperforms end-to-end world models and remains competitive across multiple public benchmarks. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Diagnosing and Correcting Bias in MLLM for Long Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36210)**  `arXiv:2610.36210`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xusheng Liang, Jianqiao Sun, Hao Zhang, Yulei Niu, Hengshuang Zhao, Jiawei Ma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-video question answering is challenging since the answer often hinges on a few decisive moments scattered throughout the video, while memory constraints force Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to sample frames at extremely low rates, resulting in sparsity that risks omitting key evidence. Though aggressively enlarging the context size is intuitive, we argue that performance remains fundamentally constrained by the bias, \emph{i.e.}, the misleading cues caused by spurious \textit{linguistic} correlations and salient yet irrelevant \textit{visual} observations. In this paper, we first introduce {S-MME}, a benchmark designed to systematically diagnose shortcut-biased behavior in MLLMs. To mitigate this failure, we further propose a training-free framework, Counterfactual Long-video Evidence-Aware Reasoning (CLEAR). By treating the full video-question pair as a complete view, CLEAR estimates bias effects via augmented views by only keeping the question or locally salient visual clips. Then, we intervene hidden states to mitigate the discrepancy at inference such that the correct answer can be predicted confidently. With experimental studies across five benchmarks, we show that our method is generalizable and can be integrated in different MLLMs, yielding consistent gains in prediction and robustness. We hope CLEAR can offer a complementary direction in test-time scaling for long-video understanding.
  </details>

- **[Any3R: Feedforward 3D Reconstruction from Any Camera Type](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36150)**  `arXiv:2610.36150`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jingong Chen, Qingwen Zhang, Youming Deng, Chulwoo Pack, Olaf Wysocki, Boris Jutzi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Geometry foundation models recover camera poses and dense geometry from a set of images with a feed-forward network, but they expect pinhole images. That assumption is inside the backbone too: the positional encoding and the training loss both bind an image position to a direction in space, and both depend on the lens. We condition both on the dense ray field, the unit direction through every pixel, which describes any central camera without a lens type, distortion coefficients or a bound on field of view. Angular coordinates give the rotary encoding the measured ray angle of each patch, evaluated per view, and solid-angle weighting scales the point loss by the area each pixel covers on the sphere, so neither the encoding nor the supervision depends on the lens. Any3R is therefore a feed-forward model that takes any central camera, and is robust to mixing many camera types per scene, with no retraining per lens. On a benchmark of thirteen central cameras across three datasets, where every method reconstructs with its own predicted camera poses, it is the most consistently accurate method. Any3R enables scalable 3D reconstruction from heterogeneous crowdsourced and archival imagery collections sourced across diverse camera types. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Learning and Reasoning with Geometry: How Generation Helps Spatial Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36022)**  `arXiv:2610.36022`  `cs.CV`  
  _Rui Yang, Yanwei Li, Sili Chen, Jingjia Huang, Yi Lin, Hengshuang Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Image and video generators can produce spatially consistent visual content, but how to use this capability to improve spatial understanding remains underexplored. We study this problem from two perspectives: geometric supervision during training and explicit geometric evidence during inference. To this end, we introduce a unified geometric understanding and generation model (GeoUG) that integrates depth and multi-view generation with spatial understanding. First, we design a multi-scale depth codec that enables metric-depth generation using a pretrained visual autoencoder, and show that joint training yields task-dependent gains in spatial understanding. Second, we introduce 3DCueBench to control geometric difficulty while balancing selected 2D cues, and find that generated depth provides larger reasoning gains on questions requiring fine-grained geometric discrimination. Building on this finding, we develop adaptive interleaved reasoning, in which GeoUG first drafts an answer and then decides whether to generate geometric evidence to confirm or revise it. We train this decision through reinforcement learning, balancing accuracy gains over retaining the draft against generation cost. GeoUG achieves circular accuracies of 97.9%, 90.8%, and 94.2% on CV-Bench-3D, 3DCueBench, and DA-2K with fewer generation calls than always generating.
  </details>

- **[VibeVideoBench: Evaluating Multimodal Agents on Code-Driven Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35864)**  `arXiv:2610.35864`  `cs.CV`  
  _Muxi Diao, Wenbin Xu, Lele Yang, Xuyang Ma, Yuzhe Chen, Jiankang Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion video models produce striking clips, but they cannot reliably render an exact formula, a correct chart, a rule-consistent game or narration synchronized to on-screen events. A different route is emerging: multimodal agents that write code to render every frame and synthesize every sound. We introduce VibeVideoBench, a benchmark for evaluating agents on code-driven video generation. Its 239 self-contained tasks in English and Chinese span 12 categories, from data stories and music-driven pieces to classroom lessons and paper explainers; each asks an agent, running in its native harness inside a sandbox with no image or video generation model, to deliver a finished MP4 with sound within one hour. Evaluation is hierarchical and evidence-based: program checks of the delivery spec, about 13k task-specific rubric checks traced to the requirements they enforce, and five quality dimensions anchored per task, scored by three VLM judge panels. Across nine frontier agent systems and 2,151 scored rollouts, GPT-6 Astra and Claude Opus 5.5 rank first and second with both the judges and human annotators, and the strongest system satisfies every core requirement on 76.3% of tasks. Delivery is a second axis: counting missing videos as zero changes the ranking, and three quarters of the lost videos are final renders that started too late, ran too slowly or never started, a failure of how agents plan their hour. Agents build their own rendering pipelines and inspect their own frames, yet how much they look does not predict quality. The judges' ranking of the systems closely matches blind human preferences.
  </details>

- **[Metric Scale for Insta360 Structure-from-Motion from the Camera's Embedded IMU](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35554)**  `arXiv:2610.35554`  `cs.CV`  
  _Davide Cuttini, Diego Di Tommaso, Alessio Antoniacomi, Luca Simonetti_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Structure-from-motion (SfM) from the dual-fisheye video of a consumer 360-degree camera recovers geometry only up to a similarity transform; in the pipeline considered here, metric scale is obtained from smartphone ARKit or ARCore poses captured alongside the camera. This paper evaluates a self-contained alternative: the IMU embedded in the Insta360 X5 and X6, recorded at 1 kHz. The sensor is characterised (noise density, camera-IMU synchronisation, accelerometer scale factor, bias stability), the camera-IMU rotation and frame timing are estimated from the data, and the scale is recovered by a linear visual-inertial alignment over the SfM poses of a whole survey. The standard formulation, with gravity and accelerometer bias shared across the survey, overestimates the scale by 3.4% on average; estimating each contiguous segment independently and averaging removes the bias. On nine surveys with a co-mounted LiDAR scanner the inertial scale deviates from the LiDAR by 0.81% in mean absolute error, against 1.94% for the smartphone scale; on printed fiducial markers both agree within the 1% uncertainty of the target. On the reference trajectories of thirty sequences of a public benchmark, brought to scales of 1/40, 1 and 40, the estimator recovers the metric scale with a mean error of -0.07% and a standard deviation of 1.28%. On fifty production surveys where the smartphone is the only reference, the two scales agree to a standard deviation of 2.3%, the inertial scale being 0.3% lower on average; the difference is uncorrelated with run length, grows with the extent of the smartphone anchor, and cannot be attributed to either reference. An ablation over window construction and a comparison with monocular metric-depth networks complete the study.
  </details>

- **[Multi-Agent Relay Thinking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35219)**  `arXiv:2610.35219`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xu Liu, Jungang Qiao, Guikun Chen, Xiaojuan Qi, Wenguan Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing multi-agent collaboration (MAC) methods have primarily explored a group thinking collaboration topology, where agents independently generate solutions and aggregate their outputs. In this work, we investigate an alternative relay thinking topology, where reasoning ownership is dynamically transferred across agents, allowing subsequent agents to inherit and refine an evolving reasoning trajectory. We introduce Multi-Agent Confidence-guided Relay (MACR), a framework that enables dynamical relay collaboration among agents. The key challenge is determining when reasoning should be relayed and which agent should continue the process. method addresses this challenge through confidence-guided relay triggering and a bidding mechanism that selects the next agent based on its confidence. Experiments on four benchmarks show that method outperforms the strongest single-model strategies in accuracy, e.g., +3.3% on MathVista and +3.9% on MMStar, and remains on par with multi-agent strategies while consuming 6.0x less token overhead on HallusionBench and MMStar. Further analysis demonstrates that relay collaboration enables heterogeneous agents to progressively integrate complementary insights, combining heterogeneous capabilities across reasoning stages. Our source code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[MemVLN: Streaming Episodic Memory for Vision-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35066)**  `arXiv:2610.35066`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yan Wang, Jiangyong Huang, Siyuan Huang, Baoxiong Jia_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language navigation (VLN) requires an embodied agent to follow language instructions through 3D environments. Maintaining persistent episodic memory remains a challenge for VLM-based streaming agents in long-horizon navigation. We present MEMVLN, a streaming episodic memory framework that maintains a bounded bank of compressed perceptual features annotated with timesteps and action-derived relative poses, and produces compact memory tokens via cross-attention. We pair the proposed memory with a frozen pretrained geometry encoder. To learn the memory module, we design a masking-based trajectory summarization task with multi-granularity sub-trajectory decomposition, encouraging route reconstruction exclusively from episodic memory. Under navigation training restricted to the official R2R/RxR trajectories, MEMVLN achieves state-of-the-art performance on most reported metrics on standard VLN-CE benchmarks. Long-horizon analyses further characterize when episodic memory is useful. For physical evaluation, we collect 30 instruction-conditioned episodes in one physical scene, use 24 as demonstrations for post-training, and hold out six for testing. On the held-out episodes, MEMVLN improves success over the VLM baseline from 17% to 40%, and post-training further raises it to 47%.
  </details>

- **[CropReward: A Human-Preference Benchmark and Reward Model for Aesthetic Image Cropping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34221)**  `arXiv:2610.34221`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xiangfei Sheng, Xiaofeng Pan, Leida Li, Qian Zhang, Biao Wang, Johan Wagemans, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Aesthetic image cropping (AIC) aims to improve image composition and aesthetic quality by removing extraneous or distracting content. Over the past decade, AIC methods have evolved from specialized vision models to multimodal large language models(MLLMs). Reliable evaluation that reflects human preferences is essential for benchmarking AIC methods and guiding model optimization. However, the current AIC evaluation relies largely on geometry-based metrics such as intersection over union (IoU), as well as image aesthetic assessment(IAA) models. Although inconsistencies with human preferences have long been observed, the robustness and reliability of these evaluation criteria remain underexplored. To bridge this gap, we first construct CropBench, a large-scale benchmark comprising 106,995 human preference pairs for 1,019 images, with each candidate set containing the original image and candidate crops produced by 14 AIC methods. A systematic study on CropBench reveals that the existing criteria, ranging from IoU to off-the-shelf IAA assessment models, only exhibit limited alignment with human preferences. Building upon CropBench, we further propose CropReward, an MLLM-based reward model that evaluates a candidate crop by comparing with the original uncropped image and outputs a score indicating explicit aesthetic improvement or degradation relative to the original. In addition, we employ CropReward to construct CropReward-300k, the largest AIC dataset to date, and further develop CropAlign, a preference-aligned AIC model trained solely with CropReward supervision, without human-annotated crop boxes. Experimental results demonstrate that CropReward aligns with human preferences significantly better than AIC existing criteria. CropAlign also surpasses the state-of-the-art AIC methods, demonstrating the advantage of CropReward in guiding AIC model optimization.
  </details>

- **[WMEval: Evaluating Interactive World Models Grounded on Physical Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33894)**  `arXiv:2610.33894`  `cs.CV`  
  _Lanxiang Hu, Yixin Huang, Abhilash Shankarampeta, Zilin Dai, Yuyuan Wu, Kun Qian, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Interactive world models hold immense potential for high-stakes applications in robotics, game engines, and immersive digital twins, yet existing benchmarks primarily focus on perceptual similarity rather than the physical-causal coherence essential for real-world utility. We introduce WMEval, a comprehensive benchmark that evaluates interactive world models against Unity-based physical simulations. By leveraging Unity as a high-fidelity ground-truth oracle, we shift evaluation beyond perceptual visual assessment toward the objective state-based verification required for downstream applications. Spanning 9 diverse scenes, WMEval assesses action controllability, scene and character consistency, prompt adherence, kinematics, fluid mechanics, physical causality, and lighting. By executing matched action sequences in both the simulator and the world model, WMEval enables evaluation at both the perceptual level and the physical-causal level, using simulator-derived states as reference. Experiments on state-of-the-art models reveal significant room for improvement in dimensions that remain hidden by standard perceptual metrics but are vital for real-world deployments. Our code is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Can One Hear Geometry? Gauge-Invariant Spectral Descriptors for Text-to-B-rep Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33484)**  `arXiv:2610.33484`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hongzhu Yi, Hao Liu, Tao Yu, Tiankun Yang, Yujia Yang, Zhenghao Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-3D solid retrieval faces a fundamental representation gap: while natural language describes intrinsic shapes, Boundary Representation (B-rep) entangles precise geometry with arbitrary variations in pose, scale, and boundary encoding conventions. Existing methods either discretize B-reps at the cost of analytic precision, or learn features from topological graphs without explicit invariance guarantees. We propose a text-to-B-rep retrieval framework based on geometric equivalence classes and spectral invariants, mathematically eliminating representation ambiguities prior to cross-modal alignment. Formalizing translation, rotation, reflection, uniform scaling, and contour re-indexing via group actions, we exploit the planar-profile-and-extrusion structure of engineering solids to construct a unified spectral descriptor across cross-sectional regions, contour boundaries, and extrusion depths. On public benchmarks, our method achieves state-of-the-art recall performance across all evaluation metrics.
  </details>

- **[VisCurate: Evaluating Behavioral Equivalence of Visual Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30405)**  `arXiv:2610.30405`  `cs.CV`  
  _Md Farhan Ishraq, Md Farhan Ishmam, Reaz Hassan Joarder, Md Hasanul Kabir_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern vision-based agents have shifted toward skill-based problem-solving, where a skill is an executable image-to-image operation, e.g., cropping, thresholding, or edge detection. As these skill libraries grow, they accumulate duplicated, mislabeled, and defective skills. The signals normally used to curate them (a skill's name, description, or source code) say little about how the skill visually changes the image. We present VisCurate, a framework and benchmark that curates visual skill libraries from observed execution rather than from text. VisCurate runs each candidate skill pair on a shared probe battery and assigns one of six behavioral relations, each tied to a library edit that is safe to make. The benchmark injects seven defect types into a clean library at controlled rates, producing 300 reproducible instances with ground-truth relations and repairs. While text baselines accept up to five of six false merges on hard negatives, our verifier accepts none and recovers 51.2% of the pairs that are truly mergeable. On two real, uncurated libraries, VisCurate finds that 92% of the skills a package shares with its downstream copy are redundant, while execution confirms only 10 of 17 published equivalence claims. The curation problem also remains open, as the strongest of the nine automated curators recovers only about half of the repairs. We emphasize that visual skill libraries should be curated by behavior, not by description.
  </details>

- **[Look Beyond the Question: Preserve-Focus-Fuse for Whole-Slide Image Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29431)**  `arXiv:2610.29431`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xiangyu Li, Jincheng Li, Tianle Du, Yanheng Li, Haoxuan Che, Yuxin Sun, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A question about a whole-slide image rarely names every morphological finding needed to answer it. Question-guided patch selection can therefore overlook supporting morphology, while slide-level compression can dilute focal evidence. Inspired by how pathologists combine broad slide review, question-directed examination, and integration of their findings, we introduce BEYONDPATH, a Preserve-Focus-Fuse framework for whole-slide image question answering. Preserve learns a question-agnostic Morphology Bank through answer supervision across multiple questions about each training slide. Focus retrieves questionspecific evidence from all foreground patches, including regions outside the Bank. Fuse integrates the preserved morphology at the retrieved evidence positions through Morphology-on-Visual-Evidence Fusion, producing a fixed 96-token visual prefix. On WSI-Bench, BEYONDPATH achieves an Avg-9 of 0.769 and leads all eight reported report-generation metrics using the same checkpoint. It also achieves 0.639 zero-shot overall accuracy on SlideBench-BCNB. Controlled ablations show complementary benefits from morphology preservation and question-directed retrieval, and support cross-question supervision and fusion at the evidence positions. These results support looking beyond the question's explicit cues: preserving broader slide morphology while retaining the ability to seek evidence for each question.
  </details>

- **[OmniSeek: Native Tool Integration for Multi-turn Audio-Visual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02181)**  `arXiv:2610.02181`  `cs.CV`  
  _Haibo Wang, Jiteng Mu, Jialu Li, Jingru Yi, Yuanjun Xiong, Jianming Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present OmniSeek, an agentic framework that transforms an Omni Large Language Model (Omni-LLM) into an active, multi-turn reasoning agent with native tool use. Rather than passively processing an entire audio-visual sequence in a single forward pass, OmniSeek makes evidence acquisition part of the reasoning process: it dynamically decides whether to look or listen, and over which temporal window, to retrieve sparse but critical evidence across different modalities within long contexts. Through an iterative multi-turn protocol, the retrieved raw audio or visual segments are appended back into the context to support subsequent reasoning. To cold-start this capability, we build a data engine that synthesizes OmniTraj-170K, a corpus of multi-hop Chain-of-Thought trajectories with interleaved audio and visual evidence. We first supervise the model on these trajectories to instill multi-turn tool-use behavior, and then further optimize the policy via a two-stage reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards. Moreover, we introduce an Audio-Visual Necessity objective that explicitly rewards successful trajectories whose reasoning depends on both modalities, discouraging single-modality shortcuts. Extensive experiments across a wide range of benchmarks demonstrate that OmniSeek learns adaptive cross-modal evidence seeking and consistently improves audio-visual reasoning performance.
  </details>

- **[CLeaR: A Unified Framework for Resolving the Leakage-Degradation Dilemma in Style Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38136)**  `arXiv:2609.38136`  `cs.CV`  
  _Teng Zhou, Yunhao Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Style transfer aims to render target content in the style of a reference image, but existing methods often suffer from content leakage, where objects, layouts, or semantics from the style reference appear in the generated output. Although prior data-driven and training-free methods can reduce leakage, they often face a leakage-degradation dilemma: stronger content suppression may weaken style fidelity, while richer style preservation may reintroduce unwanted reference content. We identify this dilemma across the full style-transfer pipeline, including feature separation, feature-space grounding, and diffusion generation. To address these issues, we propose CLeaR, a training-free framework for content-leakage-resistant style transfer. CLeaR first uses Orthogonal Subspace Projection to define content-reduced style targets in each vision foundation model (VFM) feature space. It then performs Ensemble Inversion, which optimizes a shared pixel-space style anchor satisfying style constraints across multiple VFMs. Finally, Energy-Guided Calibration maintains style alignment during diffusion sampling by steering the denoising trajectory toward the ensemble-defined style manifold. We further provide a theoretical analysis showing that the style-anchor estimation error decreases with the number of VFMs. Experiments on StyleBench demonstrate that CLeaR improves style alignment, reduces content leakage, and achieves better LLM-as-Judge evaluation compared with existing methods. The code is available at \href{this https URL}{this https URL}.
  </details>

- **[OctWorld: Long-Range World-Consistent Video Generation with Octree-Based 3D Mapping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03919)**  `arXiv:2609.03919`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zelong Lv, Sicheng Xu, Jianfeng Xiang, Ruicheng Wang, Yue Dong, Yu Deng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present OctWorld, a video diffusion framework with persistent 3D memory for generating explorable, world-consistent, and high-fidelity visual scenes. Given a single image, OctWorld performs stable autoregressive world generation along user-specified camera trajectories. We focus on long-range generation, characterized by extended camera paths and wide viewpoint coverage, where preserving spatial consistency is particularly challenging when previously generated regions are revisited. To address this problem, we introduce OctMap, an extensible and spatially adaptive 3D memory that progressively fuses generated visual observations and their corresponding depth maps into a global representation. OctMap employs TSDF fusion within a dynamic sparse octree whose spatial resolution adapts to image evidence. This design preserves geometric and appearance details across diverse scene scales while maintaining low memory overhead. Experiments demonstrate that OctWorld generates long-range, spatially consistent videos and outperforms prior methods on both existing benchmarks and challenging long-range generation settings. OctMap also provides clear advantages over point-based caches and fixed-resolution TSDF volumes. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Amortized Set Prediction for Inverse IFS Reconstruction from Density Maps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24175)**  `arXiv:2608.24175`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yutaka Yamaguti_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Iterated Function Systems (IFS) generate self-similar fractals from a few contractive affine maps. The forward map from parameters to images is inexpensive and well understood, whereas the inverse problem is difficult and is typically solved by per-image optimization. We replace this loop with a single forward pass of a learned estimator that predicts the affine-map set directly from a visit-frequency density map, thereby amortizing the inverse problem. The design follows two constraints. First, density maps do not uniquely identify IFS parameters, so we evaluate by reconstruction rather than parameter recovery and handle unordered map sets by Hungarian matching. Training nevertheless matches the ground-truth parameters: reconstruction-only training gives a better one-shot output, but refinement from its predictions converges to poorer minima, whereas refinement from parameter-matched predictions keeps improving. Second, the fully known forward model lets us generate exact synthetic training pairs and supports image-only test-time refinement. On in-distribution tests, amortized initialization plus a few refinement steps gives a better quality-speed trade-off than equal-budget random-start optimization, and over 1000 steps it reaches high-quality reconstructions more often. It is robust to the sampling settings of the observation, and on an external benchmark outside its training prior it matches the mean accuracy of a recent hour-long optimizer within a minute. On real images (MNIST and Fashion-MNIST), it improves density metrics on average over a published per-image optimizer while being roughly 2 to 500 times faster per image.
  </details>

- **[LEVIRDet: A Million-Scale 159-Category Dataset and Foundation Model for Universal Remote Sensing Object Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25312)**  `arXiv:2606.25312`  `cs.CV`  
  _Qinzhe Yang, Dongyu Wang, Haohan Niu, Jia Xu, Zhenwei Shi, Zhengxia Zou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Remote sensing object detection has advanced rapidly with the development of large-scale benchmarks and modern detection architectures. However, existing datasets and detectors remain fragmented. Most benchmarks focus on limited categories, fixed spatial resolutions, or a single sensor, while detectors still struggle to work across different sensors and categorical systems. In this paper, we introduce LEVIRDet-159, the largest and most comprehensive remote sensing object detection dataset to date, with 159 categories, 2.56 million bounding boxes, and 700k fine-grained annotations under a multi-level taxonomy. In each key scale dimension, LEVIRDet-159 exceeds the corresponding largest existing remote sensing object detection dataset, containing approximately (7x) more images, (6x) more object instances, and (4x) more categories. Based on this dataset, we design LEVIRDetNet, a scale-hierarchy-aware detection foundation model for universal remote sensing object detection. LEVIRDetNet couples online visual Ground Sampling Distance (GSD) prediction, GSD-conditioned query modulation and allocation, and a hierarchy-aware detection head for mixed-granularity remote sensing supervision. Under stringent evaluation settings, LEVIRDetNet demonstrates strong cross-domain generalization. Even without target-domain training or fine-tuning, it achieves state-of-the-art detection performance on 9 external benchmarks, improving the strongest fully supervised competing methods by 5.02 mAP on average under each benchmark's primary metric. We hope this study will facilitate the development of strongly generalizable remote sensing object detection across diverse category systems, spatial resolutions, and sensor platforms. The full image tiles, annotations, source-license manifest, code, and trained models have been released in a versioned project repository atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ReGuLaR: Relation-Grounded Latent Reasoning for Large Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30587)**  `arXiv:2605.30587`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zihu Wang, Karthik Somayaji N.S, Hejia Geng, Peng Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning has significantly improved the reasoning ability of large vision-language models (LVLMs) by verbalizing intermediate reasoning steps in natural language. However, such discrete textual rationales are often insufficient for encoding continuous visual evidence. Recent work addresses this limitation by moving reasoning into continuous latent space. Despite promising progress, existing methods leave latent reasoning insufficiently connected to the compositional and relational structure of visual evidence. To address this gap, we introduce ReGuLaR, a relationgrounded latent reasoning framework that explicitly grounds latent states in this critical yet overlooked visual evidence. ReGuLaR uses a training-time ReGFormer to focus latent reasoning on question-relevant objects and interobject relations, while at inference time the model reasons and generates answers without invoking the ReGFormer. To support training ReGuLaR, we construct RGROUNDING-351K, a vision-language dataset annotated with key object bounding boxes and inter-object relations. Extensive experiments across diverse benchmarks show that ReGuLaR consistently outperforms existing approaches and achieves state-of-the-art performance.
  </details>

- **[LESSViT: Robust Hyperspectral Representation Learning under Spectral Configuration Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18541)**  `arXiv:2605.18541`  `cs.CV`  
  _Haozhe Si, Yuxuan Wan, Yuqing Wang, Minh N. Do, Han Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modeling hyperspectral imagery (HSI) across different sensors presents a fundamental challenge due to variations in wavelength coverage, band sampling, and channel dimensionality. As a result, models trained under a fixed spectral configuration often fail to generalize to other sensors. Existing Vision Transformer (ViT) approaches either rely on implicit spectral modeling with fixed channel assumptions or adopt explicit spatial-spectral attention with prohibitive computational cost, leading to a fundamental trade-off between efficiency and expressiveness. In this work, we introduce Low-rank Efficient Spatial-Spectral ViT (LESSViT), a sensor-flexible architecture for cross-spectral generalization. LESSViT is built on LESS Attention, a structured low-rank factorization that models joint spatial-spectral interactions through separable spatial and spectral components, reducing the complexity of full spatial-spectral attention from $\mathcal{O}(N^2 C^2)$ to $\mathcal{O}(rNC)$, where $N$ is the number of spatial tokens, $C$ is the number of spectral channels, and $r$ is the rank of the low-rank approximation. We further incorporate channel-agnostic patch embedding and wavelength-aware positional encoding to support flexible spectral inputs. To enable efficient and robust pretraining, we introduce a hyperspectral masked autoencoder (HyperMAE) with decoupled spatial-spectral masking and hierarchical channel sampling. We evaluate LESSViT under a cross-spectral generalization setting that simulates cross-sensor variability. Experiments on the SpectralEarth benchmark demonstrate that LESSViT improves robustness under spectral shifts while remaining competitive in-distribution, and explicit and efficient spatial-spectral modeling is essential for scalable and generalizable hyperspectral representation learning. The code is available through the project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[GTASA: Ground Truth Annotations for Spatiotemporal Analysis, Evaluation and Training of Video Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10385)**  `arXiv:2604.10385`  `cs.CV`  
  _Nicolae Cudlenco, Mihai Masala, Marius Leordeanu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Game engines hold what video models struggle to learn: a complete, explicit world state behind every frame. We turn one into a data instrument. GEST-Engine, our production-grade open-source system, deterministically executes Graphs of Events in Space and Time (GESTs), whether procedurally generated or derived from text, into videos of synchronized multi-actor scenarios, recording ground truth as it renders: 3D entity and camera state, pairwise spatial relations, event-to-frame mappings, instance segmentation, and long descriptions, at zero marginal annotation cost. With it we release GTASA, a 938-video sample of what the system can generate at arbitrary scale, carrying, to our knowledge, the densest spatial-relation coverage of any video dataset: a complete entity-pair relation graph at every frame, ~84x denser than the state of the art, frame-for-frame. We validate GTASA both qualitatively, through human evaluation of physical validity and semantic alignment where frontier neural generators, given the same prompts, largely fail, and quantitatively, with GTASA pretraining improving VideoLLaMA3 video captioning. Probing six frozen video encoders across 11 spatio-temporal tasks enabled by GTASA's exact 3D ground truth, a previously untestable inter-entity relational probe of frozen video features, reveals that who-is-near-whom barely rises above chance for all of them. We release the engine, the corpus, and the benchmark, making this gap a measurable, trainable target.
  </details>

- **[A Closer Look at Evaluation of Text-to-Video Models Under Camera Motion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.07441)**  `arXiv:2510.07441`  `cs.CV`  
  _Nithin C. Babu, Aniruddha Mahapatra, Harsh Rangwani, Rajiv Soundararajan, Kuldeep Kulkarni_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing text-to-video (T2V) evaluation benchmarks, such as VBench and EvalCrafter, predominantly contain either subject-centric prompts or static camera scenes, overlooking the importance of camera motion, essential for producing cinematic shots with realistic scenes. Due to this, the metrics developed in these works often produce incorrect results in the presence of camera motion. To fill this gap, we introduce DynamicEval, a benchmark comprising of systematically curated prompts that emphasize dynamic camera motion, paired with 45k human annotations on video pairs. DynamicEval evaluates two key dimensions of video quality: the consistency of the foreground subjects and the consistency of the background scene. To address the shortcomings of existing metrics under camera motion, we introduce two novel metrics: 1) Tracker-FG, which evaluates subject consistency by assessing the keypoint tracks of the subject. 2) Debiased Motion Smoothness, for background consistency, devoid from issues of occlusions/disocclusions arising from camera and foreground subject movements. Extensive experiments on the large-scale DynamicEval benchmark demonstrate that our proposed metrics achieve stronger correlations with human preferences at both the video level, and the model level with an increase of 0.32 in average Pearson Linear Correlation.
  </details>

- **[Single-Step Bidirectional Unpaired Image Translation Using Implicit Bridge Consistency Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.15056)**  `arXiv:2503.15056`  `cs.CV`  
  _Suhyeon Lee, Kwanyoung Kim, Jong Chul Ye_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unpaired image-to-image translation has seen significant progress since the introduction of CycleGAN. However, methods based on diffusion models or Schrödinger bridges have yet to be widely adopted in real-world applications due to their iterative sampling nature. To address this challenge, we propose Implicit Bridge Consistency Distillation (IBCD), which enables single-step bidirectional unpaired translation without using adversarial loss. IBCD reformulates consistency distillation by using a diffusion implicit bridge model that connects PF-ODE trajectories between distributions, parametrized on a signed time axis whose sign selects the target domain, so that one network translates in both directions in a single step. Additionally, we introduce two key improvements: 1) distribution matching for consistency distillation and 2) adaptive weighting method based on distillation difficulty. Experimental results demonstrate that IBCD achieves state-of-the-art performance on benchmark datasets in a single generation step, and that it extends to brain MRI contrast translation.
  </details>

- **[LLM Hallucination Detection via Low-rank Multilinear Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36191)**  `arXiv:2610.36191`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Devansh Sharma, Faizul Rakib Sayem, Shahana Ibrahim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) frequently generate fluent yet factually incorrect responses, raising concerns about their reliability. Recent hallucination detectors increasingly exploit internal representations, but extracting informative signals from high-dimensional hidden states remains challenging. Existing approaches often rely on selected activations or pooling-based compression, potentially overlooking discriminative information distributed across Transformer layers, feature modes, and tokens. We propose LOTUS (Low-rank Order-statistic TUcker-based Subspace learning), a multilinear low-rank decomposition framework for hallucination detection that extracts compact representations from LLM hidden states while reducing dependence on labeled data. Our approach first summarizes variable-length token representations using complementary statistical descriptors that capture peak activations, activation distributions, and cross-layer changes. We then leverage low-rank multilinear decomposition to extract compact representations that capture the underlying structure of LLM hidden states, which are subsequently used to train a lightweight hallucination classifier. Experiments across multiple LLMs and question-answering benchmarks demonstrate improved hallucination detection performance over recent state-of-the-art baselines.
  </details>

- **[Denoising Hierarchical Representations: Joint Continuous Diffusion for Language Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08738)**  `arXiv:2610.08738`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Mathias Ollu, Nikos Komodakis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion Language Models (DLMs) hold the promise of order-agnostic, parallel text generation. Recently, continuous diffusion and flow matching models have seen substantial gains, driven by carefully crafted token representations and diffusion/flow spaces. In this work, we introduce Hierarchical Continuous Diffusion Language Models (H-CDLMs), a simple framework that further improves continuous DLMs with minimal compute and parameter overhead. Drawing on the discrete DLM and continuous image diffusion literature on joint diffusion, we diffuse multiple modalities in parallel. These modalities represent tokens at different semantic granularities: in our instantiation, the tokens themselves and coarser clusters obtained by clustering pretrained token embeddings. We propose a general setup that allows per-modality samplers and schedules to enhance the interplay between modalities. Applied to CoBit, this yields H-CoBit, which delivers large empirical gains across benchmarks. At dataset entropy, H-CoBit improves MAUVE and reaches a generative perplexity (GenPPL) of 49.4 on LM1B and 50.4 on OWT, improving on the baseline by 24.2 and 20.7 points and surpassing even discrete DLMs of comparable size. On GSM8K, it reaches 27.4% accuracy, outperforming prior continuous diffusion and flow-based models. We further apply H-CDLM to the flow matching model FLM, obtaining consistent gains with H-FLM and demonstrating that the framework generalizes across continuous generative paradigms. Our code will be made publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Parallel Universes for Temporal Evaluation and Knowledge Updating in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00184)**  `arXiv:2609.00184`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Jonathan Zheng, Zirui Shao, Alan Ritter, Wei Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) rely on static pretraining corpora, resulting in outdated knowledge over time. Existing approaches for evaluating knowledge edits either suffer from rapid contamination or rely on counterfactual edits that conflict with rigid existing knowledge. In this work, we propose a simulation-driven approach for evaluating and training LLMs on knowledge insertions. We introduce PARALLELEVENTS, a benchmark that simulates coherent trajectories of fictional yet realistic future events, enabling controlled evaluation without data contamination or conflicts with existing knowledge. We further propose SYNAPSE, a training framework that leverages model-generated data to integrate new knowledge into model parameters via instruction tuning. This synthetic pipeline enables scalable knowledge integration without costly human-curated data. SYNAPSE improves QA accuracy by 14.23 points over prior methods, indicating that simulation-based synthetic training yields robust, coherent knowledge insertion.
  </details>

- **[DocXEval: A Systematic Evaluation of Explainability in Multimodal Document Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36929)**  `arXiv:2610.36929`  `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Saifullah Saifullah, David Dembinsky, Stefan Agne, Andreas Dengel, Sheraz Ahmed_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal transformer models have driven unprecedented performance breakthroughs in modern document understanding. However, the inherent black-box nature of these models remains a major obstacle to their safe deployment in high-stakes sectors. Despite significant progress in explainability for vision and language tasks, explainable AI (XAI) research tailored to multimodal document understanding has received little attention. To address this gap, we present \ourframework{}, the first comprehensive explainability framework that adapts existing XAI methods and evaluation metrics to the multimodal (text, layout, and image) document understanding context, while introducing efficient multi-target adaptations of both methods and metrics for domain-specific tasks such as semantic entity recognition. Using \ourframework{}, we systematically evaluate 13 XAI methods across 3 document understanding tasks, 8 datasets, 4 models, and 17 evaluation metrics, and find that only a few methods perform consistently across all evaluation criteria, with Occlusion emerging as the most faithful and interpretable method overall. Through a dedicated meta-evaluation, we observe moderate to strong agreement among several faithfulness and complexity metrics while identifying certain ones that produce unreliable results. Finally, through multiple dataset-wide diagnostic analyses, we uncover key insights into model behavior, including the contribution of individual modalities across tasks and labels, spatial attribution patterns around target entities, and potential dataset biases. Overall, this work serves as both a benchmark and a practical guide for future XAI research in multimodal document understanding. To support reproducibility, we publicly release our code atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[DocSkill: Skill-Integrated Agentic Reasoning for Multilingual Multi-Hop Document Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36759)**  `arXiv:2610.36759`  `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Jing Huang, Zhiya Tan, Changtao Miao, Shutao Gong, Yang Wang, Joey Tianyi Zhou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multilingual multi-hop document question answering (MMHop-DocQA) requires locating and integrating evidence across complex layouts, document pages, and diverse languages. Existing approaches include single-pass processing and adaptive tool use, but learning to coordinate evidence acquisition, cross-page integration, and answer verification remains challenging. To address this challenge, we present DocSkill, an agentic framework with a learned skill-orchestration policy for adaptive evidence acquisition and reasoning. The policy selects, revisits, or skips skills according to the evolving evidence state. To construct supervision for skill orchestration, bounded Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) explores alternative skill trajectories offline. These trajectories provide demonstrations for supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and preference pairs for Direct Preference Optimization (DPO). We further introduce MMHop-DocBench, a multilingual multi-hop document QA dataset with training and test splits, containing 60K QA pairs across 10 languages and eight document domains. Its HopGen pipeline builds hierarchical page-element graphs to derive traceable cross-page evidence paths and generate grounded QA pairs with page- and element-level evidence annotations. Experiments show that DocSkill with Qwen3-VL-8B achieves 41.0 accuracy and 49.2 F1 on MMHop-DocBench, exceeding the strongest evaluated baseline by 5.5 and 6.3 points, respectively, and achieving competitive performance on MMLongBench-Doc and DocBench.
  </details>

- **[Streaming Communication in Multi-Agent Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05158)**  `arXiv:2606.05158`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Zhen Yang, Xiaogang Xu, Wen Wang, Cong Chen, Xander Xu, Ying-Cong Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent reasoning systems adopt a generate-then-transfer paradigm that forces end-to-end latency to scale linearly with pipeline depth. We introduce StreamMA, a multi-agent reasoning system that streams each reasoning step to downstream agents as soon as it is generated, pipelining adjacent agents and thus reducing latency. Surprisingly, this pipelining also improves effectiveness: because multi-step reasoning quality is non-uniform and early steps are more reliable than later ones, working with these reliable early steps instead of the full chain prevents error-prone late steps from misleading downstream agents. We formalize both advantages with the first closed-form joint analysis of stream, serial, and single protocols, deriving the effectiveness ordering, speedup upper bound, and cost ratio. Across eight reasoning benchmarks spanning mathematics, science, and code, three frontier LLMs (Claude Opus 4.6, GPT-5.4, and GLM-5.2), and three topologies (Chain, Tree, Graph), StreamMA outperforms both baselines (avg.+7.3pp, max +22.4pp on HMMT2026; Claude Opus 4.6-High). Beyond these contributions, we discover a step-level scaling law: increasing per-agent steps consistently improves both effectiveness and efficiency, a new scaling dimension orthogonal to and composable with agent-count scaling.
  </details>

- **[Specialization, Diversity, and Scaling in Child-Scale Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36679)**  `arXiv:2610.36679`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Steven Y. Feng, Michael C. Frank_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern language models are trained on far more language than children experience, but we know much less about learning from small, naturalistic datasets. We train small GPT-2 models on transcripts of speech with children from dense home recordings (BabyView) and on synthetic child-directed dialogue (TinyDialogues), varying the amount, source, and diversity of training data. Models trained on one child's language environment predict that environment substantially better than other children's environments. Much of this specialization is lexical and distributional: a simple 5-gram model reproduces the cross-child pattern (r=.91). Training on data from multiple children clearly improves prediction of pooled held-out language relative to training on a single child's input without a similarly clear improvement on grammatical benchmarks. Across data sizes, naturalistic and synthetic training inputs also produce different grammatical and lexical-semantic patterns, with important differences between grammatical phenomena. Together, these results show that small-data language learning depends not only on how much language a model sees, but on which environments it comes from and how well that input matches what the model is asked to predict.
  </details>

- **[MedJev: Evaluating Zero-Shot System-One Decision Making in Medicine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36006)**  `arXiv:2610.36006`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Simon A. Lee, Jiahang Sha, Moonseong Jeong, Yunbei Pan, Jeffrey N. Chiang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  System One decision models such as Jev answer structured questions in a single forward pass without generating text, offering fast and computationally efficient clinical decision support. We evaluate MedJev, an open System One model that runs locally on a laptop, across ten medical benchmarks spanning medical knowledge, diagnosis, and patient level prediction using MIMIC IV and MIMIC IV ED. We compare MedJev against zero shot LLM baselines, published GPT results, and supervised models. MedJev achieves accuracies of 72.5 percent on MedQA, 75.4 percent on USMLE, and 80.1 percent on NEJM, approaching published GPT 4 performance while maintaining well calibrated confidence estimates (ECE 0.028 to 0.063). On patient level tasks, MedJev achieves an AUROC of 0.926 for In Hospital Mortality, exceeding XGBoost (0.902), but underperforms supervised models on operational outcomes such as ED Triage Admission (0.745 vs. 0.809). Patient level predictions also exhibit substantial miscalibration (In Hospital Mortality ECE 0.286). We introduce a lightweight, two parameter recalibration method that adapts predictions to local patient populations using 50 to 200 labeled examples, reducing ECE to 0.007. Our findings establish open System One models as a promising zero shot baseline for medical AI, combining competitive predictive performance with low computational cost and efficient local calibration. We release our code and evaluation harness to support future research.
  </details>

- **[Counting Clues: A Lightweight Probabilistic Baseline Can Match an LLM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.12868)**  `arXiv:2512.12868`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Furong Jia, Yuan Pu, Finn Guo, Monica Agrawal_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) excel on multiple-choice clinical diagnosis benchmarks, yet it is unclear how much of this performance reflects underlying probabilistic reasoning. We study this through questions from MedQA that ask for the most likely diagnosis. We introduce Frequency-Based Probabilistic Ranker (FBPR), a lightweight method that scores options with a smoothed Naive Bayes over concept-diagnosis co-occurrence statistics from a large corpus. When co-occurrence statistics are sourced from Dolma, the pretraining corpus for OLMo, and RedPajama, an open reproduction of the LLaMA pretraining-data recipe, FBPR achieves comparable performance to the corresponding LLMs. Furthermore, direct LLM inference and FBPR exhibit complementary strengths, correctly answering largely different subsets of questions with overlap barely above chance. Combining them with a simple hybrid method yields a notable gain over either method alone. These findings show that explicit probabilistic baselines can account for a substantial portion of the benchmark performance on diagnosis questions, while serving as historically grounded reference points and complementary signals for hybridization.
  </details>

- **[CulturalRiddles: A Multicultural Benchmark of Riddles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29968)**  `arXiv:2610.29968`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _My Chiffon Nguyen, Tianyi Hu, Vinod Anbalagan, Henry Gagnier, Pouya Sadeghi, Farah Abdou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As LLMs expand their language coverage, evaluating how well they understand and represent diverse cultures becomes increasingly important. However, many benchmarks remain English-centric, with limited cultural coverage and a focus on factual knowledge over its application in real-world cultural contexts. We propose CulturalRiddles, a benchmark of original, culturally grounded riddles created entirely by humans through a participatory community effort, spanning 62 cultures (45 countries, 51 languages). These riddles are deeply rooted in their respective cultures and require abductive and compositional reasoning, alongside detailed factual and social knowledge, language skills, and an understanding of culturally specific associations. We benchmark 28 LLM configurations using both automatic and large-scale human evaluation. Even frontier models achieve at most 22% accuracy in some communities. We publicly release all artifacts alongside an open contribution process to expand the benchmark's linguistic and cultural coverage.
  </details>

- **[Learning an Anchored Prompt Space for Continual Adaptation of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32499)**  `arXiv:2609.32499`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Rongguang Ye, Zhan Zhuang, Yichen Wu, Ming Tang, Kede Ma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continually adapting large language models requires acquiring new knowledge while preserving previously learned capabilities. Although jointly adapting model parameters and task-specific soft prompts is promising, it has two key limitations: previously learned prompts may lose effectiveness as the model evolves, and relationships among prompts that could support cross-task transfer are not explicitly learned. We propose Learning an Anchored Prompt Space (LAPS), which preserves historical prompt effectiveness and learns relationships among task-specific soft prompts to facilitate positive transfer. Historical prompts are first aligned with the updated model through self-distillation. LAPS then constructs an anchored prompt space whose vertices correspond to learned task-specific soft prompts and whose intermediate geometry is shaped by learnable Bézier control prompts. Once this space is learned, LAPS identifies the best-performing prompt for each task on its validation set, allowing the selected prompt to draw on knowledge acquired from related tasks. Experiments on the TRACE benchmark across three Qwen3 model scales show that LAPS consistently outperforms existing distillation-based, prompt-based, and joint prompt-parameter adaptation methods, improving overall task performance while reducing forgetting.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Questions: Evaluating the Stated Knowledge of LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26937)**  `arXiv:2605.26937`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Luca Giordano, Simon Razniewski_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Parametric knowledge in large language models (LLMs) is a cornerstone of their success, yet remains poorly understood. Existing knowledge benchmarks typically rely on predefined questions (e.g., "What is the birth date of M.L. King?"), evaluating only knowledge that benchmark designers explicitly choose to query, a problematic availability bias. In this paper, we introduce open knowledge evaluation, a new paradigm for benchmarking the stated knowledge of LLMs. Instead of asking narrow questions, it evaluates models on the knowledge they choose to surface in response to open-ended elicitation prompts (e.g., "Tell me everything you know about M.L. King"). This shifts the focus from predefined answer retrieval toward characterizing the knowledge models naturally express. We instantiate this paradigm with BeQu (Beyond Questions), a benchmark of 10,000 entities paired with reference corpora for statement verification. Using BeQu, we evaluate a broad range of language models and analyze the effects of reasoning effort, model scale, prompt format, and knowledge domain. Data and leaderboard are available on this work's GitHub repository and at ANONYMIZED.
  </details>

- **[Latent Abstraction for Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.17866)**  `arXiv:2604.17866`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Ha Lan N.T, Minh-Anh Nguyen, Dung D. Le_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) has become a standard approach for enhancing large language models (LLMs) with external knowledge, mitigating hallucinations, and improving factuality. However, existing systems rely on generating natural language queries at each hop and maintaining a strict architectural separation between retriever and generator, preventing them from leveraging the full representational capacity of the LLM. We propose \textbf{LAnR} (Latent Abstraction for RAG), a unified framework in which a single LLM jointly performs encoding, retrieval, and generation entirely within its own latent space. Rather than generating textual queries, LAnR produces dense retrieval vectors from the hidden states of a designated \texttt{[PRED]} token and uses them to match against encoded document representations from the same model. Furthermore, LAnR adaptively decides when sufficient evidence has been retrieved using a lightweight MLP control head over those same hidden states, eliminating both the separate retriever and explicit token-level stopping reasoning. This design is motivated by our empirical observation that answer token entropy reliably signals retrieval sufficiency. Extensive experiments on six QA benchmarks spanning single-hop and multi-hop settings demonstrate that LAnR outperforms existing RAG methods, while achieving improved inference efficiency through reduced number of retrieval calls and tighter model integration.
  </details>

- **[HateSense: Hierarchical Sentiment Analysis Framework for Hate Speech Detection: Implementing Binary and Multiclass Classification Strategy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.05819)**  `arXiv:2411.05819`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Faria Naznin, Md Touhidur Rahman, Shahran Rahman Alve_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hate speech detectors must separate hateful posts from the far more common offensive but non-hateful ones, and two popular ideas for doing so are hierarchical (coarse-to-fine) classification and sentiment features. We revisit our earlier transformer-based framework, which combines both ideas, and test each component in a controlled study on two public benchmarks (Davidson et al., 24,783 tweets; HateXplain, 19,229 posts) with five random splits, validation-based tuning, and paired bootstrap tests. With linear models over word and character n-grams, the dominant-class-first hierarchy used in our framework lowered macro-F1 by 1.1-1.5 points relative to a flat classifier (p <= 0.007), and adding VADER sentiment features changed Davidson macro-F1 by at most 0.6 points, never significantly in the class-weighted settings. Class-weighted training was the only intervention with a large effect (+2.0 to +3.5 macro-F1 and +5.9 to +9.1 hate-class F1), and sentiment cannot separate the two abusive classes because hateful and offensive tweets are almost equally negative (mean VADER compound -0.35 vs. -0.29). Cross-dataset transfer cut macro-F1 to 43-53, and a reclaimed slur doubled the false hate rate of a logistic-regression detector, showing that in-domain scores overstate real-world reliability.
  </details>

- **[GEPA-Curator: Curated Learning for Self Improving Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37651)**  `arXiv:2610.37651`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zack Yu, Nicholas Lee, Minjae Lee, Lakshya Agarwal, Luca Manolache, Wonjun Kang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used in multi-turn agentic workloads that require sustained interaction with complex environments. Existing reflective prompt optimizers such as GEPA learn from failed rollouts, but agentic failures can also come from prompt weaknesses, harness limitations, stochastic execution, or noisy judges. We argue that such failures may not provide useful learning signal, and may even be harmful; and thus it is essential to curate which tasks are provided to the prompt optimizer for better performance. We introduce GEPA-Curator, a framework that augments the prompt optimizer to select which agent experiences should shape prompt optimization. In particular, GEPA-Curator consists of two components: task curation, which filters out tasks that are likely to provide noisy or unreliable learning signals, and prior success retrieval, which selectively retrieves past successful trajectories for a task and provides them as additional context to the prompt optimizer. We evaluate GEPA-Curator on GoBrowse, ALFWorld, AppWorld, and $\tau$-bench, using both Gemini 3.1 Pro and Claude Opus 5 as optimizer models. GEPA-Curator matches or exceeds the mean performance of both baselines, with gains of up to 20.7% over GEPA and 21.2% over ACE.
  </details>

- **[Multi-State Linear Attention with Generalized Second-Order Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37645)**  `arXiv:2610.37645`  `cs.CL`  
  _Hee Seung Hwang, Sanghyuk Chun, Xindi Wu, Olga Russakovsky, Zhiwei Deng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-attention plays a key role in long-sequence modeling, but its quadratic time complexity fundamentally limits the scaling of Large Language Models. Recently, Multi-State Linear Attention (MSLA) mechanisms have been proposed to make sequence modeling more efficient by splitting the context into segments and compressing each in its own memory state. MSLA aggregates the information extracted from each memory state using only the mean alignment between a query and each segment's keys, ignoring how the alignment is distributed within the segment. In order to model the relevance of a segment to a query more comprehensively and incorporate the variance of the alignment in the aggregation, we propose MERLIN (MomEnt Routing for multi-state LINear attention), a method that tracks the first and second moments of each segment's keys. Using the moments of keys, a query can recover both the mean and the variance of its alignment with each segment to perform a more content-aware aggregation of per-state readouts. Experiments on multi-state variants of Gated DeltaNet-1.3B and Mamba-2-780M show that MERLIN consistently outperforms existing aggregation methods on various long-context tasks. In particular, MERLIN outperforms baselines on RULER needle-in-a-haystack and long-context QA (+8.3% for Gated DeltaNet-1.3B and +7.0% for Mamba-2-780M) and diverse subtasks in LongBench (+13.5% and +12.0%). It even outperforms baselines that consume 4x more memory. MERLIN provides a simple and general routing mechanism for improving long-context capabilities of MSLA models.
  </details>

- **[Metacognitive Decoding: Efficient Inference for Diffusion Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37642)**  `arXiv:2610.37642`  `cs.CL`  
  _Mingyu Zhao, Shuhang Lin, Ligong Han, Xiaojing Yu, Zhenting Wang, Yifei Pang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion language models offer a promising path to efficient agents through parallel token generation. However, efficient interaction requires adapting both where computation is spent and how much text is generated. We introduce a metacognitive controller that guides diffusion generation according to the needs of the current response and the agent's interaction with the environment. It learns when to finalize tokens and how far generation should extend, reducing overall computation within responses. Our controller, a lightweight plug-in for the diffusion backbone, is trained on agent trajectories to balance correctness and generation cost. This approach connects the agent's generation process to the interaction the agent serves. Experiments across five benchmarks demonstrate that our approach reduces inference FLOPs by 20.6% on average compared with the most efficient baseline, while maintaining competitive task accuracy. Ablation studies further validate the effectiveness of our approach.
  </details>

- **[Decision Models as Judges for Hallucination Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37229)**  `arXiv:2610.37229`  `cs.CL`  
  _Haozheng Liu, Zhaoqian Xue, Zhongyin Wang, Junting Mu, Zihui Ma, Shuya Feng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are widely used as judges of model outputs (LLM-as-a-Judge), but they are costly and can hallucinate or be overconfident. Decision models are a potential alternative. They return a predefined verdict with a probability for each label instead of free-form text and are much cheaper and faster. We test this promise on hallucination detection. We build binary benchmarks for five hallucination types (9,852 items from six datasets) and compare five decision models with the generative judge gpt-6-sol. Performance depends strongly on the type: the best decision model matches gpt-6-sol in balanced accuracy on context inconsistency and comes within 3 percentage points on factual errors, but trails it by 4-8 points on logical and instruction inconsistency. Decision-model probabilities are overconfident, though less so than the GPT-6 models, and separate correct from incorrect decisions only moderately. We also find that a confidence cascade of decision models improves on the best single model by 1.9-3.3 points on three types and matches or exceeds gpt-6-sol at 1-3% of its cost. Overall, decision models are a practical low-cost judge for some hallucination types but need to be validated before use.
  </details>

- **[Hierarchical Low-Rank Reparameterization for Parameter-Efficient](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37068)**  `arXiv:2610.37068`  `cs.CL`  
  _Rong Huang, Yi Xiong, Yuan-Yuan Cheng, Xiao-Ming Fu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
