  Parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) methods adapt large models by introducing a small number of trainable parameters. However, we find that these parameters still contain redundancy and can be further compressed while maintaining strong task performance. To this end, we propose a novel hierarchical low-rank reparameterization method. Its core idea is to reorganize the trainable parameters of PEFT methods into an approximately square matrix and then express it via a low-rank decomposition, which can be applied recursively. This forms a multi-level structure with factors compressed level by level, drastically reducing the number of trainable parameters. In our experiments, we combine this reparameterization with LoRA, DoRA, MoSA, and RaSA. We evaluate commonsense reasoning on LLaMA-3.1-8B and Qwen2.5-7B, with additional GSM8K math reasoning experiments on LLaMA-3.1-8B. Under comparable trainable-parameter budgets, our method combined with LoRA or DoRA achieves better performance. Furthermore,when the parameter count of the original methods is compressed by approximately an order of magnitude, our method can still maintain competitive task performance and even surpass the corresponding baselines in average accuracy across the eight commonsense reasoning benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[The State and Fate of Multilingual, Contextual Evaluation in the NLP World](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36990)**  `arXiv:2610.36990`  `cs.CL`  
  _Manan Uppadhyay, Himanshu Beniwal, Prashant Kodali, Sunayana Sitaram_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multilingual evaluation benchmarks are the primary instrument for assessing whether large language models generalize beyond English, yet the adequacy of these benchmarks has received little systematic scrutiny. We present a data-driven audit of 51 recent multilingual benchmarks spanning 242 datasets and 219 languages, organized around three pillars: coverage, representativeness, and rigor. Our analysis reveals that coverage is wide but thin with 36% of evaluated languages appearing in only a single benchmark, entire regions (Oceania, the Americas, Central Asia) are near-zero, and a stark task equity gap leaves low-resource languages evaluated on only 1-3 task categories versus 14 for high-resource languages. Representativeness is structurally compromised: translation from English remains the dominant construction strategy where 56% of all dataset-language instances are translated introducing artifacts and English-centric framing, while culturally grounded content is concentrated in a handful of community-driven benchmarks with narrow language scope. The ecosystem thus forces a trade-off between breadth and validity. Rigor is undermined by benchmark contamination, including translated benchmark leakage and parallel corpus overlap that evade surface-form detection. We synthesize these findings into concrete recommendations for building evaluation frameworks that are natively constructed, culturally grounded, contamination-aware, and designed to serve the communities whose languages they claim to evaluate. The dashboard with all the metadata collected can be found atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Is Token Confidence Just Chain Length? A Signal-Processing Audit of Reasoning Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36709)**  `arXiv:2610.36709`  `cs.CL`  
  _Pragna Mallappa_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reasoning language models answer math problems by writing long chains of thought, and sampling many chains per problem and taking a majority vote raises accuracy at a large cost in generated text. A popular way to cut that cost scores each chain by the model's confidence in each token it writes, where a token is a word or part of a word. The method averages this confidence over a sliding window of 2048 tokens and keeps or stops chains based on the lowest average. We treat the confidence, recorded token by token, as a signal in the sense of audio or radio, and examine the method with standard signal-processing tools such as spectral analysis and statistical tests for detecting a sudden change. We sampled 16 chains for each of 500 problems from two math benchmarks with a small open reasoning model. The windowed-minimum score turns out to measure mostly how long a chain is. Ranking chains by length alone does as well as or better than the confidence score, and once length is accounted for the score carries no information about correctness. Signal processing explains why: the minimum of a longer stretch of a slowly drifting signal is lower by chance alone, and the confidence signal drifts slowly enough that this effect dominates. The spectrum does hold a small signal that length does not explain, since incorrect chains carry more rapid token-to-token fluctuation, but it is too weak to improve the vote. For stopping chains early, neither the sliding average nor a classical change detector beats the simpler choice of sampling fewer chains.
  </details>

- **[ETC-Bench: A Multilingual Benchmark for Evaluation-Side Transcript Canonicalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36527)**  `arXiv:2610.36527`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zhiheng Qian, Junhao Du, Jing Peng, Hanqi Li, Kunyang Peng, Feng Xue, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Written transcripts of the same speech can differ in ways that are not uniquely determined by the speech signal. We formulate evaluation-side transcript canonicalization (ETC), which maps multiple surface forms to a single canonical form when their differences are not determined by speech. To our knowledge, ETC-Bench is the first and most comprehensive multilingual benchmark for evaluation-side transcript canonicalization, covering 181 phenomenon types across 12 languages. Each instance is a minimal pair that shares a sentence context and differs only in a target span exhibiting one phenomenon. To evaluate both canonicalization success and semantic preservation, we propose Semantic-preserving Exact-match Normalization Accuracy (SENA), which combines exact agreement between independently canonicalized pair members with a pair-relative BERTScore constraint against information loss. We further introduce the Taxonomy-guided Agent Canonicalization Skill (TACS), which provides LLM agents with phenomenon descriptions and explicit canonicalization contracts. Experiments with five baseline configurations and three TACS-based agents show that the agents outperform the baseline systems on shared languages. ETC-Bench provides a systematic testbed for evaluation-side transcript canonicalization across languages.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking Task Takeover Across Agent Handoffs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36523)**  `arXiv:2610.36523`  `cs.CL`  
  _Wenyuan Li, Guang Li, Dongyuan Li, Keisuke Maeda, Takahiro Ogawa, Miki Haseyama_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As agents collaborate on longer tasks, responsibility may pass between models before the work is complete. The receiving model must build on prior decisions, observations, and actions while addressing the remaining requirements. Evaluating this transition requires connecting the information preserved at handoff to how the receiver continues the work, a relationship that task-success and memory-retention scores alone do not capture. We introduce the Handoff State Continuity Benchmark (HSC-Bench), comprising 1,039 tasks that evaluate two complementary aspects of task takeover: recovering relevant repository evidence and continuing from established conversational, tool, and file-work state. Comparisons across six receiving models show that structured handoff state improves repository evidence recovery for every model and continuation performance for most models. The strongest receivers differ between the two tracks, showing that evidence-recovery rankings alone do not characterize performance when continuing from inherited state. By combining these comparisons with checks of subsequent responses and actions, HSC-Bench evaluates both access to prior evidence and adherence to established commitments and completed work. This provides a basis for selecting receiving models and assessing handoff representations according to the work that must be carried forward.
  </details>

- **[Beyond IceBERT: Open-Data ModernBERT Encoders for Icelandic from 12M to 1B Parameters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36230)**  `arXiv:2610.36230`  `cs.CL`  
  _Hafsteinn Einarsson_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  IceBERT, the reference encoder for Icelandic, found that a cleaned web crawl is enough to train a strong model and that starting from a multilingual model can help on some tasks. We revisit both findings with the ModernBERT architecture. We train Icelandic encoders from 12M to 1B parameters on 9B tokens of open data, and rerun IceBERT's benchmarks and five newer tasks for 30 encoders under one protocol with confidence intervals. Our 1B model trained from scratch, to our knowledge the first monolingual Icelandic encoder of that size, is significantly better than IceBERT on all eleven tasks; warm-started from the English Ettin encoder of the same shape, it has the best score of all encoders on every task we rank. Because our model shapes match the English Ettin encoders and the multilingual mmBERT, we can also test the warm start under controlled conditions, at six model sizes and four token budgets. With our vocabulary transplanted, a warm start was never significantly worse than scratch training on the same Icelandic text, and from base size up it was not significantly worse on four tasks than scratch training on 4 times the text (8 times at base and 1B). As models grow, most of the transfer comes from the source model's transformer layers, so even an English source helps. Keeping the source vocabulary instead cost 5 F1 on question answering and nearly three times the compute.
  </details>

- **[Lipi-Ghor: A Large-Scale, Diarized, Multi-Domain Bengali Speech Corpus and a Scalable Pipeline for Building It](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36184)**  `arXiv:2610.36184`  `cs.CL`  
  _Sanjid Hasan, A H M Fuad, Risalat Labib, Bayazid Hasan Simon_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Bengali, spoken by over 230 million people, remains severely under-resourced in spoken language research. Existing open Bengali ASR corpora are confined to short, read or crowd-sourced speech and lack speaker diarization and long-form audio.We present Lipi-Ghor, a large-scale weakly-supervised Bengali speech corpus comprising 885 hours of audio sourced from 998 YouTube videos across 596 unique channels, spanning 17 diverse domains including audiobooks, drama, religious sermons, news, and regional dialects. Each recording is annotated in SSTT format (Speaker, Speech, Transcription, Timestamp), which combines automated diarization with Bengali YouTube caption tracks.To our knowledge, Lipi-Ghor is the first Bengali speech corpus to pair large-scale coverage with speaker-diarized, long-form audio annotations, both of which are absent from existing open Bengali resources. We also present the construction methodology as a replicable pipeline for any language with a sufficient captioned YouTube presence. Targeted channel curation across a handful of rich domains yields corpus scales that were previously out of reach for low-resource languages.We benchmark four widely-used ASR models under zero-shot and fine-tuned conditions. All four degrade consistently on emotive, dialectal, and long-form content. Because caption targets are noisy, we also release a human-annotated gold evaluation split, verified by 17 volunteer annotators over 30 hours of human-in-the-loop correction and stratified across all 17 domains.We release our original contributions (video-ID lists, diarization output, pipeline code, and the gold annotations) under CC-BY-4.0, and distribute caption-derived transcripts for research use only; no raw audio is redistributed.
  </details>

- **[Understanding Jev-Style System One Models: Does Structure Reduce Selection Bias?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35826)**  `arXiv:2610.35826`  `cs.CL`  
  _Mingyang Song, Mao Zheng, Zhiyuan Ma, Xinpeng Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Selection bias in large language models (LLMs) can persist despite instructions to ignore presentation order or prompts that elicit reasoning. Recent efforts to reproduce Jev-style System One behavior have produced a growing family of models that predict probabilities directly over supplied candidates. Yet systematic comparisons of selection bias across Jev versions, Jev reproductions, and generative LLMs remain limited in multi-option tasks. This leaves a central question: does Jev-style decision structure reduce selection bias? We address this question with a simple test that shuffles options while keeping question and candidate content fixed. Across two Jev versions, twelve Jev reproductions, and six generative LLMs, we trace individual answers through eight presentations and compare them with fixed-order repetition. On our primary collection of 1,700 questions from two benchmarks, Jev's accuracy varies by less than two percentage points, yet its cumulative failure set expands by 27.3--28.5\% relative to the original order, with first failures continuing into the final presentation. Reordering loses substantially more initial successes than fixed-order repetition, a gap that also appears in highly repeatable Jev reproductions. Nor does Jev show a general advantage over the LLM baselines in preserving correct choices. Aggregation offers limited protection: voting yields modest gains for Jev, while even unanimous answers are frequently wrong. These results expose a gap between repeatable decisions and decisions that remain correct across equivalent presentations, showing why stable accuracy alone cannot establish resistance to selection bias.
  </details>

- **[Do Language Models Understand Igbo Culture? Beyond Multiple Choice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35168)**  `arXiv:2610.35168`  `cs.CL`  
  _Blessing Ngozi Uzor, Valdini Douglace Lemofouet, Paula Chikaodinaka Anyanwu, Mmesoma Peace Orji, Danielle Blanche Kapsa, Tassallah Abdullahi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The value of language models for any community depends on whether a model actually understands the culture of the people it intends to serve. We use the Igbo language as a case study. Existing benchmarks test translated Western ideas, lacking depth. To address this, we introduce CAB-Igbo, a benchmark developed from indigenous cultural sources and verified by native speakers across six domains. We evaluated diverse models under varying conditions, incorporating regional personas. Performance was assessed using multiple-choice accuracy and human evaluations across Epistemic Fidelity, Representational Richness, Linguistic Quality, and Pragmatic Proficiency. Results reveal a stark dissociation between language fluency and cultural knowledge. Through statistical testing, we show that for most models, the severe lack of Epistemic Fidelity and Representational Richness is already present in English and does not significantly worsen when switching to Igbo. Instead, native language prompting mainly degrades Linguistic Quality and Pragmatic Proficiency. Gemini 2.0 Flash is the only model whose cultural knowledge scores improve under Igbo prompting. We find that current models struggle to demonstrate the knowledge needed for true alignment with Igbo, and multiple-choice formats allow pattern-matching to appear as understanding. Furthermore, automated judges agree with humans on linguistic quality but fail on epistemic fidelity. Ultimately, cultural evaluation must be built from community knowledge, establishing human evaluation as the gold standard to prevent misrepresenting low-resource communities globally.
  </details>

- **[Ask Before You Answer: A Question-Driven Evidence Reasoning Architecture (QERA) for In-Context Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35083)**  `arXiv:2610.35083`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jinhu Qi, Minda Hu, Wentao Zhang, Zixing Song, Irwin King_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI assistants are increasingly asked to reason over a user's own web traces: group chats, community threads, activity logs and revision histories. On CL-bench Life, a benchmark of such real-life context learning, frontier LLMs solve fewer than one task in five. We find that the request is not the specification: over 80% of the benchmark's rubrics share less than 60% of their content words with the final request, as for a rule set two turns earlier or every record that meets the user's criterion, and LLMs answering directly pass only 67% of these implicit rubrics against 81% of the others. We propose QERA, a Question-driven Evidence Reasoning Architecture that asks before it answers. QERA first turns the request into a requirement checklist and typed information needs, filtered by code for faithfulness to the user's words; it then answers from the whole conversation, asks the same questions again with quotes verified by code, and applies a guarded local repair. It needs no training or rubrics and makes about four LLM calls per task. On all 405 tasks with four frontier LLMs, QERA attains the highest solving rate on every LLM (21.0% on average, against 18.2% for the strongest of three inference-time baselines and 14.6% for direct answering) and leads every baseline significantly when pooled across LLMs, while using fewer tokens than PSCI and 4-5% of the tokens of Ctx2Skill. Ablations on GPT-6-Luna show that the gain is settled before the answer, by a fixed answer protocol (+3.8 rubric points) and the request-specific questions (+1.6), and that it is concentrated on implicit rubrics, with checking afterwards refining mainly numeric requirements.
  </details>

- **[BanglaBBQ: A Bias Benchmark for Question Answering in the Bangladeshi Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24764)**  `arXiv:2610.24764`  `cs.CL`  
  _Sumit Anthony Gomes, Yasin Rahman, Taeeba Tasnia, Faiza Nooren Suhita_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper presents BanglaBBQ, a question-answering bias benchmark designed to capture stereotypes grounded in the Bangladeshi context, in both Bangla and English. Following KoBBQ and PakBBQ, BanglaBBQ adapts the original Bias Benchmark for Question Answering (BBQ) by classifying its templates into three groups: directly translated (DT), modified to align target groups or contexts with Bangladesh (TM), and removed when deemed unsuitable for the Bangladeshi context (SR). This process retains 140 templates across six categories, comprising 76 DT and 64 TM templates. Five newly authored (NA) categories, based on stereotypes common in Bangladeshi society, are then added: marital status, age and political perception, regional origin, educational background, and mental-health status. Native Bangladeshi annotators judged all 466 newly authored templates, and 372 were retained because a strict majority of English annotators identified the intended target and rated the attitude as common enough. Each newly authored template is linked to a documented source and a verbatim quote from it. Each evaluated template also has a counter-stereotypical version of its disambiguated context. The dataset was evaluated using two open-weight models (Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-8B) and two closed models (GPT-4.1-mini and Gemini 2.5 Flash). All four lean towards the stereotyped person when the context is ambiguous, in both languages, and the effect of language differs by model, so English results do not predict behaviour in Bangla. On the adapted BBQ templates, both Qwen3 models are also less accurate when the context contradicts a stereotype than when it confirms it. On the newly authored templates, the closed models follow counter-stereotypical evidence almost perfectly, and all four models are more biased on the templates that annotators validated than on those they rejected.
  </details>

- **[Pair Difficulty Matters: Rethinking Pairwise LLM-as-a-Judge Evaluation and Consistency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37577)**  `arXiv:2609.37577`  `cs.CL`  
  _Bruno Brocai, Maria Becker_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Model judges are widely used to rank texts and text-generating systems through pairwise comparison, and their reliability is typically assessed via three proxies: position bias, transitivity, and pairwise agreement (self- or human-labeled). Because these proxies drive judge selection and benchmarking, a substantial literature reporting that judges perform poorly on them risks steering practitioners away from otherwise capable evaluators. We argue this assessment is misleading. Under the Bradley--Terry geometry underlying pairwise aggregation, each proxy is dominated by close-rank-gap pairs, where inconsistency is expected and errors cost little aggregate ranking accuracy; far-gap pairs determine the ranking accuracy but barely move the proxies. We formalize this argument and validate it in a controlled simulation and on two human-rated corpora: the proxies correlate only weakly with ranking accuracy against gold, and their predictive component concentrates in the far-gap regime. Judges should therefore be assessed on rank-gap-conditional metrics, ideally against human rankings. Code atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Kernel Token Contradiction: a Fast and Principled Approach for LLM Claim Uncertainty Quantification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22506)**  `arXiv:2608.22506`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jérémie Dentan, Alexi Canesse, Mahammed El Sharkawy, Sonia Vanier_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Claim-level Uncertainty Quantification (UQ) aims to mitigate the lack of reliability of Large Language Models (LLMs) by evaluating the factuality of each claim in their outputs. We introduce Kernel Token Contradiction (KTC), a lightweight approach to compute claim-level UQ under realistic white-box conditions. KTC represents the candidate tokens involved in LLM generation as a positive semi-definite kernel that integrates both the LLM's conditional distribution and a token contradiction score. We then use the Von Neumann entropy to quantify the uncertainty of this kernel. To estimate token contradiction, we develop a new approach based on frequency statistics from the Wikipedia corpus. Although CPU-only, our approach achieves over an 8.2x speedup compared to state-of-the-art GPU-accelerated methods based on cross-encoders, and over a 65x speedup compared to CPU-only methods with comparable performance. Our evaluation spans two benchmarks across four European languages and 16 different models. KTC not only matches the average performance of existing methods but also outperforms them in high-precision regimes. This combination of computational efficiency and accuracy makes real-time monitoring of LLM outputs practical in production.
  </details>

- **[Pruning via Causal Attribution Preserves Reasoning Performance in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.19350)**  `arXiv:2606.19350`  `cs.CL`  
  _Amogh Sheth, Andrew Lin, Biruk Assefa, Yi Wen Huang, Yuhao Ge_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) excel at multi-step reasoning but incur substantial inference cost. We introduce Causal Attribution Pruning (CAP), a training-free method that identifies critical attention heads by measuring their causal impact on reasoning tasks and uses these head-level scores to guide fine-grained weight pruning. For each attention head, CAP estimates the expected performance degradation when the head is masked during forward passes on a small calibration set of reasoning problems. These causal scores are then converted into weight-level importance values for the corresponding projection matrices. Unlike magnitude-only or activation-based criteria, CAP's interventional measurement directly captures each head's functional contribution, yielding relative accuracy gains of up to 61% over Wanda on ARC-Challenge at 20% sparsity. We evaluate CAP on GSM8K, StrategyQA, and ARC-Challenge using Llama-3-8B-Instruct and Mistral-7B-Instruct at 10%, 20%, and 50% sparsity. At moderate sparsity (10-20%), CAP improves over Wanda in most model-benchmark configurations. with especially large gains on ARC-Challenge for Llama-3. Our results suggest that attention-head-level causal attribution can better preserve reasoning performance on downstream benchmarks than correlational pruning criteria at equivalent sparsity, while remaining limited by coarse MLP attribution at 50% sparsity.
  </details>

- **[Agent Planning Benchmark: A Diagnostic Framework for Planning Capabilities in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.04874)**  `arXiv:2606.04874`  `cs.CL`  
  _Haoyu Sun, Wenxuan Wang, Mingyang Song, Jujie He, Weinan Zhang, Yang Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Planning is central to LLM agents: before acting, an agent must decompose goals, select tools, reason over constraints, and decide when a task is infeasible. Yet existing agent evaluations often report only end-to-end success, making it difficult to determine whether failures stem from planning or execution. We introduce Agent Planning Benchmark (APB), a planning-specific diagnostic benchmark with 4,209 multimodal cases across 22 domains and five settings, covering holistic planning, feedback-conditioned step-wise planning, and robustness under extraneous tools, broken tools, and unsolvable tasks. Across 12 MLLMs, APB reveals systematic weaknesses in long-horizon planning, tool-noise robustness, calibrated refusal, and inference-time refinement. We further validate APB on 200 ToolSandbox tasks and 200 $\tau^2$-bench tasks, where APB-guided refinement consistently improves plan correctness, plan grade, and downstream execution metrics across three representative models. APB thus serves as an upstream diagnostic complement to execution benchmarks. The APB benchmark and code are available in \href{this https URL}{this URL}.
  </details>

- **[HEART-Bench: Do LLMs Make Persona-Consistent Decisions?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30058)**  `arXiv:2605.30058`  `cs.CL`  
  _Weihan Peng, Chenxu Zhang, Qianao Wang, Yuling Shi, Heng Lian, Qihong Mao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents in interactive entertainment and social applications, including companions, role-play characters, and persona clones, need decisions grounded in personality and past experiences. We introduce HEART-Bench to evaluate memory-grounded persona-consistent response selection. The benchmark combines 11 Big Five-anchored synthetic characters, each with 1,000 autobiographical memories, and 64 DIAMONDS-guided decision scenarios into 673 multiple-choice questions. Domain experts independently compare candidate responses, with a senior expert resolving disagreements and revising responses when needed; other characters' adjudicated responses to the same scenario serve as distractors. In the main evaluation, models receive character IDs and occupations, scenarios, and options, with retrieved memories added in memory-augmented settings. Detailed personality-and-value profiles are withheld. We evaluate 16 LLMs under No-Retrieval, Naive-RAG, Mem0, and PersonaDB. Accuracy without memories stays near the 25% chance level, and the best memory-augmented configuration reaches 64.9%. On a matched 150-item subset, human annotators reach 80.8%, compared with 64.0% for the best model. Memory-content ablations show the importance of retrieving experiences that match both the target character and the decision context.
  </details>

- **[PubMedCausal: A Span-Level Annotated Corpus for Causal Relation Extraction in Biomedical Text](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28363)**  `arXiv:2605.28363`  `cs.CL`  
  _Ifeoluwa Kunle-John, Josiah Paul, Oluwatosin Agbaakin, Peter Aina, Ikenna Odezuligbo, Sydney Anuyah_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Causal relation extraction (CRE) is central to biomedical text mining, but current resources often conflate causal relations with broader associations, restrict annotation to sentence-level examples, or focus mainly on explicit causal cues. This limits their usefulness for evaluating whether models can recover causal claims as they are actually expressed in biomedical text. We introduce PubMedCausal, a span-level annotated corpus for biomedical CRE built from PubMed abstracts. The corpus contains 30,000 paragraph-level rows, including 3,945 causal rows and 6,491 adjudicated cause--effect pairs. Each causal relation is annotated with full-text cause and effect spans, causality type, and sententiality, enabling evaluation of both causal detection and full-span causal extraction. We benchmark discriminative encoders and open-source generative models across detection and extraction settings. For causal detection, biomedical encoders are strongest, with PubMedBERT reaching an F$_1$ score of 0.7391. For span-level extraction, the best generative baseline is DeepSeek-R1-32B with few-shot prompting, reaching a Cosine Pair F$_1$ of 0.6765. We further test transfer learning by evaluating PubMedCausal-trained encoders on external causal relation datasets, showing that the resource supports cross-dataset evaluation. Our results show that biomedical CRE remains difficult under class imbalance, long causal spans, implicit causality, inter-sentential relations, and prompt sensitivity. Code and Data can be found here:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Reinforced Fast Weights via Next-Sequence Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.16704)**  `arXiv:2602.16704`  `cs.CL`  
  _Hee Seung Hwang, Xindi Wu, Sanghyuk Chun, Olga Russakovsky, Zhiwei Deng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fast weight architectures offer a promising alternative to standard transformers for long-context modeling by replacing the KV-cache with a recurrently updated fixed-size memory. Despite this architectural shift, they are typically trained with the same next-token prediction (NTP) objective as standard transformers. This creates a mismatch: fast weight models rely on an evolving memory state to support future predictions, while NTP provides only token-level supervision for the immediate next token. We address this mismatch with next-sequence prediction (NSP), a sequence-level extension of NTP that trains models to produce coherent multi-token continuations from their recurrent memory state. To optimize this sequence-level objective, we propose ReFINE (Reinforced Fast weIghts via Next sEquence prediction). ReFINE selects informative token positions based on prediction entropy, generates multi-token rollouts, assigns sequence-level rewards, and optimizes the model with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). ReFINE is applicable throughout the training lifecycle of pre-trained language models: mid-training, post-training, and test-time training. Experiments on LaCT-760M and DeltaNet-1.3B show that ReFINE consistently outperforms NTP-based supervised fine-tuning in needle-in-a-haystack retrieval, long-context question answering, and diverse tasks in LongBench. ReFINE provides an effective and versatile framework for improving long-context modeling in fast weight architectures.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating Geographic Lexical Knowledge in Large Language Models Across 21 Spanish-Speaking Countries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.09346)**  `arXiv:2602.09346`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yoshifumi Kawasaki_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) increasingly serve users across regional varieties, yet their knowledge of geographic lexical variation remains insufficiently evaluated. We investigate country-conditioned lexical usage judgments in Spanish using an expert-curated database covering 934 lexical items across 21 Spanish-speaking countries. Treating ten proprietary and open-weight LLMs as virtual informants, we evaluate two complementary question formats: Yes-No question format (YNQF) and multiple-choice question format (MCQF). The evaluation comprises 10,164 YNQF questions and 16,637 MCQF questions, assessed using F1 and an adjusted Jaccard coefficient, respectively. Results reveal substantial differences across models, countries, and formats. Spain receives the highest country-level score for eight models in YNQF and nine in MCQF; Chile receives the lowest MCQF score for every model. Regression analyses suggest that digital resource volume is generally more strongly associated with YNQF performance, whereas lexical distance from Spain is more strongly associated with MCQF performance. These findings provide a fine-grained diagnostic of uneven regional lexical knowledge and highlight the importance of complementary elicitation formats and evaluation metrics. The benchmark measures explicit usage judgments, motivating separate assessment of region-appropriate generation.
  </details>

- **[OdysseyArena: Benchmarking Large Language Models For Long-Horizon, Active and Inductive Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.05843)**  `arXiv:2602.05843`  `cs.CL`  
  _Fangzhi Xu, Hang Yan, Qiushi Sun, Jinyang Wu, Zixian Huang, Muye Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The advancement of Large Language Models (LLMs) has catalyzed the development of autonomous agents capable of navigating complex environments. However, existing evaluations primarily adopt a deductive paradigm, where agents execute tasks based on explicitly provided rules and static goals, often within limited planning horizons. Crucially, this neglects the inductive necessity for agents to discover latent transition laws from experience autonomously, which is the cornerstone for enabling agentic foresight and sustaining strategic coherence. To bridge this gap, we introduce OdysseyArena, which re-centers agent evaluation on long-horizon, active, and inductive interactions. We formalize and instantiate four primitives, translating abstract transition dynamics into concrete interactive environments. Building upon this, we establish OdysseyArena-Lite for standardized benchmarking, providing a set of 120 tasks to measure an agent's inductive efficiency and long-horizon discovery. Pushing further, we introduce OdysseyArena-Challenge to stress-test agent stability across extreme interaction horizons (e.g., > 200 steps). Extensive experiments on 15+ leading LLMs reveal that even frontier models exhibit a deficiency in inductive scenarios, identifying a critical bottleneck in the pursuit of autonomous discovery in complex environments. Project homepage:this https URL
  </details>

- **[From the Rock Floor to the Cloud: A Systematic Survey of State-of-the-Art NLP in Battery Life Cycle](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.27369)**  `arXiv:2510.27369`  `cs.CL`  
  _Tosin Adewumi, Martin Karlsson, Marcus Liwicki, Mikael Sjödahl, Lama Alkhaled, Rihab Gargouri, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This work presents a systematic review of the application of natural language processing (NLP) along the entire battery life cycle, instead of the review of one stage or method, and introduces a novel conceptual technical language processing (TLP) framework for the European Union (EU)'s proposed digital battery passport (DBP) and other general battery predictions, as the main objective. It follows the Preferred Reporting Items for Systematic Reviews and Meta-Analyses (PRISMA) method and employs three reputable databases or search engines, including Google Scholar, Institute of Electrical and Electronics Engineers Xplore (IEEE Xplore), and Scopus, searched in October, 2025. The search criteria are 'text', 'language processing', and 'battery' while the exclusion criteria are non-relevant papers and those published before 2017, which was applied to all databases, except IEEE Xplore because it turned up fewer results. Consequently, it assesses 274 scientific papers before the critical review of the final 66 relevant papers. We publicly provide artifacts of the review for validation and possible reproducibility. The findings show that new NLP tasks are emerging in the battery domain, which facilitate materials discovery and other stages of the life cycle. Notwithstanding, challenges remain, such as the lack of standard benchmarks. The proposed TLP framework, which incorporates agentic AI and optimized prompts, may be apt for tackling some of the challenges.
  </details>

- **[FullFront: Benchmarking MLLMs Across the Full Front-End Engineering Workflow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17399)**  `arXiv:2505.17399`  `cs.CL`  
  _Haoyu Sun, Huichen Will Wang, Jiawei Gu, Linjie Li, Yu Cheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Front-end engineering involves a complex workflow where engineers conceptualize designs, translate them into code, and iteratively refine the implementation. While recent benchmarks primarily focus on converting visual designs to code, we present FullFront, a benchmark designed to evaluate Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) \textbf{across the full front-end development pipeline}. FullFront assesses three fundamental tasks that map directly to the front-end engineering pipeline: Webpage Design (conceptualization phase), Webpage Perception QA (comprehension of visual organization and elements), and Webpage Code Generation (implementation phase). Unlike existing benchmarks that use either scraped websites with bloated code or oversimplified LLM-generated HTML, FullFront employs a novel, two-stage process to transform real-world webpages into clean, standardized HTML while maintaining diverse visual designs and avoiding copyright issues. Extensive testing of state-of-the-art MLLMs reveals significant limitations in page perception, code generation (particularly for image handling and layout), and interaction implementation. Our results quantitatively demonstrate performance disparities across models and tasks, and highlight a substantial gap between current MLLM capabilities and human expert performance in front-end engineering. The FullFront benchmark and code are available inthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[EffiBench-X: A Multi-Language Benchmark for Measuring Efficiency of LLM-Generated Code](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.13004)**  `arXiv:2505.13004`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yuhao Qing, Boyu Zhu, Mingzhe Du, Zhijiang Guo, Terry Yue Zhuo, Qianru Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing code generation benchmarks primarily evaluate functional correctness, with limited focus on code efficiency and often restricted to a single language like Python. To address this gap, we introduce EffiBench-X, the first multi-language benchmark designed to measure the efficiency of LLM-generated code. EffiBench-X supports Python, C++, Java, JavaScript, Ruby, and Golang. It comprises competitive programming tasks with human-expert solutions as efficiency baselines. Evaluating state-of-the-art LLMs on EffiBench-X reveals that while models generate functionally correct code, they consistently underperform human experts in efficiency. Even the most efficient LLM-generated solutions (Qwen3-32B) achieve only around \textbf{62\%} of human efficiency on average, with significant language-specific variations. LLMs show better efficiency in Python, Ruby, and JavaScript than in Java, C++, and Golang. For instance, DeepSeek-R1's Python code is significantly more efficient than its Java code. These results highlight the critical need for research into LLM optimization techniques to improve code efficiency across diverse languages. The dataset and evaluation infrastructure are submitted and available atthis https URLandthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Concordat: Typed Sessions for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37461)**  `arXiv:2610.37461`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Milad Sikaroudi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agents built on large language models are increasingly allowed to take consequential actions: cancelling bookings, issuing refunds, changing accounts. The rules that govern those actions, such as "obtain the customer's explicit confirmation before changing anything", are usually stated in the prompt and followed only as reliably as the model chooses to follow them. We present Concordat, a Python library that treats an agent as an object whose interface is a finite-state protocol, in the spirit of typestate and session types, and enforces that protocol on the model at run time. On every turn the model is offered only the tools that are legal in the current protocol state, calls outside that set are rejected before they have side effects, decisions reserved for humans can only be requested, and token and tool-call budgets are charged and split linearly across handoffs. Protocols are checked when they are defined, and a subclass of an agent is rejected if it could bypass its parent's protocol, a behavioural form of Liskov substitution. We evaluate a Concordat agent against the standard agent of tau-bench on its 50 airline tasks, adding simulated customers who pressure the agent to skip confirmation. The standard agent made changes without the required "yes" in 13 of 50 tasks with gpt-4.1 and in 29 of 128 task-runs with Claude Haiku 5.5; the Concordat agent made none, with no significant difference in task success. tau-bench's own reward did not detect most of these violations. Concordat is open source.
  </details>

- **[Remembering Is Not Enough: Refining Memories and Learning When to Use Them in Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36652)**  `arXiv:2610.36652`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Yipeng Hao, Yuxuan Cai, Wenkai Ji, Jie Zhou, Liyang Yu, Qin Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Memory helps LLM-based multi-agent systems reuse experience, but stored advice can be flawed or applied in the wrong situation. Team-level success or failure alone cannot reliably show whether a memory needs correction or should not have been used. We propose DiscernMem, a training-free framework that improves both memory quality and memory use. To improve quality, Defect-Aware Memory Refinement (DMR) compares team executions with a single memory and with no memory, diagnoses rule defects, and tests repairs on current and earlier tasks to guard against losing previously useful behavior. To guide appropriate use, Boundary-Learning Meta-Memory (BLM) records each memory's past uses and effects, learning when its owner role should apply or avoid it based on task context and incoming messages. Together, these components help teams correct flawed experience while retaining useful rules that were applied incorrectly. Experiments with Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507 on four benchmarks show that DiscernMem achieves the highest average score on AutoGen, CAMEL, and MacNet, exceeding the strongest reported baselines by 0.51-4.32 points. It leads on code generation and symbolic planning across all three systems, including a PDDL score of 37.71 versus 28.48 on CAMEL.
  </details>

- **[Ontology-guided multi-agent reasoning for explainable differential diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36325)**  `arXiv:2610.36325`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Roxana Ramona Szomiu, George Ursu, Adrian Groza_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Effective differential diagnosis depends on well structured and unambiguous patient case descriptions, since incomplete or ambiguous clinical information readily leads to diagnostic errors and inconsistent reasoning. Large Language Models (LLMs) have recently shown promising capabilities in medical question answering and clinical reasoning; however, even when achieving competitive performance on medical benchmarks, they often rely on pattern recognition learned from training data rather than grounded clinical evidence, and can generate plausible yet unsupported diagnostic conclusions. Their reasoning remains largely opaque, making it difficult for clinicians to assess the evidence and rationale behind a given recommendation. To address this gap, we propose an ontology-guided, multiagent differential diagnosis framework that replicates the iterative reasoning process of physicians. The framework grounds diagnostic inference in a structured, expert-curated medical knowledge base, enabling interpretation of symptoms, clinical findings, or disease relationships, while decomposing diagnosis into explicit, auditable reasoning steps. This ontology-based grounding constrains inference to evidence-backed associations, reducing hallucination and improving diagnostic transparency, traceability, and consistency. The system then identifies the most discriminative missing symptoms and generates targeted follow-up questions to reduce uncertainty through a process that mirrors to clinical inquiry. Together, these components improve the reliability, explainability, and diagnostic accuracy of AI-assisted differential diagnosis. The source code is publicly available.
  </details>

- **[Agent-Conditioned Routing: Diverse Reasoning Paths Within One Frozen Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37375)**  `arXiv:2610.37375`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG` `cs.MA`  
  _Ananth Kalyanasundaram, Ankush Kadu, Athira Gopal, Aswanth Krishnan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generating several solutions can expose alternative methods, but additional trajectories often paraphrase one dominant approach. We investigate whether the conditional capacity of a frozen sparse mixture-of-experts (MoE) model can provide such diversity from one problem prompt, without user-assigned roles, prescribed methods, fine-tuning, or model copies. Agent-Conditioned Routing (ACR) assigns $m$ parallel trajectories persistent, untrained shared/private expert-eligibility masks while preserving the learned, input-dependent token router. On a screened 126-question multi-mode mathematics and physics benchmark, with model judges reading the reported derivations in full, we count the number of problems yielding at least two distinct primary methods among soft-correct solutions in four trajectories. ACR reaches approximately twice as many such problems as the strongest temperature setting under GLM 5.3 (37.3 versus 18.7), and 1.67 times as many under GPT-5.6 Sol (45.00 versus 27.00), at matched output budgets. A blinded four-expert audit on a stratified sample of 30 problems also finds more multi-method solutions with ACR on both backbones, with lower correctness. Matched-eligibility ablations show modest multi-method count advantages for ACR, while token-resampled masks attain higher correctness. An informed sequential prompt elicits more diversity still, using previous solutions and explicit requests for alternative methods. ACR adds about 160 KiB of mask state. In a three-problem microbenchmark capped at 1,024 tokens, its latency and peak VRAM usage remain within 3% of batched temperature sampling.
  </details>

- **[Hard Constraints, Smooth Gradients: Learning Feasible Inventory Policies via Differentiable Projection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02343)**  `arXiv:2608.02343`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Patrick Helm, Jan-Niklas Doerr, Joren Gijsbrechts, Stefan Minner_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many operational problems are constrained sequential decision processes with combinatorial action spaces and interdependent constraints. Mixed-integer linear programs (MILPs) handle such constraints flexibly but scale poorly in stochastic environments. Deep reinforcement learning (DRL) promises scalable decision rules, but existing methods either penalize constraints rather than enforce them, or rely on feasibility mechanisms that break down once constraints interact. We bridge this gap by embedding a differentiable optimization module inside the policy: a neural network proposes continuous action targets, a quadratic program projects them onto the relaxed feasible set, and a dual-informed integer mapping restores integrality while preserving feasibility. Given a differentiable simulator, the policy trains end-to-end from sampled trajectories using pathwise gradients, while handling hard constraints with similar flexibility to MILPs. We show that our approach has bounded error relative to an exact integer projection and ensures the entire feasible action space is reachable. We apply the method to multi-echelon production-inventory planning under shared resource and material constraints. Our policy attains an average optimality gap of 1% on 3-item instances and consistently outperforms a rolling-horizon multi-stage stochastic program by at least 5.6% in 10-item networks. Compared to state-of-the-art echelon base-stock policies, savings reach 15.3% on 40-item instances. On an industry-scale case study with nonstationary demand from ASML, we reduce average cost by up to 1.8% relative to the best-known benchmark policy. The savings are largest where planning is hardest: in tightly capacitated systems with high demand variability. More broadly, we show that DRL can improve upon state-of-the-art baselines in sequential decision problems with hard constraints, which are widespread in practice.
  </details>

- **[ScalableRAG: High-Quality RAG with Exact Corpus Operations at Minimal Ingestion Cost](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25135)**  `arXiv:2607.25135`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Hilaf Hasson, Aditya Chakravarty, Jayant Thomas, Krishna Gogineni_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  RAG systems face a capability-cost gap: lightweight retrievers locate evidence but expose no operands for exact corpus-level operations (counts, intersections, aggregations), while knowledge ingestion systems obtain them only through expensive ingestion. ScalableRAG closes this gap with a persistent typed-set workspace: an agent builds named document and value sets at query time and composes them through set algebra, typed extraction, aggregation, and diagnostics.Zero-Ingestion ScalableRAG needs no preprocessing, not even a vector index. It matches the leading baseline on four conventional QA benchmarks (74.75 versus 74.64) and on aggregative Hotels-50 beats every evaluated system, scoring 71.74: 36.23 above the leading baseline (SRAG), and 40.22 points above A-RAG. Removing the workspace drops Hotels-50 to 38.4. On Hotels-350, the partition SRAG itself reports on, ScalableRAG leads SRAG by 9.3 points. Limited-Ingestion ScalableRAG adds a lightweight index and is the only evaluated system above 80 judge score at 5M documents, at 13 to 16 seconds per question and under 20 minutes ingestion; at 1M it scores 84.5 to A-RAG's 80.0 with 16x lower latency and 0.5% of its ingestion time. ScalableRAG combines minimal ingestion, conventional-QA parity, exact corpus operations, and million-document viability; no evaluated alternative provides all four.Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[The Trinity:Capability-Driven Recursive Self-Improvement for Multimodal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36077)**  `arXiv:2610.36077`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Weidong Tang, Ruiheng Wang, Jingxuan Zhang, Hexin Wang, Haoyuan Chen, Zhengfeiyang Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal intelligence can be viewed as human-guided co-evolution between worlds and agents through interaction. However, scaling this process faces two bottlenecks: constructing heterogeneous, verifiable environments is costly, and manual Harness iteration cannot keep pace with growing task diversity and complexity. A key insight is that tasks with different modalities and surface forms can share the same underlying capability requirements. We therefore introduce The Trinity, a recursive self-improvement framework coupling world generation with Harness evolution through three roles: Creator, Explorer, and Evolver. Drawing on verified execution trajectories, Evolver shifts the basis of task organization from surface categories to shared requirements, reorganizing the original task tree into a hierarchical capability tree. We introduce Capability-Driven Real2Sim2Real: for heterogeneous tasks at the same node, Creator abstracts their common requirements into simplified, verifiable simulated worlds for capability learning, while evaluation on original tasks measures transfer. Along this capability tree, Explorer progressively evolves reusable capability modules through selective inheritance, forming a Harness tree whose validated updates accumulate across iterations within a fixed topology. The resulting Harnesses specialize in different capabilities, motivating a Capability-Aware Harness Router that uses public task information to select compatible module configurations from the tree. Our evolved Harnesses improve mean performance by 34 points across 28 training benchmarks and 26 points across 18 unseen benchmarks, including two real-world embodied tasks.
  </details>

- **[Seeing the Options: Typed Decisions over Visual Candidates with Reusable Option Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33702)**  `arXiv:2610.33702`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Fengxin Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agents end many steps in a typed decision over a set of candidates: which screen element to click, which product to pick. System One models answer in one forward pass, and Jeva showed that four properties the setting demands - a "none of the above" probability, invariance to candidate order, scalability in the number of candidates, and reuse of candidate computation - follow from encoding the request and each candidate separately and letting a request state read a candidate memory. Jeva is text-only, and existing multimodal System One models (OneJev, Jev-Omni) put images in the request while candidates stay text in letter slots. This paper asks what happens to the four properties, and to accuracy, when the candidates themselves are images. Iris admits image, text, or mixed candidates and leaves Jeva's decision module untouched. On a visual-candidate benchmark built from Multimodal-Mind2Web (every candidate is the element's screenshot crop, K=16, three splits), independent encoding reaches 78.4/72.1/74.8% with a flip rate of exactly zero, while joint encoding (OneJev-4B fine-tuned on the same data) reaches 90.6/89.5/91.6% but changes its answer on 8-10% of records when the crops are reordered. Most of the gap is the candidate not seeing the request: a third design that encodes each candidate after the state - but never after another candidate - keeps order invariance exactly, shares the state pass through a forked cache, and reaches 92.1/87.0/90.2% at a third of OneJev's latency with a calibrated null probability (AUROC 0.93-0.97). Letting pooled candidates see each other (set attention, token-level reads, multi-vector memory) buys nothing, because the encoder maps visually distinct elements to vectors within cosine 0.999 of each other. Reusing image candidates across requests cuts latency 37x at K=256 and 191x at K=2048. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Batching the Reflective Optimization Loop: Parallel Proposals Make GEPA Faster and Better](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36774)**  `arXiv:2610.36774`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Jialin Zhang, Lakshya A Agrawal, Shangyin Tan, Donghyun Lee, Lutfi Eren Erdogan, Dan Klein, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reflective text optimization makes agentic systems better, but the optimization loop is itself a resource-hungry workload whose sequential structure leaves parallel evaluation capacity idle. We extend the open-source GEPA optimizer to propose and evaluate a batch of candidates on each optimization step instead of one candidate at a time. In our sweep on two tasks, most batched runs finished in half the wall-clock time or less, and the fastest in about a quarter to a third. Batched settings also achieved higher held-out test scores, from 68.9% to 72.1% on LiveBench-Math (with $2\times2$) and from 49.0% to 60.0% on HoVer (with $8\times1$). A simple runtime model explains the measured speedups under a finite worker pool, and batched settings also spend their metric-call and dollar budgets more efficiently. We frame the axes along which a reflective optimization step can scale in parallel, a first look at allocating a fixed optimization budget across them.
  </details>

- **[Guided-RLVR: Guided Exploration in Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37041)**  `arXiv:2610.37041`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Bo Yang, Zishuai Zhang, Weihua Li, Qinnan Zhang, Sijia Wen, Hainan Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has shown strong empirical gains in improving the reasoning ability of large language models (LLMs) on tasks such as mathematics and programming. However, recent studies have shown that RLVR rollouts often collapse onto a narrow set of reasoning trajectories, limiting the explored reasoning space. Motivated by findings that thinking tokens at critical steps serve as critical structural elements in LLM reasoning pathways, we propose Guided-RLVR, a novel approach that augments RLVR with a lightweight controller. The controller strategically injects semantically meaningful steering tokens into the sampling distribution, encouraging the model to approach problems from multiple reasoning perspectives and thereby expanding the exploration space. Experiments on mathematical reasoning and code generation benchmarks show that Guided-RLVR consistently outperforms existing RLVR methods while producing more diverse reasoning trajectories.
  </details>

- **[AutoVend: Benchmarking LLM Agents on Long-Horizon Autonomous Vending Operations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36980)**  `arXiv:2610.36980`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Qiming Shi, Yikun Li, Beidi Luan, Rui Sun, Yan Ma, Jing Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents are extending agent-mediated commerce on the Web from shopping for consumers to operating businesses for sellers. In online-to-offline (O2O) commerce, decisions are issued through Web-based platforms but realized through costly physical execution. Evaluating such agents requires going beyond individual transactions to ask whether decisions made online turn into sustained economic returns. We introduce AutoVend, a benchmark in which one LLM agent manages 20 vending machines across five site types through a Web-based operating interface for 180 simulated days. Its data-grounded simulator combines a shared warehouse, procurement lead times, paid replenishment, inventory expiration, and recurring operating costs. We evaluate five LLM agents against a rule-based inventory-control baseline and diagnose their site coordination, execution, and performance over time. Four of five evaluated LLM agents fall short of this baseline, and returns per dollar of inference cost rank models differently from returns alone. Operational analyses show that gains in individual metrics do not consistently yield stronger economic growth, as inventory and execution costs erode these gains. Trajectory analysis further reveals difficulties in grounding feasible actions, managing inventory commitments, and converting feedback into effective policy revisions.
  </details>

- **[MedConstBench: Benchmarking How Constitutions Steer LLMs in Medical Ethics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36513)**  `arXiv:2610.36513`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Yibo Yang, Zerui Huang, Haomiao Yang, Zixia Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As large language models advance rapidly, they are increasingly used to answer medical questions, and many such models are now deployed locally. Once a model is deployed, its behavior is governed mainly by the ethical policy written into its constitution. However, most existing work focuses on factual correctness rather than the trade-off between competing ethical values. Even benchmarks that do evaluate ethics treat it as a fixed property of the model rather than as a variable, so how a constitution reshapes medical-ethics behavior remains largely unexplored. We introduce MedConstBench, a medical-ethics benchmark that treats the deployment constitution as a controlled variable, is organized around Beauchamp's four principles, and is scored by human-calibrated multi-model peer review. It includes MedDilemma, our own dataset of clinical dilemmas, and supports a systematic analysis of how constitutions steer LLMs. Evaluating ten open-source LLMs under four constitutions, we find that the effect of a constitution is dimension-selective and model-dependent, and that no constitution is universally optimal. Motivated by these findings, we propose a do-no-harm adaptive selector that chooses a constitution per model, matching the per-model oracle while guaranteeing a worst-case floor and generalizing to unseen models at low cost.
  </details>

- **[AC-JEPA: Anchored Cross-Modal Joint Embedding Predictive Architecture for Visual-to-Symbolic Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36493)**  `arXiv:2610.36493`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Chaofei Hong, Ziyang Chen, Baochen Wang, Christina Yi Jin, Qing Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cross-modal translation requires models to capture shared semantics across modalities while preserving the fine-grained structure required by the target modality. Yet in real-world scientific domains, paired data are often imperfect, while obtaining clean pairs at scale is costly. Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) directly optimizes cross-modal translation through categorical token-level supervision, but this objective is sensitive to errors in the target sequences. JEPA offers a complementary representation-space objective, but most existing formulations focus on prediction within a modality rather than translation across modalities. We introduce Anchored Cross-Modal JEPA (AC-JEPA), an SFT-compatible plug-in that predicts masked intermediate states in both cross-modal directions using paired data. The joint-embedding objective targets cross-modal structure predictable from context, while the causal SFT path retains direct token-level supervision for the target sequence. During self-training, AC-JEPA learns from continuous latent targets derived from imperfect pseudo-labeled pairs, offering intrinsic tolerance to local data errors without promoting pseudo-tokens to hard categorical supervision. Using optical chemical structure recognition as a testbed, AC-JEPA trained on clean pairs achieves 76.07% accuracy on the Simple set of the WildMol benchmark, compared with 67.40% for SFT alone. Using the same pseudo-labeled data for both approaches, AC-JEPA-based self-training further raises accuracy to 80.23%, whereas accuracy under direct SFT drops from 67.40% to 63.47%. These results support SFT-anchored joint-embedding prediction as an effective strategy for cross-modal translation under both curated and imperfect supervision.
  </details>

- **[Don't Pick a Winner: Knowledge Conflicts Need a Prior, Not a Rule](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36169)**  `arXiv:2610.36169`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Minh Pham Van, Cong Tran_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A retrieval-augmented language model answers from two sources, the retrieved context and its own knowledge, and the two disagree whenever the context is incomplete or wrong. Existing methods settle the disagreement with a rule fixed at design time: follow the context, amplify it in proportion to how far it moves the model, or trust the model's memory. Each rule is confidently wrong in some deployment. In a decision-theoretic model of two sources, the score of any fixed rule is linear in the share of reliable contexts while the best attainable score is convex, so every fixed rule is beaten wherever it is not exactly optimal; and a symmetric divergence between the two sources, the signal adaptive decoding uses, carries no information about which one is right. Resolving a conflict therefore needs two inputs that no existing method keeps: evidence about each source separately, and a prior on how often the context is right. We test these claims on GRACE, a benchmark that intervenes on the knowledge-graph context of a fixed question, so that what the model knows, what the context says and what is true are measured separately. There, every baseline from four families is strong on right contexts or on wrong ones, never on both. We then build the two inputs: the model states its knowledge as notes, a small policy voices each note with a calibrated confidence, a reader answers in two voices, and a selector commits or abstains under a prior $\pi$ applied as an exact prior shift. With $\pi$ set to the true share of right contexts, the system is above the best baseline at every mixture of GRACE contexts, by up to 26 points. Its overall score is 45.3 and 35.0 (Qwen3.5-35B-A3B, Llama-3.1-8B), against 29.4 and 27.5 for the best baseline. On preregistered ClashEval it beats same-backbone CK-PLUG and TCR by 12 and 26 points. Where the model lacks the knowledge, the gains vanish, as the theory predicts.
  </details>

- **[EvoIn: Bridging Evolution and Internalization for Agent Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35290)**  `arXiv:2609.35290`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Shihan Dou, Shaofan Liu, Zhonghang Lu, Jiahang Lin, Shichun Liu, Binghai Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent work has explored improving agents by jointly evolving their harnesses and models, but often takes a ''potpourri'' approach that bundles together new tools, new decision-making procedures, and model adaptation to the evolved harness under a single notion of agent improvement. In this paper, we instead investigate how agents can improve their decision-making procedures. In particular, we propose EvoIn, an agent fine-tuning framework that bridges evolution and internalization. EvoIn first analyzes agent execution traces to evolve and validate new decision-making procedures by temporarily instantiating them in the harness. The validated procedures guide the agent to generate improved reasoning traces. These traces are then rewritten into self-contained reasoning traces, removing explicit references to harness instructions while expressing the induced decision logic as the model's own reasoning. Finally, EvoIn fine-tunes the model on the rewritten traces, internalizing these procedures so that the improved decision-making persists without the evolved harness at inference time. We evaluate EvoIn on diverse benchmarks and find that it consistently enables agents to learn stronger decision-making procedures, raising the pass rate by 10.9 points in-domain and by 9.2 points out-of-domain. Results further show that the internalized decision procedures generalize to unseen tasks. Case studies show that agents can learn to decide how to solve a task before solving it, for example by checking a document's length to choose between reading it in full and searching it. EvoIn is also broadly applicable, showing consistent improvements on another model family.
  </details>

- **[KPPax: preprocess differentiable kinetic ODE for machine learning in atmospheric chemistry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37655)**  `arXiv:2610.37655`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wenqing Peng, Michael Boy, Zhi-Song Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Atmospheric chemical mechanisms are commonly distributed in the language of the Kinetic PreProcessor (KPP), which generates efficient Fortran or C code but does not fit into the differentiable programming frameworks in which machine-learning models are developed. We present KPPax, a kinetic preprocessor that generates differentiable, just-in-time compiled, and vectorisable chemistry code in JAX from mechanisms written in the KPP language. KPPax evaluates the rate law with sparse operations in log space, which keeps it differentiable, and integrates the stiff system with the implicit solvers and the forward- and reverse-mode adjoints of the \texttt{diffrax} library, so that exact sensitivities are obtained without hand-derived adjoint code. We demonstrate two applications with the POLLU benchmark mechanism. In the first, the Fisher information matrix computed from an automatically differentiated Jacobian predicts, before any fit, which of the 25 rate coefficients a flow-tube-like design can identify, and noisy synthetic estimation confirms the predicted uncertainties of the nine identifiable rate coefficients to within about 30\% while revealing clusters of fast reactions of which only ratios are constrained. In the second, a neural-network correction of an emission inventory is trained offline on per-scenario 4D-Var retrievals and then fine-tuned online through the chemical model, which reduces the error of the biogenic correction by a factor of 2.4--5 and recovers information on low-temperature emissions that the individual retrievals lose. KPPax makes KPP mechanisms directly usable for parameter estimation and hybrid physics--machine-learning modelling.
  </details>

- **[What Improves During Pretraining? Tracking Knowledge Acquisition with Point-in-Time Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37631)**  `arXiv:2610.37631`  `cs.AI`  
  _Chang Su, Bin Wu, Emine Yilmaz, Yao Lu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language models pretrained on web corpora collected over multiple years learn both general and domain-specific knowledge. How these different kinds of knowledge are learned as pretraining progresses remains unclear. Recent point-in-time models trained on web snapshots from different years provide a way to probe how knowledge prevalence varies over time. Using these models, we propose a temporal variability score (TVS), defined as the coefficient of variation of an item's negative log-likelihood across models. A higher score suggests that the knowledge may be less consistently represented across years of web text. We evaluate six benchmarks covering diverse topics using six models from multiple open-source model families. We find that low- and high-TVS items are learned similarly early in training, but high-TVS items improve faster later on. At the end of the pretraining run, high-TVS items outperform low-TVS items in 34 of 36 model-benchmark settings, with an average advantage of 7.3 points of chance-normalized accuracy. This pattern is most robust on knowledge-intensive tasks, even when comparing items of similar difficulty. Our findings reveal differences in how knowledge is learned over the course of pretraining, with temporal variability providing a diagnostic of these learning dynamics beyond aggregate benchmark scores.
  </details>

- **[Timestral](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37585)**  `arXiv:2610.37585`  `cs.AI`  
  _Adrien Petralia, Alexandre Gavaudan, Artjom Joosen, Etienne Goffinet, Richard Kurle, Yassine El Ouahidi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce Timestral, a family of four time series foundation models for probabilistic forecasting, with 5M, 28M, 123M, and 209M parameters. Timestral sets a new state of the art on GIFT-Eval, TIME, and fev-bench, where Timestral-209M and Timestral-123M take the top two places on every metric. We achieve this with three ingredients: Decoupled Horizon Forecasting (DHF), which separates prediction tokens from the causally encoded context, so that longer horizons are trained without masking context and each observed value is both a forecast target and context for later forecasts; a covariate framework that natively supports known-future covariates within a causal architecture; and a two-stage pipeline that pretrains exclusively on a diverse, large-scale synthetic corpus before post-training on a mix of real and synthetic data with extended context. This report describes the model design and its benchmark results. Weights for all four models are available on Hugging Face and inference code on GitHub.
  </details>

- **[TeamGym: A Gym for Team Decision-Making by AI Agents with Distributed Information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37571)**  `arXiv:2610.37571`  `cs.AI`  
  _Chanwoo Park, Jaywoong Jeong, Zachary Wojtowicz, Yubin Kim, Taekyun Lee, Kaiqing Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many real-world decisions require teams to integrate information distributed across participants. Yet AI agents are still primarily trained and benchmarked in isolation. We introduce TeamGym, an environment for evaluating and training language model-based agents on team decision-making problems constructed from real-world legal, finance, medical, and software engineering corpora. TeamGym introduces a method to identify a minimal set of facts, each element of which is necessary and all of which are collectively sufficient for a correct final decision, which we call pivotal information. This enables TeamGym to both control the distribution of pivotal information and track how well agents identify and integrate pivotal information when making team decisions. Examining 8 language models on 12 source corpora, we find that current AI agents often fail to effectively share information: Distributing pivotal information across participants lowers success rate by 22.9 percentage points on average relative to giving the same information to a single model (a communication gap). We also document that participants do not effectively retrieve decision-relevant information on their own: A team's success rate is lowered by a further 19.9 points when participants must identify and extract pivotal information from source material rather than receiving it directly (a retrieval gap). To help remedy these deficits, we use TeamGym to train policies through reinforcement learning on team decision loss. Across two policy-training experiments, we find that training improves final-decision quality and induces emergent coordination in participation, speaker selection, and communication, even though the reward evaluates only the final decision.
  </details>

- **[From Interaction to Capability: Task Advancement as State and Supervision for GUI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37482)**  `arXiv:2610.37482`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ziyang Chen, Qian Li, Chengwei Qin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  GUI memory should preserve the results of past actions and learn reusable operations. This is difficult in long-horizon workflows, where agents must use information across applications and useful operations are interleaved with failed attempts. We introduce Task Advancement Memory (TAM), which defines task advancement as verified task progress, and supports ongoing task execution and learns reusable operations. During execution, Task Advancement State represents how verified task progress changes the remaining goals, allowing the agent to build on earlier achievements across applications. After each task, advancement supervision identifies and abstracts the interactions that produced a verified local result. The learned operations preserve the relation between required inputs, expected outcomes, and verification conditions. This retains locally successful operations from both successful and failed tasks. On MobileWorld GUI-only tasks, Advancement State alone raises success from 12.0% to 28.2%. Full TAM achieves a 17.6% relative gain in task success over the strongest evaluated baseline and reduces lifecycle tokens per solved task by 38.7%. On SPA-Bench, TAM achieves state-of-the-art task success in the single-app English Level 3 setting.
  </details>

- **[M4Bench: Evaluating Procedural Specification for Clinical EHR Derivation Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37459)**  `arXiv:2610.37459`  `cs.AI`  
  _Hannes Ill, Rafi Al Attrach, Rajna Fani, Ahram Han, Ryohei Yamamoto, Marlene Louisa Moerig, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clinical research over electronic health records depends on derived variables whose definitions sit outside the schema, in item codes, time windows, missingness rules, and output-grain choices that change cohorts and downstream conclusions. We introduce M4Bench, 28 clinical derivation tasks across 15 task families on MIMIC-IV and eICU, to test whether AI coding agents can reproduce such derivations and how clinician-reviewed agent skills change their results. Its controls decompose the value of skills into specification completeness, delivery format, and schema familiarity. In a Docker-isolated Codex campaign (GPT 5.4 Mini and GPT 5.5), skills raise reward by 0.136 over the no-skill baseline, with 26 of 28 tasks improving. Specification completeness accounts for most of this gain: on an eight-task control subset, an operational spec alone raises reward by 0.162, and skills add only 0.024 on top, indistinguishable from zero. On a ten-task subset, decoy skills score like no skill and raw reference SQL nearly matches targeted skills, arguing against generic context or the skill format as the explanation. Schema obfuscation and restructuring preserve 86% and 70% of the skill gain (GPT 5.4 Mini, 26 MIMIC-IV tasks), a descriptive robustness check rather than a bound on memorization. A descriptive Claude Code replication on all 28 tasks (Opus 4.7 and Sonnet 4.6, 560 runs) is directionally consistent (+0.120, 21 of 28 tasks improving). These gains are partial agreement with the reference, not completed tasks: only 25 of 280 with-skill and 13 of 280 no-skill Codex runs pass every strict check, and skills barely change which rows agents return (key F1 +0.003). Skills are released as a versioned, clinician-reviewable markdown delivery format, with a family-level reference-validity audit. M4Bench releases tasks, skills, evaluation code, and sanitized per-run evidence as an open-source benchmark.
  </details>

- **[Clearing the FoG: Training KG Agents to Retrieve the Evidence Behind Their Answers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37405)**  `arXiv:2610.37405`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zongyuan Sun, Shuai Zhao, Yingjun Du, Ke Shu, Ziyue Zhu, Haoran Luo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) can answer many factual questions correctly, but a correct answer is not necessarily a supported one. For an LLM agent, a knowledge graph (KG) plays two roles: as a source of answers, it supplies facts that the model lacks; as a source of evidence, it supplies the supporting triples that make an answer traceable and auditable. However, existing KG agents treat the KG only as a source of answers: their training data carries answers but not evidence, and their training rewards check the answer but not the retrieved evidence, so agents often answer correctly without retrieving the supporting triples. We propose FoG, a framework that trains KG agents to retrieve the evidence behind their answers. FoG consists of evidence-grounded synthesis, a KG interaction agent, and an evidence-coverage process reward (ECPR): the synthesis recursively hides explicit entities behind verifiable sub-questions, so that every question carries its supporting triples by construction; the agent records the triples returned by the KG; and ECPR credits each supporting triple to the turn that first retrieves it. Experiments on three KGQA benchmarks demonstrate that FoG enables 3B--4B open-source agents to outperform substantially larger baselines in answer accuracy and evidence coverage, with zero-shot cross-KG transfer.
  </details>

- **[TCM-DiagCommit: A TCM-SD-Derived Benchmark for Evidence-Conditioned Syndrome Selection and Commitment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37243)**  `arXiv:2610.37243`  `cs.AI`  
  _Erwei Hou, Yue Hu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Traditional Chinese Medicine (TCM) syndrome differentiation is usually evaluated as static final-label selection over complete clinical records. This setting hides three diagnostic behaviors: whether a model distinguishes semantic-neighbor syndrome labels from easy random distractors, whether it updates predictions as evidence fields are revealed, and whether it should commit under uncertainty.
