We introduce TCM-DiagCommit, a benchmark derived from the public TCM-SD/ZY-BERT resource while retaining its source syndrome labels; it converts 1,834 source cases into 23,842 task rows over 148 syndrome labels. The benchmark combines random, profile-neighbor, and full-vocabulary candidate ladders with staged t1/t2/t3 evidence. It also includes fixed-label tongue/pulse perturbations and insufficient-evidence variants. A benchmark-defined action contract scores commit, acquire, defer, and abstain. Across five zero-shot DirectLLM calibrations, random10 top-1 accuracy is 63.2% to 66.4%, but hard20 falls to 24.7% to 30.3% and full148 to 14.8% to 22.3%; on hard20, WT(all) wrong-terminal commits remain 62.2% to 74.3%. These results show that final-answer accuracy alone is insufficient for diagnostic benchmark evaluation: answer selection, evidence use, and benchmark commitment scoring should be measured separately.
  </details>

- **[Does Your Generalist Skill Optimizer Really Incentivize Agentic Tasks? Rethinking Skill Optimization via Bipartite Attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37201)**  `arXiv:2610.37201`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jiawei Zhou, Bo Peng, Yuxuan Huang, Ming Zhang, Youbang Sun, Xiang Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Skill optimizers adapt an agent to long-horizon tasks, such as repairing a terminal environment, by rewriting its skill, a natural-language procedure in its context, and keeping the edits that raise success on a small dev set. On such tasks success is noisy, so a gain on a small dev set does not show that an edit helps, and a success score does not show what the edit costs. We introduce BiPA, which replays an optimizer's acceptance rule on candidates identical to its seed to measure what selection alone reports, and records each episode as a bipartite graph of turns and the text they read to trace extra input tokens to the behavior behind them. Across three optimizers, three agentic benchmarks and two models, the learned skills succeed about as often as their hand-written seed, 0.1 points higher on average. Their dev gains vanish on test: keeping the best of several candidates on at most 30 tasks reports gains this large even when none changed. They read about 16% more input tokens: the optimizers almost always add rules, half to re-check work and some to act one call at a time, and the agent follows them as extra turns, each re-sending the whole conversation. Four principles follow: accept an edit only if its gain exceeds what selection alone reports; charge it for the turns it adds, not for its length; let the editor delete and merge rules; show it what earlier rules cost. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[LiveSearch: Questions as Executable Verifier Code for a Live Benchmark of Professional-Source Financial Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37166)**  `arXiv:2610.37166`  `cs.AI`  
  _Sinuo Wang, Wentao Zhao, Jiacheng Lu, Rui Sun, Beidi Luan, Qiming Shi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In finance, the answer that matters is usually the current one: prices, rates, and disclosures hold only as of a given day, so retrieving up-to-date facts is a core capability of a financial agent. Yet most financial benchmarks overlook timeliness. They fix each answer at annotation time, so the gold goes stale as markets move and, once leaked into training data, rewards recall rather than search and analysis. We introduce LiveSearch, a live benchmark that stores each question as executable verifier code: a typed program of calls to a professional financial data service, followed by a declarative extractor. The question is frozen, while its gold answer is determined by executing the program against professional financial data at evaluation time, and a daily job re-executes it with no human or language model in the loop. A memorized answer is thus likely outdated, and a model must reach the current one through tools and analysis. Construction is equally automatic: a language model only verbalizes programs enumerated from real tool responses and never chooses an answer. Under general web search, six contemporary tool-calling models reach 70.9% to 76.7% answer accuracy. Over three consecutive days, 70% of served questions change their gold value, and even under tolerance-aware matching, a literally reused earlier snapshot fails on a majority of questions within a single day. Per-question tracking uncovers dynamic shifts in correctness masked by aggregate accuracy.
  </details>

- **[WorkFactory: Turning Professional Intents into Executable Work for Scalable Agent Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37161)**  `arXiv:2610.37161`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ziyang Zeng, Tingyang Chen, Jia Deng, Qiyao Wang, Shuo Tang, Yike Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scaling supervised training for working agents requires diverse professional tasks that yield reliable expert demonstrations. However, missing or inconsistent task information can obstruct teacher execution and weaken the resulting supervision. We propose WorkFactory, a scalable framework for constructing coherent and executable professional tasks. Starting from professional intents, information-level planning builds an information support graph to guide task instantiation, explicitly representing the dependencies and locations of information required for the intended outputs. Probe executions then identify support failures and guide repairs while preserving the intended work. We construct 112,686 tasks spanning 612 occupations and use the collected teacher trajectories to fine-tune Qwen models at 4B, 27B, and 35B-A3B scales, yielding the WorkPilot family. All three models improve over their respective base models across four professional-work benchmarks, with WorkPilot-35B-A3B improving from 879 to 1565 Elo on GDPval-AA v2.1. Scaling analyses reveal model-dependent diminishing returns, with execution length first increasing and then decreasing. Controlled component analyses show that planning and repair reduce probe-diagnosed task environment defects and improve downstream performance. The data and models are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Correctness Conditioning as Selection in Chain-of-Thought Faithfulness Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37070)**  `arXiv:2610.37070`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yueer Pan, Yuexi Pan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Restricting chain-of-thought (CoT) faithfulness evaluation to correctly answered instances changes the population being measured. We study this selection step in a seven-variable structural causal model and derive the exact observed gap $\Delta = \operatorname{Cov}(A, C)/P(C=1)$. The model distinguishes difficulty confounding from shortcut-induced collider bias and states the conditional-independence assumption needed to express the gap through latent response propensities. A protocol review of 18 papers distinguishes answer-correctness restrictions from agreement-based selection, benchmark construction, and training-data filtering. We evaluate four sub-ceiling deployments on arithmetic tasks and observe positive selection gaps in all four. The primary GLM-5.3 run yields a +7.4 percentage-point gap ($n = 1000$, $p < 0.0001$), and pooled Cochran-Mantel-Haenszel analysis gives OR = 1.70 ($p < 10^{-6}$). An assumption-free covariance decomposition assigns 42.8% of the GLM-5.3 gap to between-stratum variation in interventionable-operation count, leaving the majority unattributed. When counterfactual compliance is observable for every instance, evaluating all traces directly estimates population faithfulness without correctness filtering.
  </details>

- **[Proactive Memory for Event-Driven LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36967)**  `arXiv:2610.36967`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jinyang Li, Zichuan Fu, Xian Wu, Wenlin Zhang, Yimin Deng, Kaifeng Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Proactive LLM agents anticipate service opportunities from ongoing observations and accumulated history. Existing approaches emphasize recent context or retrieval of related memories, providing limited support for validating recurring behavioral regularities and assessing their applicability to the current event. We propose Proactive Memory (ProactMem), which organizes history into recent events, temporal trajectories, and behavioral regularities. Trajectory facts are checked against source events, and regularities are assessed through historical replay before being matched to current conditions. We also construct ProactiveStream, a longitudinal benchmark covering three application scopes, where agents process isolated event streams chronologically under episode-aware opportunity labels. On ProactiveStream, ProactMem achieves an overall effectiveness (F1) of 52.6%, a 21.4% relative improvement over the strongest of five external baselines. Code is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Evolving Agent Teams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36848)**  `arXiv:2610.36848`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shiyi Cao, Ziming Mao, Dacheng Li, Joseph E. Gonzalez, Ion Stoica_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The rapid progress of software engineering agents drives interest in applying them to open-ended problems such as GPU kernel optimization. However, we find that existing coding agents converge to local optima on real-world, expert-written kernels (e.g., FlashInfer). We introduce Evolving Agent Teams, a multi-agent framework that escapes such local optima. It features (1) a central supervisor that diagnoses each local optimum and restructures a team of agents; and (2) a persistent file system that comprises a learning document, an evolution history, and per-team artifacts, grounding restructuring with accumulated experience. Under a matched token budget, Evolving Agent Teams consistently outperforms strong coding-agent baselines. With an extended token budget, Evolving Agent Teams generates a CUDA kernel that reaches 1.2x mean speedup over the production FlashInfer MLA paged decode kernel from scratch and wins on 36 of 47 production workloads from FlashInfer-Bench (0.86-0.99x on the remaining 11). It also demonstrates superior performance on tasks such as KDA prefill on AMD MI350X (3.01x over vLLM) and NVIDIA B200 (3.44x over FlashKDA), MoE expert-offload serving on Apple silicon (30.3 tokens/s decode for Qwen3.8-Flash-Next in MLX 4-bit on a 48 GB M4 Pro), and RoboDojo robot manipulation (62.1 on held-out seeds vs. 55.3 for the best leaderboard entry). Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[An Incremental Knowledge-Graph Learner Digital Twin for Evidence-Grounded Early Warning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36820)**  `arXiv:2610.36820`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ngoc Luyen Le, Marie-Hélène Abel, Bertrand Laforge_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Early-warning models in learning analytics can identify learners at risk of failure or withdrawal, but the resulting alerts are often only weakly connected to inspectable learning evidence. A risk score may indicate that intervention is needed without showing which traces, assessment events, or temporal patterns justify the alert. This paper introduces an incremental Learner Digital Twin for evidence-grounded early warning, implemented as a lightweight knowledge graph. The twin is updated at temporal checkpoints by replaying learning traces and assessment records, and it serves both as a feature-extraction substrate and as an explanation-grounding substrate. Predictive features are derived through graph-mediated queries, while features, attributions, alerts, evidence items, and raw records are represented as queryable graph entities. The framework is instantiated and evaluated on a public learning-analytics benchmark under a temporal protocol. The results show that graph-mediated feature extraction preserves the predictive behavior of equivalent raw-table features across model families and checkpoints. Generated alerts are materialized as evidence chains linking risk decisions to feature attributions, evidence items, and raw platform records. The study shows that early-warning prediction can be routed through a semantic and provenance-aware representation while preserving predictive performance and adding traceability, verifiability, and evidence-grounded explanation.
  </details>

- **[Beyond the Evidence Coherence Trap: Agent-Driven Evidence Selection for Temporally Disordered Fake News Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36764)**  `arXiv:2610.36764`  `cs.AI`  
  _Chi Wang, Min Gao, Yifei Zhang, Bincheng Gu, Zongwei Wang, Wentao Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fake news undermines public trust and the integrity of online information ecosystems. Temporally disordered fake news is especially elusive because it rearranges factual events into a false chronology. Individual events may remain factually valid even when their temporal relations are misrepresented. When similar events involving the same entity recur, retrieval may return semantically plausible evidence from the wrong period. Consequently, independent event verification may rely on locally coherent but temporally mismatched evidence and incorrectly validate the article. We call this the \textit{evidence coherence trap}. Because existing benchmarks rarely isolate this setting, we construct ChronoFake, a source-disjoint benchmark of 30,706 articles derived from temporally anchored real events. It distinguishes temporal manipulation from valid narration, including flashbacks. To address this, we propose DelSA, a \underline{D}ocument S\underline{el}ection \underline{S}elf-Updating \underline{A}gent that iteratively refines evidence selection during training and applies a frozen rulebook for one-pass evidence selection at inference. DelSA uses an LLM to characterize candidate evidence, while a verifier-trained reranker balances local relevance against timeline consistency. Finally, the classifier compares the article-implied chronology extracted by the LLM with the evidence-derived chronology to reveal global temporal conflicts. DelSA outperforms existing methods on ChronoFake, while remaining competitive on PolitiFact and GossipCop.
  </details>

- **[PsyJev: Psychological Analysis via System-1 Judgment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36689)**  `arXiv:2610.36689`  `cs.AI`  
  _Fangyuan Liu, Sirui Zhao, Xiaohao Wang, Guo Cheng, Peng Zhang, Enhong Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-disclosures on the Web support diverse psychological analyses, from recognizing expressed experiences to identifying symptom-relevant information. We study a shared, question-conditioned decision interface for this heterogeneous problem in content analysis. We organize PsyJudgeBench, comprising 13 annotated datasets and 82 questions across six psychological areas, while preserving source-specific criteria and masks for unobserved labels. We introduce PsyJev, a System-1 model that directly produces distributions over answers specified in each request. Joint supervised training establishes the shared judgment function, followed by Judgment-Anchored RLCD (JA-RLCD), which adapts logit-space reward optimization to heterogeneous psychological judgments through balanced supervision, selective retention of correct SFT predictions, and question-group reward optimization. The Qwen3.5-based 2B, 4B, and 9B models achieve 64.33, 66.21, and 67.56 average test F1, respectively. The 9B model exceeds the strongest external comparison by 2.54 points. Controlled 2B comparisons examine the training stages and anchored refinement. Further analyses examine question-conditioned judgment, inference cost, and probability quality, complemented by qualitative comparisons across models.
  </details>

- **[The View from Nowhere: Population-Centered Agent Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36660)**  `arXiv:2610.36660`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shuo Yan, Ningning Hou, Haoyu He, Haochen Pei, Shang Gao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-improving AI systems can revise the designs that govern their own behavior. Yet existing approaches center improvement on selected parent agents and their evolutionary experience, leaving the broader population largely underused. We identify a substantial gap between population coverage (i.e., the tasks solved by at least one evolved variant) and the performance of the best individual agent. This gap shows that useful capabilities and diagnostic evidence emerge across the population without being consolidated into its leading agents.These observations motivate the view from nowhere, a principle for agent evolution: an agent should be improved by interpreting its behavior through population-wide patterns of success, failure, and prior self-modification. We instantiate this principle as Population-Centered Evolution (PCE). PCE distils task records and modification histories from the population into a population block, which captures recurring failures, complementary successes, and the effects of previously attempted modifications to guide improvements to a selected parent's harness.Under matched models, initial harnesses, and evolution schedules, PCE achieves 72.1% success on SWE-bench Verified and 96.7% on Polyglot, improving over a strong group-evolution baseline by 3.2 and 5.9 percentage points. In our evaluation, PCE reduces the gap between population coverage and best-agent performance by one-third on SWE-bench and closes it on Polyglot. These results suggest that treating the population as evidence--not merely as candidates for selection--provides a complementary principle for agent evolution.
  </details>

- **[ADTGuard: Adaptive Depth-Time Safeguard for Streaming Inference in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36574)**  `arXiv:2610.36574`  `cs.AI`  
  _Chuming Wei, Churui Yang, Zihan Zhang, Ruien Li, Wusheng Zhang, Yan Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) can generate fluent responses to diverse user requests, yet streaming may expose users to harmful content before generation is complete. Existing guardrails face two limitations in this setting. First, post-hoc moderation at response or chunk boundaries delays intervention, risking harmful exposure when content is released before verification. Second, even with token-level detection, coarse response-level supervision does not specify when harmful content begins, while fixed-layer readouts may miss relevant safety signals at other depths. To address these limitations, we introduce ADTGuard, which checks each token before release, progressively refines token-level training targets, and adapts layer selection to the current prefix. ADTGuard models risk across depth and time through Depth-Time Safety Dynamics (DTSD), which integrates safety evidence across layers at each token and uses it to guide risk accumulation over successive tokens. To adapt the choice of observation layers to the current prefix, we propose the Causal Depth Router (CDR). With only response-level labels available, we further develop Risk-Onset Target Refinement (RTR) to progressively construct token-level training targets for both components. Through extensive experiments across multiple safety benchmarks, we demonstrate that ADTGuard consistently achieves strong streaming detection performance, matching or surpassing state-of-the-art safeguards while requiring only 16M trainable parameters and maintaining competitive per-token latency.
  </details>

- **[When to Intervene? An Empirical Study of Errors and Self-Recovery in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36471)**  `arXiv:2610.36471`  `cs.AI`  
  _Chiming Duan, Tong Jia, Yingxi Chen, Minghua He, Lingzhe Zhang, Ying Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents interact with Web services to perform tasks on users' behalf. Errors during these interactions can disrupt execution and prevent task completion, motivating external intervention. However, agents may self-recover through continued execution, raising the question of when to intervene and when to wait for self-recovery. We present an empirical study of errors and self-recovery in LLM agents. We construct ReInBench, a dataset containing 1,259 agent traces and 1,403 error events across computer-use and Web tasks. Our study examines how often and how quickly agents self-recover, how self-recovery relates to subsequent execution and task completion, and whether self-recovery can be predicted when an error occurs. We find that 725 error events self-recover, often within a few steps, with self-recovery performance differing across Execution LLMs. Self-recovery can also help agents recover from subsequent errors of the same type and support overall task completion. Intervention should therefore prioritize errors unlikely to self-recover, while allowing agents to continue when self-recovery is likely. Building on these empirical findings, we propose ReCoF, a method that predicts whether an error will self-recover to inform whether additional intervention is needed. ReCoF achieves an average relative improvement of 9.02% in Macro-F1 over the strongest baseline on ReInBench. Our code and data can be found atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Correction or Manipulation? Measuring Epistemic Vigilance in LLM Agents Under User Pressure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36453)**  `arXiv:2610.36453`  `cs.AI`  
  _Nardine Basta, Venus Haghighi, Dali Kaafar, Amin Beheshti_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents that answer questions and act on behalf of users must decide when to yield to a user who disagrees. Disagreement may correct a genuine error or may be an attempt at manipulation. Prior work shows that models frequently abandon correct answers when challenged by users, yet it remains unclear whether they distinguish correction from manipulation or merely differ in their propensity to concede. We examine this question across seven LLMs from four vendors in two studies, one on the answers agents give and one on the actions they take as customer-support agents whose policy permits account changes only for verified users. Simulated users push toward correct and incorrect answers with graded pressure. Following research on epistemic vigilance, the pressure either appeals to the source, such as claimed authority, or offers content the agent cannot verify, namely a cited source. Drawing on signal detection theory, we measure on a set of benchmark questions how much more readily each agent concedes to correction than to manipulation, separately from its overall propensity to concede. Citations made five of seven models readier to concede and four worse at discriminating, while stronger wording added little. In the agentic study, five models never violated the verification policy, including models that readily abandoned correct answers, so concession on answers did not predict violations of policy. Ministral 3B violated the policy mainly under claimed authority, and Ministral 8B reported changes it had not made. An instruction to revise only on verifiable evidence chiefly shifted propensity to concede rather than improving discrimination. Concession rates alone are therefore misleading, and agents should be evaluated on both their discrimination and the actions they take.
  </details>

- **[RouteDRAG: Learning to Route and Control Agentic Execution for Multimodal Document RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36444)**  `arXiv:2610.36444`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zekai Chen, Hao Zhu, Yu Liu, Xunkai Li, Zejian Kang, Rui Qian, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Web-based applications increasingly rely on understanding long multimodal documents, where answering questions often requires combining information across pages, tables, and figures. Agentic retrieval-augmented generation addresses these tasks through iterative retrieval and reasoning. However, Agentic execution is not always necessary, introducing additional computation and potentially incorrect answers. Our empirical analysis reveals that Agentic execution provides query-dependent benefits, while additional operations may improve, preserve, or degrade answer accuracy. These findings motivate controlling both Agent invocation and subsequent execution. To address these challenges, we propose RouteDRAG, a learning-based framework comprising four components. Unified Document Index organizes multimodal content into a graph-backed index and derives query representations from initial retrieval. Adaptive Path Routing preserves exact answering and predicts the relative benefit of Agent over shorter execution paths. Dynamic Agent Execution maintains an evolving workspace and refines Planner proposals to control tool use and termination. Sequential Policy Learning first trains operation control from observed outcomes and then trains path routing using the resulting Agent's performance. Across two benchmarks, RouteDRAG achieves an average accuracy improvement of 3.47 percentage points over shorter-path-only execution while assigning only 79.3% of queries to Agent. Notably, it also surpasses uniform Agent execution on both benchmarks while requiring fewer answering calls. Our code is now available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[OA-RAG: Ontology-based Agentic RAG for Faithful LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36335)**  `arXiv:2610.36335`  `cs.AI`  
  _Hui Yang, Jiaoyan Chen, Yongsheng Gao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) perform well across many real-world tasks but remain prone to hallucination, limiting their reliability in domains such as biomedicine and healthcare, where answers must be grounded in established knowledge. Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) mitigates this problem by incorporating external information, but often overlooks logical relationships and relies heavily on the LLM's own reasoning, which can compromise answer faithfulness. We propose OA-RAG, an agent that integrates formal reasoning with LLM reasoning, drawing on both the logical structure and informal annotations of domain ontologies. For each answer, OA-RAG reports the ontology observations used as evidence and a model-assessed grounding level (entailed, supported, related, or unrelated) that characterises the strength of that evidence. Across benchmarks derived from three large-scale ontologies and six real-world medical question-answering tasks, OA-RAG achieves the best overall performance among the evaluated ontology-fine-tuned and popular RAG methods, with particularly strong results on reasoning-intensive tasks. The reported grounding levels also offer a useful indication of answer reliability, with stronger grounding generally associated with higher accuracy. Finally, our results demonstrate the value of ontologies as knowledge sources: OA-RAG is competitive with, and sometimes outperforms, domain-specific RAG systems that draw on sources such as textbooks.
  </details>

- **[When Retrieval Is Not Enough: Obsolete Facts in Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36308)**  `arXiv:2610.36308`  `cs.AI`  
  _Oleeviya Babu Poikarayil, Tony Basil Babu, Abdulrahman Nahhas, Het Darshan Mehta, Jawid Ahmad Baktash, Ernesto William De Luca, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Memory-agent benchmarks often collapse retrieval and answer generation into one score, making it hard to tell whether a failure comes from missing an update or from not using an update that was already retrieved. We examine this distinction on the single-hop FactConsolidation task of MemoryAgentBench. With Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct on 6K-history BM25 accuracy drops from 45% to 32% when the retrieved evidence budget grows from 64 to 512 tokens, even though a gold-assisted post-hoc audit finds the newest statement in every retrieved set. Most of the errors occur on questions that also contain an obsolete statement version. Under a frozen prompt and equal four-statement evidence size, replacing one unrelated statement with the obsolete competing statement reduces accuracy from 100% to 44.6% for Qwen2.5 and from 100% to 87.8% for Qwen3-14B, with all 50 harmful paired flips classified as stale. A 32K-history replication likewise favors BM25-64 over BM25-512 (69% versus 58%). These results suggest that retrieving the correct fact is not enough. An obsolete competing statement can be more harmful than an unrelated piece of evidence of the same size.
  </details>

- **[Spiking Neural Networks Are Harder to Steal: A Benchmark Study of Model Stealing Attacks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36298)**  `arXiv:2610.36298`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ayana Moshruba, Maryam Parsa_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Machine learning models deployed behind query interfaces expose more than their predictions, because an adversary can use those predictions to rebuild the model itself. Model stealing attacks (MSAs) exploit this exposure by training a surrogate model that replicates the decision behavior of a target through black-box queries alone, without access to its parameters or training data. These attacks are well characterized for conventional artificial neural networks (ANNs), yet spiking neural networks (SNNs) process information in a fundamentally different way. SNNs communicate through sparse, discrete spikes, and their outputs depend on membrane potentials that evolve over time rather than on a static mapping from input to output. This temporal mode of computation suggests that the decision behavior of an SNN may be harder to reconstruct from its responses. This work tests that possibility by benchmarking four representative MSAs, namely the Papernot, Tramèr, T-RND, and color perturbation attacks, against SNNs and their ANN counterparts on MNIST, Fashion-MNIST, and CIFAR-10. Across every attack and dataset, SNNs prove consistently more difficult to steal. Surrogates of SNNs achieve 10% to 20% lower agreement with the target model and 15% to 25% lower adversarial transferability than surrogates of ANNs. We attribute this resistance to the way SNNs compute, as their spike-based and time-dependent dynamics yield decision boundaries that ANN-based surrogates struggle to approximate, even under large query budgets. These findings extend the known resilience of SNNs beyond attacks on training data to the replication of model functionality, strengthening the case for neuromorphic architectures in security-sensitive applications.
  </details>

- **[AOS-Bench: Can LLM Agents Manage Dynamic Workloads?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33178)**  `arXiv:2610.33178`  `cs.AI`  
  _Erle Zhu, Genrui Zhang, Keyang Hu, Mingyu Su, Hongning Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Coding agents are moving from handling one request at a time to serving as persistent assistants to which users hand a stream of work: a build, a data-processing job and a bug fix may all be pending on the same machine at once. Such an agent must decide when to take on new tasks, how to delegate and overlap them, and how to stay within the machine's CPU and memory. Existing benchmarks cannot measure this, because they evaluate each task in isolation. We introduce AOS-Bench, which evaluates an agent system as a workload manager: verified tasks arrive over time and must be delivered under shared CPU and memory limits, and the same tasks run one at a time serve as a per-system reference.Across five model--harness pairs, isolated task success does not carry over evenly. On heavy workloads every system loses 13 to 23 points. On medium workloads, systems span 11 points in isolated success but 26 points in stream success, and two systems 0.6 points apart in isolation deliver 54\% and 68\% of the same tasks. Execution-policy interventions trace much of this gap to task intake: serial dispatch leaves 73\% of heavy-workload tasks unclaimed. Yet more elaborate management does not necessarily help. A fixed concurrency of two comes within four points of the best agent-managed policy on both medium and heavy workloads, and under every policy median CPU use stays below 30\% of the provisioned cores while tasks wait. These results identify workload management as a distinct capability for agents, and AOS-Bench as a way to measure it.
  </details>

- **[ARCANA: A Benchmark for Abstraction and Analogical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29271)**  `arXiv:2610.29271`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ruqi Chen, Zilei Wang, Wei Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Analogical reasoning is widely considered a fundamental component of human intelligence. Few benchmarks are designed to explicitly test far analogical reasoning - reasoning between domains that are superficially dissimilar yet share deep relational or structural similarities. In this work, we introduce an analogical reasoning benchmark called Abstraction and Reasoning Corpus of ANAlogy (ARCANA). The tasks of ARCANA require performing far analogical mappings from real-world objects or physical phenomena to abstract grid-based transformations, different from the well-known ARC-AGI benchmark which primarily emphasized innate human priors. We validate ARCANA through a human study, demonstrating that humans solve these tasks reliably. In contrast, evaluations across a range of large language models show a distinct performance gap compared to humans, revealing systematic failure modes that remain challenging for current models. To address this gap, we propose ConceptSIR, a reasoning framework based on concept slippage, introspection, and reflection. Experiments demonstrate that ConceptSIR enables standard LLMs to solve several challenging ARCANA tasks that were unsolved by direct prompting in our evaluation. Overall, our results suggest that far analogical reasoning remains an open problem for contemporary LLMs. All the materials are available atthis http URL. The complete codebase, including benchmark, and ConceptSIR, is publicly available atthis https URL. All ARCANA data and task instances can be accessed and explored atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Provenance-Aware Belief Updating in Human-AI Feedback Loops](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13008)**  `arXiv:2610.13008`  `cs.AI`  
  _Tianyi Huang, Eric Liu, Jay Chong, Emily Yin, Wanqi Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In repeated human-AI interaction, a single observation can be quoted, summarized, and returned to a model as multiple reports, making repetition look like independent corroboration. We study whether language models can use supplied report lineage to update according to evidence provenance rather than report count. We introduce Latent Quotient Evidence Training (LQET), a relational objective that combines sparse Bayesian supervision with constraints that encourage stable predictions when reports add no new evidence and consistent updates to the same new evidence across relay histories. On a controlled binary benchmark, LQET reduces new-evidence update RMSE by 41-44\% and log-odds drift after eight relay steps by 89-91\% relative to task-only training across Qwen3-4B and Phi-4-mini, with three paired training seeds per backbone. The gains extend to a closed-loop simulation in which model confidence controls how many relayed reports re-enter the model's context in later rounds. By grounding belief updates in supplied provenance, LQET offers a route toward dynamic human-AI systems in which influence follows new evidence, not the number of times the same evidence returns.
  </details>

- **[Diversity Combining for Multi-Path LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38829)**  `arXiv:2609.38829`  `cs.AI`  
  _Guangsheng Yu, Litianyi Zhang, Qin Wang, Xu Wang, Mingyuan Li, Shaoxiong Ji, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-path reasoning methods such as self-consistency (SC) sample $K$ reasoning paths and choose the most frequent answer. However, their gains quickly plateau as $K$ increases, and locating this saturation point currently requires fitting a scaling model to accuracies measured at several values of $K$ or stopping online per query. We formalize multi-path LLM reasoning as a diversity combining problem from wireless communications: each path is a noisy channel observation, and the pairwise correlation of path correctness caps the design-effect effective sample size of the vote at a finite ceiling. Generalized least squares (GLS) analysis shows that, for paths with equal gains, the optimal linear combiner of latent embeddings is uniform exactly when every row of the path covariance has the same sum, as under exchangeability, supporting majority vote as the natural default in standard SC while leaving room for weighting or pruning under heterogeneous prompt-template branches. Across 5 models and 12 benchmarks, prompt-template diversity reduces path correlation in $55$ of $60$ model-benchmark cells, with a mean relative reduction of $16\%$ and reductions in every task domain. We derive an Adaptive-K rule that uses a four-path pilot to select $K^*$, retaining $96$--$103\%$ of MV@$K{=}32$ accuracy across Math, QA, and NLU.
  </details>

- **[Learn from the Gap: Differential-Aware Advantage Pruning with Adaptive Rollout Sampling for GRPO](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36932)**  `arXiv:2609.36932`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jiahua Yang, Zhiwei Yang, Xianpeng Zhang, Dongyu Chen, Xing Chen, Tianhuang Su, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recently, Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) and its variants have been developed for policy optimization and demonstrated notable performance gains. However, these methods usually incur substantial computational overhead due to per-question multi-rollout sampling and repeated per-token probability evaluation across rollouts. Furthermore, low-information or highly homogeneous trajectories can degrade downstream learning signal efficiency, hindering model optimization and limiting final performance. To address these issues, we propose FastRL, a novel plug-and-play reinforcement learning framework that simultaneously improves training efficiency and the effectiveness of policy learning. Specifically, 1) We introduce an advantage-aware pruning strategy to selectively preserve high-advantage trajectories while maximizing inter-trajectory gradient diversity. 2) Then, we design an adaptive rollout sampling mechanism to dynamically adjust the sampling scale across different training stages based on historical pruning distributions, balancing exploration adequacy and computational efficiency. Experiments demonstrate that FastRL can be seamlessly integrated into GRPO, DAPO, and GSPO variants, achieving an average 2.07$\times$ training speedup on Geometry3K and GeoQA8K-R1V, along with an approximately 1.64\% improvement in average accuracy on visual reasoning benchmarks. Source codes will be available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Lightning Weave: Improving the Accuracy-Efficiency Frontier of Reasoning Models through Capability Composition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14708)**  `arXiv:2609.14708`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yecheng Wu, Song Han, Han Cai_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A core goal of efficient reasoning is to improve the accuracy-efficiency frontier. However, jointly improving reasoning accuracy and inference efficiency can be challenging, as the two objectives can favor different reasoning behaviors. Independently post-trained models already offer distinct strengths in accuracy and efficiency. We introduce Lightning Weave, a post-training framework that extracts and composes these independently learned capabilities in a single student through on-policy distillation. Each acquired capability is represented by the policy shift from the model before post-training to the resulting specialist. Lightning Weave combines aligned log-ratio shifts at shared student token states and uses Tilted-Target DOPD to convert the cached signals into a stable learning target. Each anchor pair scores the cached trajectories once, enabling subsequent student training without serving multiple live anchor models concurrently. Across diverse student models and benchmarks in mathematics and code, Lightning Weave substantially improves upon the base students and achieves a state-of-the-art accuracy-efficiency frontier. On Qwen3.5-4B, it raises HMMT 2025 accuracy from 59.2% to 64.0% with 10.7% fewer response tokens, and LiveCodeBench v5 accuracy from 41.7% to 54.2% with 9.6% fewer response tokens. Adjusting the relative strengths of the anchor signals yields a strong empirical accuracy-efficiency Pareto frontier. These results establish Lightning Weave as a new practical route to efficient reasoning through capability composition. Code is released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[When Rubrics Fail: Hallucinations Reveal Blind Spots in Medical AI Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12718)**  `arXiv:2609.12718`  `cs.AI`  
  _Griffin Farrow, Lily Sijia Li, Jack Johnson, Tingyan Wang, Philip Torr, William Bolton, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hallucinations can undermine clinician trust in LLMs, making it important that evaluation methods capture clinically relevant errors. Rubric-based evaluation has become the leading approach for assessing LLMs in medicine, but it is unclear whether rubric scores reflect such errors. We first study this in a controlled setting using MedHallu, finding that more specific rubrics better distinguish correct from hallucinated responses. To test this systematically, we develop a taxonomy of medical hallucination types and a clinician-validated error-injection pipeline that creates matched correct and error-injected responses. Across HealthBench, HealthBench Professional, and LiveMedBench, our clinically relevant hallucinations are missed by rubrics, often leaving scores unchanged. We find that rubrics are most effective when explicitly checking facts, and are less effective for additional or unexpected errors they do not anticipate. A preliminary retrieval-based factuality check recovers some of the rubric-blind errors, suggesting a complementary approach. These findings reveal systematic blind spots in current medical evaluation of LLMs and suggest that rubric scores alone are insufficient to establish clinical reliability, potentially undermining clinician trust and confidence in clinical deployment.
  </details>

- **[MemPrism: Task-Conditioned Relational Memory Views for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06745)**  `arXiv:2608.06745`  `cs.AI`  
  _Bingfan Zeng, Zhisheng Chen, Zhengwei Xie, Bangde Cao, Yuxuan Li, Jinhan Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon agents performing multi-step Web tasks must reuse earlier page observations and interactions to support subsequent actions. However, preserving relevant history does not ensure that it exposes the relations needed for the current decision. We call this gap representation mismatch. To this end, we propose MemPrism, a task-conditioned relational memory framework that separates persistent experience storage from decision-time working memory. MemPrism records interactions as the event stream and dynamically constructs relational views according to the current task context. A lightweight view policy selects the relation structure, evidence range, outcome condition, and granularity, while a deterministic composer and renderer transform historical facts into a temporary optical working-memory view for a frozen task policy. Experiments on long-horizon embodied and web-agent benchmarks show that MemPrism consistently improves the task performance, especially as trajectories become longer, while reducing memory token consumption. Furthermore, the learned view policy transfers across different VLMs without additional adaptation, demonstrating the effectiveness of task-conditioned relational views as a general memory interface for agents.
  </details>

- **[Tracing the Cascade: A Topology-Aware Evaluation Framework for Scientific Agent Hallucinations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00711)**  `arXiv:2608.00711`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xinshun Feng, Ziqi Miao, Lijun Li, Jing Shao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly deployed in scientific research, where reliability is critical and the underlying knowledge is densely interconnected. In such settings, hallucinations are particularly damaging: a single erroneous claim on a foundational concept can propagate through multi-step reasoning and corrupt entire trajectories. Existing hallucination benchmarks largely operate at the surface level, treating facts in isolation and relying on uniform accuracy metrics that ignore this topological structure. We address this gap with SCHEMA, the first evidence-grounded, topology-aware evaluation framework for hallucinations in scientific agents. SCHEMA automatically constructs scientific concept graphs from benchmark seeds and literature evidence, synthesizes graph-grounded tasks spanning claim verification, multi-hop reasoning, open-ended explanation, and experimental code generation, and evaluates agents with two complementary diagnostics. A trajectory hallucination pipeline audits intermediate reasoning at scale via a topology-weighted severity score, while a multi-agent counterfactual attribution module pinpoints the causal mechanism behind selected failures. SCHEMA reveals that hallucinations concentrate at a small set of highly connected knowledge hubs, and that final-answer accuracy decouples from trajectory honesty; models often reach correct conclusions through structurally flawed reasoning. These results indicate that for high-stakes scientific applications, terminal accuracy alone is an insufficient signal of agent reliability, motivating mechanism-level evaluation grounded in knowledge topology. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[LISA: Linear-Indexed Sparse Attention for Efficient Long-Context Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.19358)**  `arXiv:2607.19358`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yu Zhao, Zekun Zhang, Fan Jiang, Bo Zeng, Xintong Wang, Chonghan Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1 keep lengthening their inference chains under test-time scaling, yet standard self-attention incurs quadratic cost in context length, making long-CoT deployment costly in production. We therefore introduce LISA (Linear-Indexed Sparse Attention), a plug-and-play replacement for self-attention that requires no full pretraining. LISA combines a linear-attention branch for long-range memory with a Lightning Indexer that dynamically selects the most relevant tokens for a sparse self-attention branch. The two branches are fused through a learned gate, so the model retains broad context while focusing computation on informative tokens.We train LISA in two stages. In Stage 1, a linear-attention branch is integrated with sliding-window attention and distilled from a frozen teacher's full-attention distribution. In Stage 2, the Lightning Indexer replaces the static window and is trained with a per-head KL-divergence objective to mimic teacher attention patterns. The resulting model reduces eager-mode self-attention computation from quadratic in sequence length to linear in the number of generated tokens and the selected context budget.Experiments on DeepSeek-distilled Qwen models show that LISA cuts measured eager-mode mean sample latency by 23.9% at 16K context without degrading long-context reasoning quality. Across nine mathematical and code reasoning benchmarks, the gated LISA-1.5B model improves the equal-weight benchmark average by 4.2 percentage points relative to the full-attention baseline.
  </details>

- **[HOLogicBench: Evaluating Higher-Order Logical Reasoning in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23238)**  `arXiv:2606.23238`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yucheng Wu, Jundong Xu, Mingzhen Ju, Yue Yu, Chenpeng Wang, Haoxuan Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing LLM logical-reasoning benchmarks largely focus on object-level inference under fixed predicates and rules, leaving higher-order reasoning over rules and policies underexplored. We introduce HOLogicBench, a 1,379-instance benchmark that systematically evaluates five representative higher-order reasoning operations, with Isabelle/HOL formalizations and proof-verified answers. Beyond answer accuracy, HOLogicBench provides solver-verified reference reasoning paths for each instance, enabling fine-grained evaluation of model-generated reasoning against valid derivations. Across 16 model configurations, average accuracy is 53.86\%, with the best configuration reaching 65.99\%. Further analyses reveal that LLMs exhibit limited alignment with valid derivations, remain vulnerable to minimal counterfactual perturbations, and display distinct failure patterns across higher-order reasoning types.
  </details>

- **[Hedge-Bench: Benchmarking Agents on Hard, Realistic Tasks Pertaining to Financial Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03918)**  `arXiv:2606.03918`  `cs.AI`  
  _Mahesh Ramesh, Eric Cho, Alice Lu, Shawn Huang, Baiqing Lyu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern AI agents are increasingly capable of routine financial-analysis tasks such as retrieving documents and updating spreadsheets. A harder challenge is forecasting an unknown future from incomplete information. Public markets provide a natural testbed: analysts routinely predict discrete events with information from that point in time. We present Hedge-Bench, a benchmark of 92 real-world environments derived from hedge fund analysts' reasoning traces paired with subsequently resolved prediction questions. Agents reason from frozen information snapshots, and their forecasts are evaluated against real-world outcomes. Across 11 frontier models and three trials per environment, joint success rates, which require both a correct forecast and over 60% research coverage, range from 2.54 to 31.52%. Using GRPO with turn-level credit assignment, we train Muse-Glimmer on 165 environments, increasing coverage on 22 held-out environments from 17.17% to 33.36% at low reasoning effort and from 30.19% to 47.02% at extra-high effort. The trained model at low effort also exceeds the base model's extra-high-effort coverage while using 47.9% fewer tokens. We also observe evidence of generalization: held-out forecasting accuracy at high effort improves by 10.06 percentage points, while out-of-distribution evaluation scores improve by 3.94 points on GDPval, 2.29 onthis http URL, 7.07 on Harvey LAB, and 4.96 on Frontier Finance under their respective metrics. Code:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Difficulty-Estimated Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.06375)**  `arXiv:2602.06375`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yu Zhao, Fan Jiang, Tianle Liu, Bo Zeng, Xintong Wang, Jiaxuan Teng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advancements in Large Reasoning Models (LRMs), exemplified by DeepSeek-R1, have underscored the potential of scaling inference-time compute through Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). However, GRPO frequently suffers from gradient signal attenuation when encountering problems that are either too trivial or overly complex. In these scenarios, the disappearance of inter-group advantages makes the gradient signal susceptible to noise, thereby jeopardizing convergence stability. While variants like DAPO attempt to rectify gradient vanishing, they do not alleviate the substantial computational overhead incurred by exhaustive rollouts on low-utility samples. In this paper, we propose Difficulty-Estimated Policy Optimization (DEPO), a novel framework designed to optimize the efficiency and robustness of reasoning alignment. DEPO integrates an online Difficulty Estimator that dynamically assesses and filters training data before the rollout phase. This mechanism ensures that computational resources are prioritized for samples with high learning potential. Empirical results show that DEPO achieves approximately 1.86x faster rollouts than DAPO in the profiled configuration, while improving benchmark performance over GRPO at comparable training cost. Our approach significantly lowers the computational barrier for training high-performance reasoning models, offering a more sustainable path for reasoning scaling.
  </details>

- **[HiMA-Ecom: A Long-Horizon Memory Benchmark for Hierarchical Multi-Agent E-commerce Assistants](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.19846)**  `arXiv:2506.19846`  `cs.AI`  
  _Junxing Hu, Ai Han, Haolan Zhan, Pu Wei, Zhiqian Zhang, Yuhang Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon agentic systems become long-context systems by accumulation rather than through a single long document, since every step reassembles a prompt from role instructions, tool and sub-agent descriptions, recalled memories, dialogue history, and previous tool outputs, separately for every agent. This paper studies how such accumulated context can be trained, evaluated, and managed in hierarchical multi-agent assistants. We present HiMA-Ecom, a hierarchical multi-agent benchmark for e-commerce assistants with 22.8K instances, of which 17.7K carry memory entries in context, pairing agent-specific supervised fine-tuning data with system-level supervision for end-to-end multi-agent reinforcement learning. Building upon it, HiMA-R1 keeps the context budget of a trajectory under control through Variance-Reduction Group Relative Policy Optimization (VR-GRPO), which samples along an initial trajectory so that sampling cost grows additively with trajectory length and updates the agents with the largest reward variance, together with an efficiency reward on trajectory length and a memory evolution mechanism that repurposes GRPO rewards as cost-free signals for what stays in context. Agents built upon 3B and 7B open-source models match DeepSeek-R1 and surpass DeepSeek-V3 by 6.0 points on average, while reward-driven memory reduces the update steps needed for convergence by roughly 55%.
  </details>
